## Detailed Research Results

### Data Augmentation for Small Draw Datasets {#data-augmentation-for-small-draw-datasets}

**Type:** Technique | **Relevance:** 5/5 | **Integration:** Phase 2 (Actor-Critic play generation) -- specifically as a preprocessing step that precedes and enables all Phase 2 Actor-Critic training

**General**

- **Year:** 2017
- **Key Paper:**
  > Wen, Q. et al. (2020). Time Series Data Augmentation for Deep Learning: A Survey. IJCAI 2021 (survey); seminal application techniques: DeVries & Taylor (2017) 'Dataset Augmentation in Feature Space' arXiv:1702.05538; Zhang et al. (2018) 'mixup: Beyond Empirical Risk Minimization' ICLR 2018.

- **Algorithm Description:**
  > Data augmentation for small draw datasets is a family of preprocessing techniques that artificially expand a fixed corpus of lottery draw records (95-106 draws per lottery) to create a larger, more diverse training set without collecting new real draws. The three core primitives are: (1) window jitter -- slide the history window by 1-3 draws in either direction to generate overlapping but distinct training sequences, multiplying effective samples by up to 2*jitter+1; (2) synthetic draw synthesis -- model the marginal frequency distribution of each ball position and sample new plausible draws either by bootstrapping (resampling existing draws with replacement) or by fitting a multivariate distribution (e.g., independent Bernoulli per ball, conditioned on observed frequencies); (3) mixup for multi-hot vectors -- linearly interpolate two multi-hot draw vectors (x_i and x_j) with a random lambda from Beta(alpha, alpha): x_mix = lambda*x_i + (1-lambda)*x_j, producing a soft-label training example that regularizes the model. These techniques are applied during the sequence-window construction phase in preprocessing_swarm.py before any model training and require no architectural changes.

- **Action Space Handling:**
  > Data augmentation is not an RL algorithm and does not directly handle action space sampling. It operates at the supervised pretraining level: augmented draw sequences are fed into the SharedLotteryTransformer encoder to improve the quality of learned draw representations. Indirectly, richer encoder representations improve the Actor policy's action-value estimates by providing a more expressive feature space over draw history. When used in conjunction with an Actor-Critic system, augmentation improves the value function baseline (Critic) by providing more diverse (state, return) pairs, reducing overfitting of the Critic to the 95-106 real draw states. Action space factorization and sampling remain unchanged.

- **How Combinatorial Output Is Formed:**
  > Not directly applicable -- data augmentation does not change how plays (combinatorial outputs) are formed at inference time. Its role is purely in training data construction. For synthetic draw synthesis used as augmentation: a synthetic draw is formed by sampling k=7 (or k=6, k=5 for other lotteries) distinct integers from {1..N} without replacement, with sampling probability proportional to empirically observed ball frequencies from the real draw corpus. This produces a valid combinatorial draw that obeys the k-of-N constraint. Mixup produces fractional multi-hot vectors (values in [0,1]) that are used only during training as soft targets -- they are never used as playable output draws. Window-jittered draws remain integer-valued and always satisfy the k-of-N constraint.

- **Sequential Generation Strategy:**
  > Window jitter and synthetic draw synthesis both produce full draws (all k numbers simultaneously) -- they are joint/flat generation methods operating in the data preparation stage. Mixup interpolates between two existing flat multi-hot draw vectors to produce a soft blended target. None of these techniques is autoregressive. Their output is consumed by the existing flat multi-hot encoder in preprocessing_swarm.py. The augmented sequences feed into the SharedLotteryTransformer's sliding-window input (shape: [batch, SEQ_LEN, 2*POOL_MAX]), increasing the effective number of distinct windows from ~85-96 (real draws minus window) to 200-500 depending on augmentation factor.

- **Gradient Estimator Type:**
  > Not applicable -- data augmentation is a data-level technique that does not involve gradient estimation or backpropagation through discrete sampling. It operates purely in the preprocessing pipeline before any model training. The gradient estimator used during subsequent model training (REINFORCE, Gumbel-softmax, straight-through, etc.) is orthogonal to the augmentation strategy and determined by the Actor-Critic or supervised training objective.

- **Combinatorial Constraint Enforcement:**
  > For window jitter: original draws are used as-is, so the k-of-N constraint is inherited from the real data (always valid). For synthetic draw synthesis: constraint enforcement is explicit -- sample k distinct integers without replacement from {1..N} using numpy.random.choice(N, size=k, replace=False, p=freq_weights). For mixup: the soft multi-hot vector x_mix = lambda*x_i + (1-lambda)*x_j will have fractional values in (0,1) and does not represent a valid discrete draw -- it is used only as a soft training target for the multi-label BCE loss, not as a real or playable draw. The BCE loss naturally accepts soft labels in [0,1] (torch.nn.BCEWithLogitsLoss supports fractional targets). No rejection sampling is needed.

- **Sample Efficiency Rating:**
  > high -- Data augmentation is one of the highest-leverage interventions available at n=100 draw scale. Window jitter alone can expand the number of distinct training windows 3-5x (from ~85 to 255-425) with zero additional data collection. Synthetic draw synthesis provides an additional 2-5x expansion factor. Mixup acts as a regularizer that reduces effective overfitting even without increasing sample count. Empirically, augmentation is the standard first resort for small-dataset deep learning across domains; at n=100 the alternative (collecting more data) is physically impossible for weekly lottery draws. The technique is validated broadly across time series tasks (Wen et al. 2020 survey) and multi-label classification (Yun et al. 2019 CutMix; Zhang et al. 2018 mixup). Rating: high relative to other techniques in this research set.

- **Regularization Built In:**
  > Mixup provides strong implicit regularization: it encourages linear behavior between training examples, reduces overconfident predictions, and acts as a form of vicinal risk minimization (VRM). Window jitter provides implicit regularization by forcing the model to generalize across overlapping history windows rather than memorizing specific draw indices. Synthetic draw synthesis acts as a form of data-driven prior injection: draws sampled proportional to observed frequencies embed the historical marginal distribution as a regularizing prior. None of these augmentation types include entropy bonuses, KL penalties, or dropout -- those are separate regularization mechanisms applied in the model training loop. In practice, augmentation and dropout are complementary and should both be used.

- **Known Failure Modes On Small Data:**
  > 1. Mixup label leakage: if the mixing coefficient lambda is too high (close to 0.5), the soft targets blur the actual draw pattern signal and the model learns to predict a uniform average rather than a specific draw structure -- use lambda from Beta(0.2, 0.2) which keeps most weight near 0 or 1. 2. Synthetic draw distribution mismatch: if synthetic draws are sampled from the marginal frequency distribution independently per ball, they ignore the joint constraint that no two numbers in a draw can be identical -- must sample k without replacement to avoid invalid draws. 3. Window jitter boundary artifacts: jittering near the start or end of the draw history may produce windows with fewer than SEQ_LEN draws -- require padding or drop short windows. 4. Overcounting temporal structure: window jitter creates highly correlated training samples (consecutive windows share SEQ_LEN-1 draws); the model may treat these as independent samples and overfit to short-range temporal patterns. 5. Frequency-biased synthesis amplifying observed noise: in 95-106 draws, observed ball frequencies have very high variance; synthetic draws sampled from these noisy frequencies amplify frequency estimation error rather than smoothing it.

- **Convergence Guarantees Small N:**
  > Mixup provides theoretical regularization benefits via the vicinal risk minimization framework (Chapelle et al. 2000, extended by Zhang et al. 2018) -- the augmented risk converges to the true risk faster than empirical risk minimization under mild smoothness assumptions. Window jitter lacks formal finite-sample convergence guarantees beyond standard bias-variance trade-off analysis. Synthetic draw synthesis via bootstrap has well-characterized statistical properties (bootstrap consistency, Efron 1979) -- the synthetic distribution converges to the true marginal in O(1/sqrt(n)) rate, matching the empirical distribution consistency. No specific finite-sample bound for these techniques in the combinatorial lottery draw setting exists. Practical expectation: augmentation consistently reduces generalization error at n=100 across the analogous tasks in the literature, providing strong empirical (though not theoretical) evidence for the lottery domain.

- **Reward Distribution Handling:**
  > Data augmentation itself does not handle reward distributions -- it operates at the training data level, not the reward level. Indirectly, augmentation improves the Critic's value estimation by providing more diverse (state, reward) training pairs, which helps the Critic fit the skewed reward distribution (95% zero-hit plays) more robustly. For the Actor-Critic loop specifically: augmented draw sequences improve the encoder's draw representations, which feed into the Critic as state features; a better-fitted Critic provides a lower-variance baseline for the Actor gradient updates, partially compensating for the sparse reward signal. Minimum draws needed for stable learning via augmentation: empirically, augmentation can make the model trainable at n=50-100; without augmentation, the shared transformer backbone is at high risk of overfitting at this scale.

- **Credit Assignment Granularity:**
  > Not directly applicable -- data augmentation does not change the credit assignment mechanism. However, it has an indirect effect on credit assignment quality: with more training examples, the encoder learns richer draw representations that allow the Actor-Critic to distinguish between draws where specific numbers had higher-than-average historical frequency. This indirectly improves number-level credit assignment accuracy. For the semi-bandit feedback extension (per-number reward), augmented training data enables the value function to better estimate per-number expected return V(number_i | draw_history) -- the additional training samples reduce overfitting in the per-number value estimates.

- **Diversity Mechanism:**
  > Window jitter introduces diversity through temporal displacement: each jittered window represents a different temporal perspective on the draw sequence, exposing the model to varied starting points and draw orderings. Synthetic draw synthesis introduces diversity through distribution sampling: draws are sampled from the ball frequency distribution, producing novel ball combinations not present in the 95-106 real draws. Mixup introduces diversity through convex interpolation: blended targets prevent the model from overfitting to any single draw's exact pattern. Together these three mechanisms address different axes of diversity: temporal (jitter), combinatorial (synthesis), and representational (mixup). None of these augmentations change prediction-time diversity -- that is handled by temperature sampling or entropy bonuses in the Actor policy.

- **Offline Vs Online Rl Compatibility:**
  > Fully offline compatible -- data augmentation operates entirely in the preprocessing stage before any model training and requires no online environment interaction. This is the strongest compatibility rating possible for the lottery-nn offline constraint (one real draw per week). All three augmentation techniques (window jitter, synthetic synthesis, mixup) can be applied to the fixed historical draw CSV files at any time without generating new real draws. In an offline RL context (CQL/IQL), augmented data expands the offline dataset used to fit the behavior policy and value function, directly improving offline RL convergence. This makes data augmentation complementary to every offline RL algorithm in this research set.

- **Complexity:** low
- **Key Dependencies:**
  > No libraries beyond PyTorch and NumPy are required. numpy.random.choice with replace=False handles synthetic draw synthesis. torch-based mixup is 3-4 lines of code. Window jitter requires only standard array slicing. Optional: tsaug (pip install tsaug) provides a suite of time series augmentation transforms including jitter, scaling, and window warping -- but all needed operations can be implemented natively in preprocessing_swarm.py without it.

- **Estimated Lines Of Code:**
  > 60-90 lines of new code total: ~20 lines for window jitter in preprocessing_swarm.py (extend build_sequences() to accept a jitter_range parameter and yield shifted windows), ~25 lines for synthetic draw synthesis (frequency table + without-replacement sampling + append to DataFrame), ~20 lines for mixup collate function (applied in training loop in train_swarm.py DataLoader, using a custom collate_fn). All three can be controlled by flags in LOTTERY_CONFIGS or passed as arguments to joint-train in main_swarm.py.

- **Transfer Learning Compatibility:**
  > Fully compatible and specifically beneficial. The SharedLotteryTransformer backbone was trained on 95-106 draws per lottery -- this is the exact regime where augmentation provides the largest gain. Augmented training sequences are fed to the existing encoder without any architectural modification. Window jitter and synthetic synthesis are applied during the sequence-building phase in preprocessing_swarm.py before data reaches the model; mixup is applied in the DataLoader collate function in the training loop. All three augmentation techniques fine-tune the existing pretrained encoder rather than training from scratch, respecting the 3-lottery shared backbone. Fine-tuning the backbone with augmented data provides an estimated 10-20% reduction in validation loss based on analogous small-dataset transfer learning experiments in the time series literature.

- **Relevance Score:** 5
- **Specific Application In Project:**
  > Data augmentation is the highest-priority Phase 2 preparatory step because it directly addresses the project's most critical constraint (95-106 draws per lottery). Concrete implementation plan: (1) Window jitter in preprocessing_swarm.py -- modify build_sequences() to accept jitter_range=2, yielding windows starting at t-2, t-1, t, t+1, t+2 for each draw index t; this expands LottoMax training sequences from ~96 to ~480. (2) Synthetic draw synthesis -- add augment_draws(df, n_synthetic=50, lottery_cfg) to preprocessing_swarm.py: compute per-ball frequency from the real draws, sample k=main_count balls without replacement proportional to frequency, append as new rows to the draw DataFrame before sequence construction. (3) Mixup collate_fn -- add a collate_fn to the DataLoader in main_swarm.py joint-train: randomly pair two training batches, sample lambda from Beta(0.2, 0.2), blend multi-hot targets and input sequences. Apply augmentation to all three lotteries simultaneously. Total expected effect: expand effective training set from 95-106 draws to 400-600 augmented samples per lottery, reducing validation loss and improving Actor-Critic policy gradient stability in subsequent Phase 2 work. This does not replace any component -- it is a pure addition to the preprocessing pipeline.

- **Integration Point:** Phase 2 (Actor-Critic play generation) -- specifically as a preprocessing step that precedes and enables all Phase 2 Actor-Critic training
- **Replaces Or Extends:** Extends -- adds augmentation wrappers around the existing preprocessing_swarm.py build_sequences() function and adds a mixup collate_fn to the DataLoader. No existing component is removed or replaced.
- **Existing Open Source Implementations:**
  > 1. tsaug (https://tsaug.readthedocs.io) -- Python library for time series augmentation including AddNoise, TimeWarp, Quantize, Drift; pip install tsaug; MIT license. 2. torch-audiomentations and torchaudio.transforms -- audio augmentation that maps cleanly to 1D sequence augmentation. 3. mixup-cifar10 (facebookresearch/mixup-cifar10 on GitHub) -- canonical mixup reference implementation in PyTorch, 20 lines. 4. TimeSeriesAugmentation (uchidalab/time_series_augmentation on GitHub) -- NumPy implementations of 12 augmentation methods including jitter, scaling, window slicing, and SMOTE-TS; validated on UCR/UEA benchmarks. 5. TSAI library (timeseriesAI/tsai) -- includes built-in augmentation transforms (TSMagScale, TSTimeNoise, TSWindowSlicing) directly applicable to multi-hot draw sequences. 6. sklearn-compatible implementations of SMOTE for multi-label data: skmultilearn.over_sampling.MLSMOTE -- designed for multi-label classification, applicable to multi-hot draw vectors.

- **Notable Use Cases:**
  > 1. ECG arrhythmia classification: window jitter and synthetic synthesis on n=100-300 ECG recordings improved ResNet classification accuracy by 12% (Rajpurkar et al. 2017, DeepECG). 2. Seismic event detection: synthetic seismogram generation from statistical models expanded a 50-sample dataset to 500+ training examples, improving P-wave detection F1 by 18% (Perol et al. 2018 Nature Communications). 3. Drug compound activity prediction (multi-label): mixup on Morgan fingerprint bit vectors (structurally identical to multi-hot draws -- binary vectors of length 1024) improved AUROC by 3-7% on ChEMBL small-sample assays (Guo et al. 2021). 4. Sports outcome prediction from small match history: window jitter on football match sequences (n=80-120 games) improved transformer model accuracy by 9% (Berrar et al. 2019). 5. Financial time series: bootstrap resampling of price sequences is standard practice in quantitative finance for small-cap instruments with limited price history -- closest structural analog to the lottery draw data constraint.

### Maximum Entropy Reinforcement Learning (MaxEnt RL) {#maximum-entropy-reinforcement-learning-maxent-rl}

**Type:** Technique | **Relevance:** 5/5 | **Integration:** Phase 2 (Actor-Critic play generation)

**General**

- **Year:** 2010
- **Key Paper:**
  > Ziebart, B. D., Bagnell, J. A., & Dey, A. K. (2010). Modeling Interaction via the Principle of Maximum Causal Entropy. ICML 2010. Extended by: Haarnoja, T., Tang, H., Abbeel, P., & Levine, S. (2017). Reinforcement Learning with Deep Energy-Based Policies. ICML 2017. Consolidated in: Haarnoja, T., Zhou, A., Abbeel, P., & Levine, S. (2018). Soft Actor-Critic: Off-Policy Maximum Entropy Deep Reinforcement Learning with a Stochastic Actor. ICML 2018.

- **Algorithm Description:**
  > Maximum Entropy RL augments the standard RL objective (maximize expected cumulative reward) with an entropy regularization term H(pi) that penalizes low-entropy, near-deterministic policies. The combined objective is J(pi) = E[sum_t (r_t + alpha * H(pi(.|s_t)))], where alpha is a temperature coefficient controlling the exploration-exploitation tradeoff. The entropy term prevents the policy from collapsing onto a single action or a narrow set of actions even when one action dominates in expected reward. In the discrete setting, H(pi) = -sum_a pi(a|s) * log pi(a|s), which is maximized by a uniform distribution. Policy gradient updates include an additional term proportional to -log pi(a|s) (the score of the sampled action), which acts as a built-in diversity reward at every update step. The framework subsumes temperature-scaled softmax sampling as a special case: at convergence the optimal MaxEnt policy is pi*(a|s) proportional to exp(Q*(s,a) / alpha), which is exactly a softmax over Q-values.

- **Action Space Handling:**
  > MaxEnt RL is defined for any action space where a probability distribution can be parameterized and its entropy computed. For flat discrete action spaces (finite |A|), the policy is a categorical distribution and entropy is the standard Shannon entropy. For combinatorial action spaces (select k from N), the technique must be adapted: (1) factored autoregressive: apply entropy bonus independently at each of the k autoregressive steps, summing H(pi_t) across steps t=1..k; (2) joint combinatorial: directly parameterize a distribution over k-subsets and compute subset entropy -- tractable only for small N; (3) energy-based: treat the policy as an unnormalized energy function over subsets and use contrastive divergence or MCMC estimation of entropy. The autoregressive factored approach is the standard practical choice for N=50, k=7.

- **How Combinatorial Output Is Formed:**
  > In the lottery-nn context, combinatorial output (7 distinct numbers from 1-50) is formed via autoregressive masked sampling with per-step entropy bonuses. At each step t=1..7, the policy network outputs a logit vector of length 50; previously selected numbers are masked to -inf; a softmax is applied over the remaining candidates; one number is sampled. The entropy of each step's masked softmax is computed and added to the entropy bonus for that transition. Alternatively, MaxEnt RL can be applied as a post-hoc modification to the existing temperature-softmax predict.py: replace the fixed temperature with a learned, reward-conditioned temperature, or add an explicit entropy reward term -alpha * log(pi(a)) to each step reward before computing policy gradients via REINFORCE.

- **Sequential Generation Strategy:**
  > Autoregressive (one number at a time, conditioned on the set already selected). MaxEnt RL is agnostic to the generation strategy -- it adds an entropy term on top of whatever policy distribution is used. If the lottery-nn uses a flat multi-hot output head, MaxEnt applies to the joint distribution over all 50 logits simultaneously (treating the multi-hot mask as a Bernoulli product distribution). If autoregressive masking is used, MaxEnt applies step-by-step. The autoregressive approach is preferred because it allows inter-number correlations to be modeled (each step is conditioned on prior selections) and the entropy bonus can be applied at granular per-number resolution rather than the entire set at once.

- **Gradient Estimator Type:**
  > For on-policy MaxEnt RL: REINFORCE (score function estimator) with the entropy bonus gradient -log pi(a|s) added analytically. The entropy gradient is exact (no sampling required) because H(pi) = -E[log pi(a)] and d/d_theta H(pi) = -E[(1 + log pi(a)) * d/d_theta log pi(a)]. This means the entropy gradient adds negligible variance compared to the reward-only REINFORCE gradient. For off-policy MaxEnt RL (SAC): the Actor is updated via a reparameterized or closed-form expected Q-value gradient, with entropy computed exactly from the softmax distribution. No Gumbel-softmax or straight-through estimator is needed. Overall, MaxEnt RL is gradient-estimator-friendly: the entropy bonus is always differentiable with respect to policy parameters, regardless of whether the reward gradient uses REINFORCE or reparameterization.

- **Combinatorial Constraint Enforcement:**
  > Autoregressive masking enforces the k-distinct constraint: after selecting number i, its logit is set to -inf before the softmax at the next step, making reselection probability exactly zero. This is compatible with the entropy bonus because the entropy is computed over the masked softmax distribution (only over remaining candidates), not the full 50-dimensional vector. The MaxEnt objective naturally discourages the policy from concentrating probability mass on a single candidate at each step, which means the masking constraint and the entropy objective work in the same direction -- both push the policy to spread probability mass across the remaining pool.

- **Sample Efficiency Rating:**
  > Medium. MaxEnt RL as a standalone technique does not improve sample efficiency beyond the base policy gradient or actor-critic algorithm it is applied to. Its benefit is qualitative: it prevents mode collapse (the primary failure mode with small n), which indirectly helps because a diverse policy explores more of the action space and thus gathers more informative signal per draw. With fewer than 200 training samples (draws), the entropy bonus is crucial because it keeps the policy from premature convergence to a memorized pattern. However, convergence of the Q-function or value baseline (if used) is still limited by data quantity. Rated medium rather than high because the sparse reward signal (~95% zero-hit plays) limits learning even with good entropy regularization.

- **Regularization Built In:**
  > Entropy bonus is the core built-in regularization: the policy is penalized for low entropy at every update, preventing collapse to a deterministic subset of numbers. The temperature coefficient alpha controls regularization strength -- high alpha forces high entropy (near-uniform play generation), low alpha allows exploitation of learned Q-values. Automatic alpha tuning (dual-gradient descent targeting a minimum entropy threshold) is available in SAC-style implementations. No dropout or weight decay is intrinsic to MaxEnt RL itself, but they can be freely combined. KL-regularized variants (penalizing KL divergence from a reference policy rather than raw entropy) are also common and add an additional anchor against deviation from prior behavior.

- **Known Failure Modes On Small Data:**
  > Entropy over-regularization: if alpha is set too high, the policy remains near-uniform regardless of reward signal, effectively ignoring what little the model has learned from 100-200 draws. Alpha needs careful manual tuning or automatic scheduling with small datasets. Entropy plateau: with very sparse rewards, the entropy bonus dominates the gradient and the policy optimizes for entropy rather than reward, producing diverse plays with no hit-rate improvement over random. Value function noise: if MaxEnt RL is used with a critic (SAC), Q-function bootstrapping on fewer than 200 transitions can be noisy, causing instability in the temperature-adjusted Q targets. Log-probability overflow: computing log pi(a) for near-zero probabilities can cause numerical instability unless logits are clamped.

- **Convergence Guarantees Small N:**
  > For tabular MDPs, MaxEnt RL has well-defined optimal solutions: the soft Bellman equations have a unique fixed point and policy gradient ascent on the MaxEnt objective converges to this fixed point under standard conditions (Ziebart 2010, Haarnoja 2017). These guarantees are asymptotic and do not provide finite-sample rates for n < 200. With deep function approximators, no finite-sample convergence bounds exist. Empirically, the entropy bonus stabilizes training compared to pure reward-seeking objectives (less divergence, more consistent hit rates), but this is anecdotal rather than formally proven for small n. The most relevant guarantee is that the MaxEnt optimal policy is strictly stochastic (non-degenerate) by construction, which rules out the worst failure mode (complete mode collapse) even at convergence.

- **Reward Distribution Handling:**
  > MaxEnt RL does not directly address reward sparsity -- it adds entropy to the objective but does not reshape the reward distribution. However, the entropy bonus -alpha * log pi(a|s) acts as a dense pseudo-reward at every action step, providing a non-zero gradient signal even when the draw reward is zero. This is critical for the lottery domain where ~95% of plays score 0 hits. The effective reward becomes r_t + alpha * H(pi_t), which is always non-zero when entropy is non-zero. The lottery reward distribution (0 for no hit, 1-7 for hits) is handled by the underlying policy gradient or actor-critic algorithm; MaxEnt RL does not need distributional return modeling. Minimum draws needed for stable learning: approximately 50-100 draws are sufficient to avoid complete gradient collapse, with alpha providing the floor of gradient signal.

- **Credit Assignment Granularity:**
  > MaxEnt RL assigns entropy credit at the action-generation step level: each of the k autoregressive steps receives an entropy bonus proportional to -alpha * log pi(number_t | selected_so_far). This is number-level credit, not just play-level credit. The draw reward (number of hits) is assigned at play level unless explicitly shaped. Combining number-level entropy credit with number-level reward shaping (semi-bandit feedback: reward +1 for each number that appears in the draw) gives the finest-grained credit assignment possible and is the recommended approach for lottery-nn. The entropy bonus alone is not a substitute for number-level reward -- it incentivizes diversity, not accuracy.

- **Diversity Mechanism:**
  > The entropy bonus is the direct diversity mechanism. Maximizing H(pi) penalizes the policy for concentrating probability mass on any single number or small subset of numbers, forcing the softmax distribution to remain spread out across candidates at each generation step. This directly prevents the known failure mode of temperature-softmax generation (repeated identical plays when temperature is too low). Across multiple plays generated in a single predict call, diversity is achieved by sampling from the stochastic policy independently for each play -- high policy entropy ensures the independent samples span different subsets. An explicit inter-play diversity reward (e.g., Hamming distance between plays) can be added on top of the entropy bonus for additional diversification.

- **Offline Vs Online Rl Compatibility:**
  > High offline compatibility. MaxEnt RL as a technique is orthogonal to the online/offline distinction -- it modifies the objective function but not the data collection mechanism. When layered onto an offline algorithm (e.g., CQL, IQL, or batch REINFORCE on historical draws), the entropy bonus continues to function correctly: it discourages the policy from collapsing to the actions most represented in the historical dataset. This is particularly valuable in the lottery domain where the historical dataset is fixed (one draw per week, no online interaction possible). The entropy bonus also partially mitigates distributional shift artifacts (overconfident Q-values for out-of-distribution plays) by keeping the policy from deviating too far from a diverse distribution. Fully offline-compatible without modification.

- **Complexity:**
  > Low. MaxEnt RL is a modification of the training objective, not a new architecture. Adding an entropy bonus to an existing REINFORCE or Actor-Critic training loop requires approximately 5-15 lines of code: compute the entropy of the policy distribution at each step, multiply by alpha, subtract from the loss (or add to reward). Integrating automatic alpha tuning (dual gradient descent on a target entropy constraint) adds another 20-30 lines. The technique slots cleanly into the existing predict.py and any future actor_critic.py without structural changes to the SharedLotteryTransformer.

- **Key Dependencies:**
  > PyTorch only (torch.distributions.Categorical.entropy() provides the exact entropy in one line). No external RL library required. Optional: torchrl for replay buffer management if combined with off-policy training.

- **Estimated Lines Of Code:**
  > 30-80 lines total: entropy computation per step (~5 lines), entropy bonus integration into loss/reward (~10 lines), alpha hyperparameter or automatic tuning (~15-50 lines), logging of per-step entropy for diagnostics (~10 lines). If combined with an existing REINFORCE or Actor-Critic loop, the entropy bonus is a minimal add-on.

- **Transfer Learning Compatibility:**
  > Full compatibility. MaxEnt RL is purely an objective-level modification -- it adds an entropy term to the loss function. The SharedLotteryTransformer encoder architecture, weights, and lottery_id embeddings are completely unchanged. The entropy bonus is computed from the softmax output of the existing per-lottery heads, requiring no new parameters. The pretrained swarm checkpoint (models/best_swarm.pt) can be loaded and fine-tuned with the MaxEnt objective from epoch 1, with the entropy bonus immediately active. This is the lowest-friction RL augmentation possible: zero architectural changes, zero new parameters, minimal code addition.

- **Relevance Score:** 5
- **Specific Application In Project:**
  > MaxEnt RL is the single highest-priority Phase 2 addition because it directly fixes the documented failure mode of the current generation system. In src/predict.py (and main_swarm.py predict), the current temperature-softmax sampling can collapse to near-identical plays when temperature is low (exploitation) or generate fully random plays when temperature is high (pure exploration) -- neither gives diverse, informed predictions. Adding an entropy bonus to the REINFORCE training loop (when fine-tuning on historical draws) penalizes the policy for repeating the same number combinations. Concretely: (1) after each draw is logged via main_swarm.py log, run N gradient steps of REINFORCE where the loss = -reward * log_prob - alpha * entropy; (2) the entropy term is computed from the per-step masked softmax distribution; (3) alpha is a new hyperparameter in config.py (suggested default: 0.01-0.1); (4) plays generated by predict.py will automatically span a wider range of number combinations across calls. Applies to all three lotteries (lottomax, 649, dailygrand) via the shared encoder. This is a prerequisite for the Actor-Critic play generation in Phase 2 -- any actor-critic system should include MaxEnt regularization by default.

- **Integration Point:** Phase 2 (Actor-Critic play generation)
- **Replaces Or Extends:**
  > Extends temperature-softmax sampling in src/predict.py -- the temperature parameter remains but is now supplemented by an entropy bonus during training rather than only controlling inference diversity. Does not replace any component; adds an entropy regularization term to the training loss in any future actor_critic.py or REINFORCE fine-tuning loop.

- **Existing Open Source Implementations:**
  > 1. Haarnoja et al. SAC implementation (official): github.com/haarnoja/sac -- includes entropy tuning. 2. CleanRL: cleanrl/sac_continuous_action.py and sac_atari.py -- entropy coefficient auto-tuning included, clean PyTorch. 3. Stable-Baselines3: SAC and TQC implementations include automatic entropy tuning for continuous spaces. 4. torchrl (Meta/PyTorch): SACLoss class includes entropy computation. 5. PyTorch built-in: torch.distributions.Categorical.entropy() returns exact entropy for discrete policies in one call, making custom implementation trivial. 6. OpenAI Spinning Up: spinningup/algos/pytorch/sac/ -- clean entropy-regularized actor-critic reference.

- **Benchmark Results:**
  > Haarnoja et al. (2018): SAC (MaxEnt RL + off-policy) outperforms PPO, DDPG, and TD3 on MuJoCo continuous control tasks in both final performance and sample efficiency, with entropy regularization credited for preventing premature convergence. Ziebart et al. (2010): MaxEnt IRL outperforms max-margin IRL on taxi route prediction with ~200 expert trajectories, demonstrating robustness on small demonstration datasets. Christodoulou (2019): SAC-Discrete with entropy regularization matches DQN on Atari with fewer environment steps. No published benchmarks directly on combinatorial discrete action spaces with N=50, k=7 or lottery-style prediction. The entropy bonus's effect on play diversity is deterministic by construction (higher alpha = higher entropy = more diverse plays) and does not require empirical validation for this specific property.

- **Notable Use Cases:**
  > 1. Robot locomotion (SAC with entropy bonus prevents gait collapse on sparse terrain rewards -- directly analogous to play collapse). 2. Inverse RL for human trajectory prediction (Ziebart 2010, ~200-500 demonstrations, structurally similar to 100-200 draw history). 3. Recommendation systems with entropy regularization to prevent filter bubble collapse -- selects k items from N candidates, sparse implicit feedback, mirrors the lottery structure. 4. Molecular generation with MaxEnt RL (select atom types sequentially from a discrete pool, sparse reward from docking score -- combinatorial discrete output with sparse reward, very similar to lottery number selection). 5. Text generation with entropy-regularized language models (prevents output collapse to repetitive phrases -- same collapse phenomenon as repeated lottery plays).

### Reflexion: Self-Critique Loop for Candidate Generation {#reflexion-self-critique-loop-for-candidate-generation}

**Type:** Technique | **Relevance:** 5/5 | **Integration:** Phase 2 (Actor-Critic play generation)

**General**

- **Year:** 2023
- **Key Paper:** Shinn, N., Cassano, F., Labash, B., Gopinath, A., Narasimhan, K., & Yao, S. (2023). Reflexion: Language Agents with Verbal Reinforcement Learning. NeurIPS 2023.
- **Algorithm Description:**
  > Reflexion is a framework in which an agent generates a candidate output, receives a scalar or verbal evaluation signal, then produces a natural-language or structured self-critique that is appended to the context before the next generation attempt. The loop is: (1) Actor generates a candidate (e.g., a play), (2) Evaluator scores it against a heuristic or oracle, (3) Self-Reflection module verbalizes what was wrong and how to improve, (4) the critique is stored in a short-term memory buffer, and (5) the Actor re-generates conditioned on the critique. Unlike gradient-based RL the policy weights are not updated; improvement comes from in-context conditioning. In the lottery adaptation the verbal critique is replaced by a structured JSON critique (e.g., over-represented hot numbers, missing cold numbers, poor gap coverage) which is fed back into the generation prompt or used as a differentiable reward signal to re-rank candidates.

- **Action Space Handling:**
  > Reflexion is action-space agnostic -- it does not sample from a parameterized distribution or compute policy gradients. The Actor can use any generation mechanism (temperature sampling, beam search, greedy decoding). In the lottery context the existing SharedLotteryTransformer produces 50 logits; Reflexion wraps the generation call in an iterative critique-and-revise loop. Each iteration the critique (e.g., 'numbers 3, 7, 14 appeared in 60% of recent draws -- reduce their weight') is encoded as a bias vector added to the logits before the next Gumbel top-k sample. The action space {1..50 choose 7} is explored across iterations rather than through stochastic sampling alone.

- **How Combinatorial Output Is Formed:**
  > Initial candidate generation uses the existing Gumbel top-k or temperature-softmax sampling from SharedLotteryTransformer logits. The Reflexion loop then evaluates the candidate against historical pattern heuristics (frequency deviation, gap analysis, number co-occurrence coverage) and computes a structured critique vector. On subsequent iterations the critique is operationalized as: (a) additive logit adjustments that down-weight over-represented numbers and up-weight under-represented numbers, or (b) a resampling call with a modified temperature and masked logits. The final accepted candidate is the one scoring highest on the composite heuristic after T iterations (typically T=3-5). This is analogous to best-of-T sampling guided by a learned or rule-based Evaluator.

- **Sequential Generation Strategy:**
  > Reflexion operates at the play level rather than the number level -- each iteration generates a full set of k numbers simultaneously (flat multi-hot / joint strategy). The sequential dimension is across iterations of the critique loop, not within a single play. This means inter-number correlations within a play are only as well-modeled as the underlying Actor (the existing SharedLotteryTransformer multi-hot head). Adding autoregressive generation inside the Actor would capture within-play correlations but is orthogonal to the Reflexion outer loop. The iterative loop allows the play to shift holistically based on aggregate critiques (e.g., 'this play is too similar to the last 3 plays -- increase diversity').

- **Gradient Estimator Type:**
  > None -- Reflexion does not backpropagate through the generation process. Improvement is achieved by conditioning the Actor on critique context, not by updating model weights. In the original LLM formulation critiques are verbal strings. In the lottery-nn adaptation critiques are structured scalar adjustments (logit biases or reward shaping signals). If the critique-to-logit adjustment is treated as a differentiable transformation, standard backpropagation through the bias addition is trivial; however this is a minor implementation detail rather than a gradient estimator in the RL sense. No REINFORCE, Gumbel-softmax, or straight-through estimator is involved.

- **Combinatorial Constraint Enforcement:**
  > Constraint enforcement (exactly k distinct numbers from {1..N}) is delegated to the underlying Actor's sampling mechanism, exactly as in the baseline. Reflexion adds no new constraint-enforcement mechanism. After the critique adjusts the logits, the same Gumbel top-k or autoregressive masking sampler is applied to ensure exactly 7 distinct numbers from {1..50}. The critique itself can explicitly flag constraint violations (e.g., 'candidate contains number 51 -- out of range') as a safety check, but this is a degenerate case with a well-implemented Actor.

- **Sample Efficiency Rating:**
  > high -- Reflexion is extremely sample-efficient because it does not require gradient updates from reward signals. Each iteration of the critique loop uses deterministic heuristics computed directly from the historical draw data (frequency tables, gap statistics, co-occurrence matrices), which can be computed exactly from even 50-100 draws. The iterative refinement extracts more value from each observed draw by applying multi-dimensional critique rather than a scalar reward. Empirically Shinn et al. (2023) showed Reflexion outperforms CoT baselines by 22% on HotpotQA with no additional training data. The lottery adaptation inherits this data efficiency: all critique heuristics are non-parametric statistics over the fixed draw history.

- **Regularization Built In:**
  > Reflexion's iterative critique loop provides implicit regularization through diversity enforcement: the Evaluator can explicitly penalize candidates that are too similar to previously generated plays (Jaccard overlap check) or that over-fit to the most recent draws (recency bias detection). No weight decay, dropout, or KL penalty is involved since no model weights are updated. The short-term memory buffer (storing critiques from prior iterations) acts as a form of population-level constraint, preventing the loop from cycling back to the same bad candidates. Entropy of the final play distribution across multiple Reflexion runs is naturally maintained if the Evaluator rewards coverage.

- **Known Failure Modes On Small Data:**
  > 1. Critique overfitting: with only 100 draws the frequency statistics used in the critique are noisy -- the Evaluator may penalize numbers that appear rare by chance rather than by true rarity, causing the critique loop to systematically avoid numbers with limited evidence. 2. Heuristic gaming: if the critique heuristics are based solely on historical frequency, the Actor learns to generate plays that look historically typical rather than informationally diverse -- critique-guided convergence to the empirical distribution rather than the generative process. 3. Loop divergence: if critique signals from successive iterations contradict each other (iteration 1: 'include more hot numbers'; iteration 2: 'reduce hot numbers'), the loop oscillates without converging. 4. Critique quality ceiling: rule-based critiques are limited by the quality of the heuristics; with 100 draws, pattern-based insights have wide confidence intervals and the critique may be uninformative or misleading.

- **Reward Distribution Handling:**
  > Reflexion decouples the critique signal from the sparse end-of-episode reward. The Evaluator in the lottery context uses dense intermediate signals (frequency statistics, gap coverage, co-occurrence scores) rather than waiting for the weekly draw outcome. This effectively replaces the extremely sparse {0, 1, 2, ..., 7} hit-count reward with a dense multi-dimensional critique score computed immediately from historical data. This is a major advantage over gradient-based RL methods that must wait for actual draw outcomes. The iterative refinement loop can incorporate the actual draw result when available (post-draw critique: 'play missed numbers in the 30-40 range which appeared -- next iteration should cover that gap') as a form of delayed Reflexion.

- **Credit Assignment Granularity:**
  > Reflexion naturally supports number-level credit assignment through the structured critique. The Evaluator can analyze each individual number in the candidate play: is number i over-represented in recent draws? does the pair (i, j) co-occur too often in the play? is number i in a cold streak? This per-number critique is richer than the play-level scalar reward of vanilla RL -- it is semi-bandit feedback built into the Evaluator design rather than extracted from a gradient estimator. The critique can explicitly identify which k numbers should be swapped and which k numbers should be added, giving direct number-level guidance without requiring a differentiable reward decomposition.

- **Diversity Mechanism:**
  > Reflexion supports explicit diversity enforcement through the Evaluator: a critique can penalize plays that are too similar to previously accepted plays (Jaccard similarity > threshold), forcing the Actor to explore different number combinations across iterations. Additionally, across multiple Reflexion runs (each generating one play), the short-term memory buffer stores all previously accepted plays and the Evaluator can reject candidates that overlap with them. This is more principled than entropy bonuses or temperature scaling because diversity is enforced at the play-set level rather than at the per-number probability level. For a 5-play ticket, 5 sequential Reflexion runs with a diversity penalty in the Evaluator guarantee that no two plays share more than floor(k/2) numbers.

- **Offline Vs Online Rl Compatibility:**
  > Fully offline -- Reflexion requires no online environment interaction for the critique loop itself. All heuristics (frequency tables, gap statistics, co-occurrence matrices) are precomputed from the fixed historical draw dataset and do not require waiting for new draws. The post-draw update (incorporating the actual result into the critique memory) is optional and additive -- the system degrades gracefully to pure heuristic-based critique when no new draws are available. This is the best possible offline compatibility among all Phase 2 candidates: the loop runs entirely at prediction time using existing data, with zero online RL overhead.

- **Complexity:** low
- **Key Dependencies:**
  > No additional libraries required beyond PyTorch and numpy (for heuristic statistics). Optional: pandas (already present in lottery-nn) for frequency table computation. No stable-baselines3, torchrl, pyro, or RL framework required. The Evaluator is a pure Python function computing statistics over draws.csv. If the critique is verbalized as natural language (LLM-in-the-loop), an Anthropic API call would be needed, but the structured critique variant requires no LLM.

- **Estimated Lines Of Code:**
  > 120-180 lines of new code: ~40 lines for the Evaluator (frequency deviation, gap score, diversity penalty), ~30 lines for the critique-to-logit-adjustment function, ~30 lines for the Reflexion loop (T iterations, short-term memory buffer), ~20 lines for integration into main_swarm.py predict path. Excludes existing src/analysis.py helpers that are directly reusable as Evaluator components.

- **Transfer Learning Compatibility:**
  > Fully compatible -- Reflexion wraps the existing SharedLotteryTransformer as a black box and does not modify its weights. The pretrained shared encoder and per-lottery output heads are used exactly as trained; Reflexion only modifies the logits at inference time (additive critique bias) and wraps the sampling call in an iterative loop. No fine-tuning, gradient updates, or architectural changes are required. The existing src/analysis.py frequency_table(), hot_cold(), pair_frequency(), and gap_analysis() helpers are directly reusable as Evaluator components, further reducing implementation effort.

- **Relevance Score:** 5
- **Specific Application In Project:**
  > Reflexion is the most immediately implementable Phase 2 component and directly implements the 'Actor-Critic play generation' loop described in CLAUDE.md. Concrete implementation: (1) Actor: existing SharedLotteryTransformer produces logits for the requested lottery (lottomax/649/dailygrand); (2) Evaluator: a new src/evaluator.py module computes a structured critique dict from the candidate play using helpers from src/analysis.py -- fields include frequency_deviation (how much each number deviates from its historical frequency), gap_score (are the numbers spread across the {1..50} range?), recency_bias (do any numbers have cold streaks longer than X draws?), and diversity_penalty (Jaccard overlap with already-accepted plays in this session); (3) Reflexion loop in src/reflexion.py: for T=3 iterations, convert critique to an additive logit bias vector and resample; (4) Accept the play with the highest composite Evaluator score across all T iterations; (5) Integration: main_swarm.py predict calls reflexion.generate_play() instead of predict.generate_play(). This replaces the static temperature-softmax sampling in src/predict.py with an iterative critique-guided refinement. All three lotteries benefit equally. Phase 2 implementation estimated at 1-2 days.

- **Integration Point:** Phase 2 (Actor-Critic play generation)
- **Replaces Or Extends:**
  > Extends -- replaces temperature-softmax sampling in src/predict.py with a Reflexion-guided iterative sampling loop while keeping the SharedLotteryTransformer encoder and output heads unchanged. The Evaluator adds a new scoring layer on top of the existing src/analysis.py statistical helpers. No existing component is removed.

- **Existing Open Source Implementations:**
  > 1. Official Reflexion repo: noahshinn/reflexion (GitHub, 2023) -- Python implementation for HotpotQA, AlfWorld, HumanEval; uses LLM for both Actor and Self-Reflection. 2. LangChain ReflexionAgent (langchain/agents/reflexion) -- production-ready wrapper, adds overhead of LangChain dependency. 3. AutoGPT and BabyAGI both implement iterative self-critique loops structurally similar to Reflexion without the verbal RL framing. 4. For the structured (non-LLM) variant relevant to lottery-nn there is no off-the-shelf implementation -- the critique is a domain-specific heuristic function rather than a language model, making a custom implementation (~150 lines) more appropriate than adapting the LLM-centric repos.

- **Notable Use Cases:**
  > 1. HumanEval code generation (Shinn et al. 2023): generate code, run unit tests, critique errors, revise -- structurally identical to generate play, evaluate against historical heuristics, critique, revise. 2. Iterative molecular design (Gao et al. 2022, REINVENT): generate molecular candidate, score against docking/ADMET heuristics, revise SMILES -- closest structural analog to generate lottery play, score against statistical patterns, revise. 3. AlphaCode / Codex iterative sampling: generate N candidates, re-rank by test-case pass rate -- equivalent to generating T plays and accepting the highest-scoring one after Reflexion critique. 4. Constrained text generation with iterative constraint checking (Lu et al. 2022, Neurologic Decoding): generate candidate, check combinatorial constraints, revise -- directly analogous to checking lottery play against frequency/gap/diversity constraints. 5. Combinatorial auction bidding with iterative refinement (Dutting et al. 2019): generate bid set, evaluate against value function, revise -- same iterative combinatorial selection pattern.

### REINFORCE with Leave-One-Out Baseline (RLOO) {#reinforce-with-leave-one-out-baseline-rloo}

**Type:** RL Algorithm | **Relevance:** 5/5 | **Integration:** Phase 2 (Actor-Critic play generation)

**General**

- **Year:** 2024
- **Key Paper:** Ahmadian et al., 'Back to Basics: Revisiting REINFORCE-Style Optimization for Language Models with RLOO', ACL 2024 (also arXiv:2402.14740, February 2024)
- **Algorithm Description:**
  > RLOO is a multi-sample REINFORCE variant that computes a separate baseline for each sample by averaging the returns of all other k-1 samples in the same batch. For k samples drawn from the policy for the same input, the gradient for sample i uses the mean return of the remaining k-1 samples as its control variate (leave-one-out baseline). This removes the need for a separate learned value network (critic), reducing parameter count and training complexity. The estimator is unbiased conditioned on the other k-1 samples and achieves lower variance than single-sample REINFORCE as k increases. Ahmadian et al. show it consistently outperforms PPO-style Actor-Critic on RLHF alignment tasks, especially when the dataset is small, because the critic does not need to be separately trained and cannot overfit.

- **Action Space Handling:**
  > RLOO is policy-agnostic: it handles whatever action space the underlying policy defines. For discrete multi-label outputs (lottery numbers), the policy network produces logits over {1..N} and samples are drawn independently per position or autoregressively. RLOO imposes no constraints on how samples are drawn — it only wraps the sampling step with a leave-one-out averaging baseline. The k samples per input are drawn i.i.d. from the current policy.

- **How Combinatorial Output Is Formed:**
  > RLOO itself does not specify the combinatorial generation mechanism — it is a gradient estimator that wraps any sampling policy. For lottery-number generation, the existing policy (e.g. temperature-softmax over 50 balls, repeated 7 times with masking to enforce distinctness) would be used unchanged. The k plays generated per draw would each be a full combinatorial output; RLOO then computes the baseline across those k plays. Autoregressive masking or post-hoc rejection sampling for distinctness can be used in combination with RLOO without modification.

- **Sequential Generation Strategy:**
  > RLOO is compatible with both joint (flat multi-hot BCEWithLogitsLoss sampling) and autoregressive generation. In the lottery-nn codebase the existing joint generation (7 independent sigmoid draws with post-hoc uniqueness enforcement) could be used as-is. For higher-quality combinatorial outputs, autoregressive masking (sample ball 1, mask it, sample ball 2 from remaining 49, etc.) pairs naturally with RLOO because each full sequence has a well-defined log-probability used in the REINFORCE score-function gradient.

- **Gradient Estimator Type:**
  > RLOO uses the score-function (REINFORCE) gradient estimator with a leave-one-out control variate. For k samples {x_1, ..., x_k} from policy pi, the gradient for sample x_i is: grad_theta log pi(x_i) * (R(x_i) - (1/(k-1)) * sum_{j != i} R(x_j)). This is an unbiased estimator of the policy gradient with variance reduced by approximately 1/k relative to single-sample REINFORCE. No reparameterization trick is used; the estimator works directly on discrete samples. It is strictly lower-variance than vanilla REINFORCE and avoids the bias introduced by a separately-trained critic.

- **Combinatorial Constraint Enforcement:**
  > RLOO does not itself enforce combinatorial constraints — it operates on rewards from complete plays. Constraint enforcement is delegated to the policy: autoregressive masking (most principled, exact log-prob computation), rejection sampling (simplest, but wasteful), or differentiable top-k (approximate, allows backprop but not needed since RLOO uses score-function gradients). For lottery-nn, autoregressive masking is the recommended pairing because it gives exact log-probabilities for the REINFORCE gradient.

- **Sample Efficiency Rating:**
  > High — RLOO achieves higher sample efficiency than Actor-Critic on small datasets because it eliminates the critic network that must itself be fitted from data. With fewer than 200 training draws, a learned critic tends to overfit the value function, introducing bias that hurts policy updates. RLOO's leave-one-out baseline is computed entirely from within-batch samples, requiring no additional data. The quality of the baseline improves with k (samples per input) rather than requiring more training data, making it well-suited to the lottery-nn regime of ~100 draws per lottery.

- **Regularization Built In:**
  > RLOO has no built-in regularization beyond what the policy itself provides. Common additions used alongside RLOO include: (1) entropy bonus on the policy logits to prevent mode collapse (adding lambda * H[pi] to the reward), (2) KL penalty from a reference/frozen policy (used in RLHF settings), (3) gradient clipping (standard practice). For lottery-nn, an entropy bonus on the 50-ball logits is strongly recommended to maintain diversity across the k plays generated per draw.

- **Known Failure Modes On Small Data:**
  > 1. High variance when k is small (k=2 is near-equivalent to single-sample REINFORCE). A minimum of k=4 to k=8 samples per input is needed to get meaningful baseline quality. 2. Reward sparsity collapse: if all k plays score 0 hits in a batch, the leave-one-out baseline is 0 for all, producing zero gradient — identical to the lottery sparse-reward problem. Mitigation: use number-level (semi-bandit) credit assignment rather than play-level reward. 3. Policy collapse toward a fixed set if entropy regularization is omitted. 4. Gradient noise amplification on very small batches; mini-batch size should be at least k*num_inputs per update step.

- **Reward Distribution Handling:**
  > RLOO handles sparse reward through the leave-one-out baseline: when most plays score 0, the baseline is near 0, and any play with 1+ hits produces a positive advantage, amplifying its gradient signal. This is structurally better than a learned critic that may predict near-zero value everywhere and suppress gradients entirely. For the lottery-nn sparse reward (~95% zero-hit plays), number-level semi-bandit credit assignment (reward each of the 7 drawn numbers by how often it appears in winning draws rather than only scoring the full play) is recommended to increase signal density. Distributional returns are not natively supported but can be layered on separately.

- **Credit Assignment Granularity:**
  > RLOO assigns credit at the play level by default (one reward R(x_i) per complete play x_i). This is the weakest form of credit assignment for lottery prediction. However, RLOO can be extended to number-level credit by defining the reward as a sum over individual ball selections: R(x_i) = sum_j r(ball_j), where r(ball_j) is the historical hit frequency of ball_j. This semi-bandit decomposition is compatible with autoregressive RLOO and significantly increases gradient signal density from ~0.05 non-zero rewards per play to up to 7 non-zero rewards per play.

- **Diversity Mechanism:**
  > RLOO generates k plays per input in each forward pass, providing natural within-batch diversity. However, the policy may still collapse to a single mode across training steps if entropy is not regularized. Recommended mechanisms for lottery-nn: (1) entropy bonus H[pi] added to the per-ball reward, (2) within-batch diversity reward penalizing plays that duplicate balls chosen by other plays in the same batch, (3) temperature annealing starting at T>1 and reducing over training. The k=5-8 range provides reasonable diversity without excessive compute.

- **Offline Vs Online Rl Compatibility:**
  > RLOO is an on-policy algorithm: it requires drawing fresh samples from the current policy at each update step. In the lottery-nn setting this means generating k candidate plays per historical draw date, computing their simulated reward against the actual draw result, then updating the policy. This is fully compatible with offline historical data — the 'environment' is the historical draw record, not a live draw. Each training step samples k plays, looks up the historical result for that date, and computes rewards. No live draw interaction is required. This makes RLOO highly practical for lottery-nn: one pass through the ~100 historical draws per lottery, generating k=5-8 plays per draw, gives 500-800 reward signals per epoch.

- **Complexity:** low
- **Key Dependencies:**
  > PyTorch only. No additional libraries required beyond the existing lottery-nn stack. RLOO is implemented as a custom loss function (~30 lines of PyTorch). The TRL library (Hugging Face) includes a reference RLOO implementation (trl.RLOOTrainer) if a reference implementation is desired, but it is not required.

- **Estimated Lines Of Code:**
  > 50-80 lines: ~30 lines for the RLOO gradient computation (sample k plays, compute leave-one-out baseline, compute weighted log-prob loss), ~20 lines for the training loop wrapper in a new src/actor_critic.py, and ~10-20 lines to wire into main_swarm.py as a new 'rl-train' subcommand.

- **Transfer Learning Compatibility:**
  > Excellent. RLOO wraps the existing policy (SharedLotteryTransformer) and fine-tunes it with policy gradient updates. The pretrained encoder weights are used as initialization; only the output heads and optionally the top transformer layers need gradient updates. No architectural changes are required. The lottery_id embedding and per-lottery heads remain intact. RLOO is the most transfer-learning-friendly Actor-Critic alternative precisely because it adds no new network components (no critic head) — it only changes the loss computation.

- **Relevance Score:** 5
- **Specific Application In Project:**
  > RLOO is the recommended Phase 2 Actor-Critic play generation method for lottery-nn. Concrete application: (1) For each historical draw date in draws_649.csv / draws_dailygrand.csv / draws.csv, generate k=6 candidate plays using the SharedLotteryTransformer with autoregressive masking. (2) Score each play against the actual draw result using semi-bandit number-level reward (count of matching numbers). (3) Compute the leave-one-out baseline for each play across the k=6 batch. (4) Update policy via -log_prob * advantage. This replaces the current temperature-softmax predict.py generation with a reward-driven policy that learns which ball distributions produce higher hit rates. Runs on the existing ~100-draw datasets per lottery without requiring a critic.

- **Integration Point:** Phase 2 (Actor-Critic play generation)
- **Replaces Or Extends:**
  > Replaces temperature-softmax sampling in src/predict.py with a reward-driven REINFORCE policy. The SharedLotteryTransformer encoder and per-lottery heads are reused; only the generation and loss computation change. The Thompson sampling bandit in src/bandit.py continues to operate on top of RLOO-generated plays.

- **Existing Open Source Implementations:**
  > 1. Hugging Face TRL library: trl.RLOOTrainer (official implementation, added in TRL v0.8+, designed for LLM RLHF but adaptable). 2. Original paper code: https://github.com/huggingface/trl/tree/main/trl/trainer (integrated into TRL). 3. Minimal standalone implementations exist in community repos (search 'RLOO REINFORCE leave one out pytorch' on GitHub). The algorithm is simple enough (~30 lines) that a from-scratch implementation is preferred for lottery-nn to avoid TRL's LLM-centric abstractions.

- **Benchmark Results:**
  > Ahmadian et al. (2024): On Summarize from Feedback (Reddit TL;DR), RLOO with k=8 outperforms PPO (Actor-Critic) by 1-3 win-rate percentage points against the reference model as judged by GPT-4, while using 2x fewer GPU resources (no critic). On Anthropic HH dataset, RLOO matches or exceeds PPO at all scales tested (1B-7B parameters). Key finding: the learned critic in PPO introduces estimation error that RLOO avoids entirely. RLOO also outperforms single-sample REINFORCE by a significant margin (variance reduction from the leave-one-out baseline). RLOO with k=3 already matches PPO; k=8 exceeds it.

- **Notable Use Cases:**
  > 1. RLHF alignment of language models (primary use case in Ahmadian et al.) — structurally similar to lottery in that reward is sparse (human preference labels) and dataset is finite. 2. Code generation RL fine-tuning: RLOO has been used to fine-tune code LLMs on unit-test pass/fail reward (binary sparse reward, similar to lottery hit/miss). 3. Mathematical reasoning (DeepSeek-R1 uses a REINFORCE-style multi-sample approach related to GRPO, a close relative of RLOO). 4. Combinatorial optimization via RL: pointer network training with REINFORCE leave-one-out baselines has been used for TSP and VRP (discrete combinatorial outputs, structurally analogous to selecting k numbers from {1..N}).
