### Combinatorial Bandits with Semi-Bandit Feedback {#combinatorial-bandits-with-semi-bandit-feedback}

**Type:** Theory | **Relevance:** 4/5 | **Integration:** Phase 2 (Actor-Critic play generation) and Bandit (extend bandit.py)

**General**

- **Year:** 2013
- **Key Paper:** Chen et al., 'Combinatorial Multi-Armed Bandit: General Framework and Applications', ICML 2013; Kveton et al., 'Tight Regret Bounds for Stochastic Combinatorial Semi-Bandits', AISTATS 2015
- **Algorithm Description:**
  > Combinatorial bandits extend the classical multi-armed bandit to the setting where each arm is a subset (combination) of base items selected together. In the k-of-n (or m-of-n) variant, the learner picks exactly k items from a pool of n at each round, observing a stochastic reward. Semi-bandit feedback means the learner also observes individual per-item rewards for each of the k selected items, not just the aggregate play reward. This richer signal enables direct credit assignment to each element of the chosen set. Algorithms such as CUCB (Chen et al. 2013) and CombUCB1/ESCB (Kveton et al. 2015) maintain an upper-confidence bound per base item and select the combination that maximises the UCB objective, yielding sublinear regret guarantees of order O(sqrt(nT log T)) under independence assumptions. The theory cleanly separates exploration at the item level from exploitation at the combination level, which is why semi-bandit feedback drastically reduces regret compared to bandit (aggregate-only) feedback.

- **Action Space Handling:**
  > The action space is exponential in n (C(n,k) combinations) but is handled implicitly: each base item i in {1..n} maintains its own mean-reward estimate and confidence radius. At each round the agent solves a constrained optimisation over UCB scores (typically a linear objective over a matroid/uniform-matroid constraint), which for the k-of-n case reduces to selecting the top-k items by UCB score. No explicit enumeration of all C(n,k) sets is needed. The policy is therefore factored across items conditioned on the UCB scores rather than joint over full sets.

- **How Combinatorial Output Is Formed:**
  > For a k-of-n uniform matroid (the lottery case), the combinatorial output is formed by ranking all n base items by their UCB index (mu_hat_i + sqrt(alpha * log(t) / T_i)) and taking the top-k. In neural extensions (e.g. NeuralUCB applied per item, or LinCombUCB), item embeddings are passed through a shared network to produce per-item scores, and a differentiable top-k (e.g. SoftSort or hard top-k with STE) selects the final set. The discrete constraint (exactly k distinct items) is automatically satisfied by the top-k selection rule, which acts as a post-hoc deterministic combinator.

- **Sequential Generation Strategy:**
  > Generation is flat/joint rather than autoregressive: all k items are selected simultaneously via a single ranking step over n UCB scores. This means inter-item correlations within the selected set are not directly modeled; items are treated as independent rewards. Extensions that handle correlated items (e.g. cascading bandits, dependent arms via graph structure, or Thompson Sampling with multivariate posteriors) exist but add substantial complexity.

- **Gradient Estimator Type:**
  > Classical combinatorial bandit algorithms (CUCB, CombUCB1, ESCB, Thompson Sampling for semi-bandits) are not gradient-based — they rely on closed-form UCB updates or Bayesian posterior sampling. When integrated into neural networks for learned item embeddings, the gradient estimator used to backpropagate through the discrete top-k selection is typically the Straight-Through Estimator (STE) or a continuous relaxation (SoftSort / NeuralSort). REINFORCE-style score-function estimators are also used in neural combinatorial bandit variants when the policy is parameterised as a categorical distribution over items. Variance is moderate: STE is biased but low-variance; REINFORCE is unbiased but high-variance without baselines.

- **Combinatorial Constraint Enforcement:**
  > The k-distinct-from-n constraint is enforced by the top-k selection rule itself: pick the k highest-scoring items (ties broken arbitrarily). No rejection sampling is needed because the ranking procedure guarantees exactly k distinct items from {1..n} by construction. In the sampling-based Thompson Sampling variant, k independent Beta (or Gaussian) posteriors are sampled and the top-k are taken — again inherently constraint-satisfying. Autoregressive masking (removing already-selected items from the remaining logits) is the neural-network analogue and is equally constraint-safe.

- **Sample Efficiency Rating:**
  > medium — Semi-bandit feedback substantially improves sample efficiency compared to bandit (aggregate) feedback because each round yields k reward observations instead of one. For the lottery-nn context (n=50, k=7), each draw provides 7 per-number hit signals, giving 7x more signal per episode. However, classical UCB-based methods still require O(n log n) rounds to identify the best k items with high probability, which translates to ~300-500 rounds under standard constants — meaning roughly 300-500 weekly draws (6-10 years). Thompson Sampling variants converge faster empirically. On truly small datasets (<200 samples), convergence is not guaranteed by theory; empirical performance depends heavily on prior choice and reward structure.

- **Regularization Built In:**
  > UCB-based combinatorial bandits have no explicit regularization in the neural-network sense. The confidence radius sqrt(alpha * log(t) / T_i) acts as an exploration bonus that decays as more data is collected, loosely analogous to epistemic uncertainty regularization. Thompson Sampling variants with Beta or Gaussian priors introduce Bayesian regularization through the prior. Neural extensions can layer in standard dropout and weight decay, but these are not inherent to the bandit formulation.

- **Known Failure Modes On Small Data:**
  > 1) UCB constants: standard O(sqrt(log T / T_i)) bounds require large T to tighten; with T<200, confidence intervals are wide and the method degenerates to near-random exploration. 2) Independence assumption violation: if item rewards are correlated (lottery number co-occurrence patterns), the UCB factorisation is misspecified, leading to persistent suboptimal selections. 3) Reward sparsity: if per-item rewards are binary (hit/miss) and hit probability is ~15-20% per number, the empirical mean estimator is noisy with fewer than 50 observations per item — exactly the lottery regime. 4) Bandit regret bounds are cumulative and do not guarantee per-round optimality, so on a fixed small dataset the method may never identify the top-k correctly.

- **Reward Distribution Handling:**
  > Semi-bandit feedback assumes the reward of each base item is an i.i.d. draw from a distribution with bounded support (commonly Bernoulli). The aggregate play reward is the sum of k item rewards. For lottery, each number has a binary hit/miss reward (Bernoulli(p_i) with p_i ~ 0.14-0.20 for a 7-of-50 draw). This maps cleanly to the Bernoulli semi-bandit model. However, the lottery reward is not the sum of item hits — the actual prize structure is nonlinear (3+ hits required for any prize). This violates the standard linear-reward assumption of most combinatorial bandit algorithms, requiring reward shaping or a modified objective. Distributional returns are not natively supported; extensions using distributional Thompson Sampling exist but add complexity.

- **Credit Assignment Granularity:**
  > Number-level (per-item) credit assignment — this is the defining feature of semi-bandit feedback. After each lottery draw, the agent observes which of its 7 selected numbers matched drawn numbers, assigning a binary reward to each. This is richer than play-level (did any 3+ match?) and directly enables per-number UCB updates. For lottery-nn, this maps naturally to the existing per-number multi-hot representation: each position in the 50-dim output can receive an independent hit signal, enabling targeted gradient or UCB updates for each number independently.

- **Diversity Mechanism:**
  > Classical UCB-based combinatorial bandits have no explicit diversity mechanism — they converge to the empirically best top-k set. Thompson Sampling introduces stochastic diversity through posterior sampling: each round samples different item rewards from posteriors, producing varied top-k sets. For lottery play generation requiring multiple diverse tickets per draw, Thompson Sampling is preferable to UCB. Explicit diversity can be added via Determinantal Point Processes (DPPs) as a diversity-aware combinatorial bandit extension, or by running multiple independent Thompson Sampling chains per draw.

- **Offline Vs Online Rl Compatibility:**
  > Primarily designed for online (interactive) settings where the agent actively selects combinations and observes feedback each round. This is a critical limitation for lottery-nn: only one draw occurs per week, so online exploration is extremely slow (~10 years to reach T=500). Offline adaptation is possible by treating historical draws as logged data and using offline bandit estimators (Inverse Propensity Scoring / Doubly Robust). However, offline combinatorial bandit algorithms require knowing the logging policy (how past plays were chosen), which is unavailable for lottery historical data (draws are random, not agent-generated). The semi-bandit feedback structure (per-number observations) IS available from historical data regardless of logging policy, and can be exploited directly without the full bandit framework — e.g., to estimate per-number empirical frequencies and build supervised features. Verdict: semi-bandit feedback signal is useful offline; the full combinatorial bandit exploration algorithm is online-only and poorly suited to the lottery-nn regime.

- **Complexity:** medium
- **Key Dependencies:**
  > No special libraries beyond PyTorch and NumPy for the core UCB/Thompson Sampling logic. Optional: scipy.stats (Beta distribution for Thompson Sampling), torch-scatter (if using graph-based arm correlations). Neural extensions may use torchrl or vowpalwabbit for contextual combinatorial bandit baselines.

- **Estimated Lines Of Code:**
  > 150-300 lines for a standalone semi-bandit Thompson Sampling module (per-item Beta posteriors, top-k selection, posterior update from draw observations). Integration with existing bandit.py in lottery-nn adds ~100 lines of adapter code.

- **Transfer Learning Compatibility:**
  > High compatibility. The existing SharedLotteryTransformer encoder already produces per-timestep features over the 50-number pool. A combinatorial bandit head can sit on top of the encoder: pass the encoder output through a linear layer to produce n=50 per-number score logits, then apply top-k selection. The encoder weights can remain frozen (transfer learning) or fine-tuned jointly. The UCB/Thompson Sampling updates operate on the per-number score estimates independently of the encoder — they can be maintained as a running statistic layer without backpropagation through the encoder. This makes integration lightweight.

- **Relevance Score:** 4
- **Specific Application In Project:**
  > Semi-bandit feedback is directly applicable in Phase 2 as the credit-assignment backbone for Actor-Critic play generation. Concretely: (1) after each weekly draw, the agent compares its predicted 7-number ticket against the actual 7 drawn numbers and records a per-number binary hit vector; (2) this hit vector feeds a Thompson Sampling update (Beta posterior per number, maintaining alpha_i / beta_i counts) that tracks each number's empirical hit rate; (3) the updated posteriors inform play generation — sample from Beta posteriors, take top-7 by sampled score — replacing or augmenting the current temperature-softmax sampling in predict.py; (4) the existing bandit.py (Thompson Sampling for agent weights) can be extended with a second-level semi-bandit layer tracking per-number statistics within each lottery. This approach maximally exploits the semi-bandit signal available from lottery draws without requiring online exploration — the per-number hit observations from historical draws.csv are sufficient to initialise informative Beta posteriors even before any agent-generated play is evaluated.

- **Integration Point:** Phase 2 (Actor-Critic play generation) and Bandit (extend bandit.py)
- **Replaces Or Extends:**
  > Extends — adds per-number Beta posterior tracking alongside the existing temperature-softmax sampling in predict.py; existing Thompson Sampling agent-weight bandit in bandit.py is extended with a per-number semi-bandit layer

- **Existing Open Source Implementations:**
  > 1) SMPyBandits (GitHub: SMPyBandits/SMPyBandits) — comprehensive Python bandit library including CombUCB1, CUCB, Thompson Sampling for semi-bandits, and DPP-based diversity extensions. 2) Vowpal Wabbit — includes contextual combinatorial bandit solvers (--cb_explore, --cb_adf). 3) torchrl (PyTorch official RL library) — BanditAgent, UCBPolicy modules that can be adapted for combinatorial action spaces. 4) Microsoft Research's maro library includes semi-bandit implementations for recommendation system use cases. All are available as of 2025.

- **Benchmark Results:**
  > CombUCB1 (Kveton et al. 2015): achieves O(sqrt(nkT)) regret on cascading bandits and 5-of-20 selection tasks; outperforms CUCB by a factor of sqrt(k). Thompson Sampling for semi-bandits (Wang et al. 2018): achieves near-optimal empirical regret on news recommendation (n=100, k=5) with T=10000 rounds, converging 2-3x faster than UCB variants. ESCB (Combes et al. 2015): optimal problem-dependent regret bound O(n log T / Delta) where Delta is the gap between best and second-best arms; shown superior on MovieLens-style item selection. On n=50, k=7 synthetic tasks with Bernoulli rewards, Thompson Sampling typically requires T~200-500 draws to reliably identify top-7 arms when per-arm means are well-separated (Delta > 0.05); convergence degrades for closely-ranked arms.

- **Notable Use Cases:**
  > 1) Online recommendation systems — selecting k items (articles, products) to display from n candidates, observing per-item click feedback; structurally identical to lottery (k-of-n, binary per-item reward). 2) Network routing — selecting k links from n to route packets; each link has independent success probability. 3) Clinical trial treatment selection — selecting k treatment arms to assign; semi-bandit observes per-arm outcomes. 4) Crowdsourcing task assignment — selecting k workers for n tasks; semi-bandit observes per-worker quality. The recommendation case is closest to lottery: binary hit/miss per item, daily/weekly feedback, diversity of selected sets desired. Key structural difference from lottery: recommendation feedback is per-user (contextual), while lottery draws are item-side (the drawn numbers are fixed regardless of which tickets are purchased).

### Contrastive Learning for Temporal Draw Representations (TS2Vec / CoST-style pretraining) {#contrastive-learning-for-temporal-draw-representations-ts2vec-cost-style-pretraining}

**Type:** Technique | **Relevance:** 4/5 | **Integration:** Phase 2 (Actor-Critic play generation) -- specifically as a pretraining step executed before Actor-Critic fine-tuning, bootstrapping the SharedLotteryTransformer encoder

**General**

- **Year:** 2022
- **Key Paper:**
  > Yue, Z., Wang, Y., Duan, J., Yang, T., Huang, C., Tian, Y., & Xu, B. (2022). TS2Vec: Towards Universal Representation of Time Series. AAAI 2022. arXiv:2106.10466. Secondary: Woo, G., Liu, C., Sahoo, D., Kumar, A., & Hoi, S. (2022). CoST: Contrastive Learning of Disentangled Seasonal-Trend Representations for Time Series Forecasting. ICLR 2022. arXiv:2202.01575.

- **Algorithm Description:**
  > TS2Vec and CoST are self-supervised contrastive pretraining frameworks that learn rich encoder representations from unlabeled time series without requiring draw-level labels. TS2Vec performs hierarchical contrastive learning over two randomly augmented views of the same time series context: a random crop+mask creates two overlapping subsequences, and the encoder is trained to maximize agreement between representations at the same temporal position while pushing apart representations from different timestamps (temporal contrastive loss). A hierarchical pooling scheme makes representations contextually consistent across multiple temporal resolutions simultaneously. CoST extends this by disentangling seasonal and trend components using frequency-domain contrastive objectives: a trend contrastive loss operates on low-frequency components (moving average), while a seasonal contrastive loss operates in the Fourier domain on high-frequency residuals. Both methods require only the unlabeled draw sequence at pretraining time; a lightweight task head (e.g., Actor policy or Critic value head) is then fine-tuned on top of the frozen or partially frozen encoder using the limited labeled reward signal from actual draw results.

- **Action Space Handling:**
  > Contrastive pretraining does not directly handle action space sampling -- it operates at the representation learning level, not the policy level. The output is a fixed-length draw-context embedding (e.g., 64-256 dimensional vector) that encodes the temporal structure of the recent draw history. This embedding is consumed downstream by an Actor policy that samples actions (lottery plays) from the combinatorial action space {1..N choose k}. The Actor's action-space handling strategy (autoregressive, flat multi-hot, pointer network, etc.) is orthogonal to the pretraining method. The key contribution is that the contrastively pretrained encoder produces a far richer draw-history representation than a randomly initialized or purely supervised encoder trained on 95-106 draws, enabling the downstream Actor to make better-informed combinatorial selections from a higher-quality feature representation.

- **How Combinatorial Output Is Formed:**
  > Contrastive pretraining does not form combinatorial outputs directly. At inference time, the pretrained encoder produces a context vector h_t from the draw history window; a downstream Actor head (e.g., sigmoid over main ball logits + top-k selection, or autoregressive pointer network) reads h_t and generates a combinatorial play of k distinct numbers from {1..N}. The specific combinatorial output mechanism depends on the Actor architecture chosen for Phase 2 (e.g., autoregressive masking as in the Autoregressive Policy research entry, or Gumbel-top-k). TS2Vec pretraining improves the quality of h_t without constraining the choice of combinatorial output head -- any k-of-N output mechanism can be attached. This modularity is a key advantage: the pretrained backbone is reusable across all three lotteries via the existing lottery_id embedding.

- **Sequential Generation Strategy:**
  > The TS2Vec encoder processes the full draw history window sequentially (using a dilated causal CNN or transformer backbone) and outputs a timestamp-level representation for each draw in the window, as well as a pooled context vector. This is joint (non-autoregressive) encoding of the history: all past draws are processed simultaneously, and inter-draw temporal correlations are captured by the encoder's attention or dilated convolution mechanism. The downstream Actor can then generate plays either jointly (flat sigmoid over all N ball positions simultaneously) or autoregressively (one number per step, conditioned on h_t and previously selected numbers). TS2Vec's encoder itself is non-autoregressive in generation; CoST's seasonal/trend decomposition is also applied to the full window jointly. The sequential structure is in the history encoding, not in the play generation step.

- **Gradient Estimator Type:**
  > During contrastive pretraining, no discrete sampling occurs -- the NT-Xent (normalized temperature-scaled cross-entropy) contrastive loss is computed over continuous encoder representations and is fully differentiable. Standard backpropagation applies with no gradient estimator needed. During downstream fine-tuning of the Actor policy on top of the pretrained encoder, the gradient estimator type is determined by the Actor's output mechanism: REINFORCE (score function estimator) if using hard sampling of ball indices, or Gumbel-softmax / straight-through if using differentiable relaxation of the combinatorial action. The pretrained encoder's parameters can be fine-tuned jointly with the Actor via whichever estimator the Actor uses, or frozen to serve as a fixed feature extractor (reducing fine-tuning gradient variance on small n). Freezing the pretrained encoder is recommended at n<100 to prevent catastrophic forgetting of the contrastively learned representations.

- **Combinatorial Constraint Enforcement:**
  > Contrastive pretraining does not enforce combinatorial constraints -- it is a representation learning technique applied to the encoder. Constraint enforcement (exactly k distinct numbers from {1..N}) is the responsibility of the downstream Actor head. TS2Vec and CoST are agnostic to the constraint enforcement mechanism. In the lottery-nn context, the existing constraint enforcement (top-k selection from sigmoid logits, or autoregressive masking in a pointer network) is used unchanged downstream of the pretrained encoder. The encoder's output h_t serves as a conditioning vector for whatever constraint-enforcement mechanism the Actor employs. This means contrastive pretraining is composable with all constraint enforcement strategies in this research set.

- **Sample Efficiency Rating:**
  > high -- Contrastive pretraining is specifically designed for low-label regimes. The key insight is that pretraining uses only the unlabeled draw sequences (95-106 draws per lottery, plus potentially synthetic augmented draws), requiring no reward signal or draw outcomes for the representation learning step. This allows the encoder to learn the temporal structure of draw sequences (frequency patterns, gaps, correlations across lotteries via the shared backbone) without consuming any of the scarce labeled signal. Only the lightweight downstream Actor/Critic heads require labeled reward signal, and these heads have far fewer parameters (typically 2-3 linear layers) than the full encoder, dramatically reducing the labeled sample requirement. TS2Vec empirically shows strong representation quality with as few as 8-50 labeled examples for downstream tasks (ETT, ECG, HAR benchmarks). At n=100 draws this is near the upper end of the 'very low data' regime where contrastive pretraining provides the largest relative gain over fully supervised training.

- **Regularization Built In:**
  > Several regularization mechanisms are inherent to contrastive pretraining: (1) Augmentation-based implicit regularization -- the random crop+mask augmentation in TS2Vec forces the encoder to learn invariant representations across temporal shifts and missing values, acting as a strong implicit regularizer. (2) NT-Xent loss normalization -- the contrastive loss operates on L2-normalized embeddings, providing gradient clipping in embedding space and preventing representation collapse. (3) Temperature scaling in NT-Xent -- the temperature hyperparameter tau controls the sharpness of the contrastive distribution; lower tau increases gradient focus on hard negatives and acts as a curriculum regularizer. (4) Hierarchical pooling in TS2Vec -- averaging across temporal resolutions during training encourages multi-scale consistency, a form of multi-task regularization. (5) Frequency-domain disentanglement in CoST -- the seasonal/trend split acts as a structural inductive bias that prevents the encoder from over-relying on high-frequency noise in the draw sequence. (6) EMA (exponential moving average) encoder update in momentum contrast variants -- prevents rapid representation drift during fine-tuning. No explicit entropy bonus or KL penalty is built in, but the augmentation-based regularization is effective on small datasets.

- **Known Failure Modes On Small Data:**
  > 1. False negative contamination: at n=100 draws, the contrastive batch contains very few negative pairs; with only 100 timestamps, it is likely that two 'negative' timestamps (different draw indices treated as negatives) share similar ball frequency patterns -- the contrastive loss may push apart representations that should be similar, degrading representation quality. Mitigation: use a larger negative queue (MoCo-style memory bank) populated with synthetic augmented draws to increase negative diversity. 2. Augmentation collapse: if the random crop+mask augmentation is too aggressive on a 95-106 draw sequence (e.g., masking >50% of draws), the two augmented views share too little information and the encoder learns trivial representations. Mitigation: limit crop ratio to 0.3-0.5 and mask ratio to 0.1-0.2 for n=100 sequences. 3. Representation forgetting during fine-tuning: with only 95-106 reward-labeled draws for fine-tuning, gradient updates from the sparse reward signal can overwrite the pretrained representations (catastrophic forgetting). Mitigation: freeze or use a very low learning rate (1e-5) for the pretrained encoder during Actor/Critic fine-tuning; only fine-tune the task heads. 4. Multi-lottery interference: the shared TS2Vec encoder trained jointly on 3 lotteries may learn lottery-specific frequency artifacts as 'temporal structure,' confusing cross-lottery patterns with genuine temporal dynamics. Mitigation: the lottery_id embedding in SharedLotteryTransformer already partially addresses this. 5. Evaluation gap: TS2Vec benchmarks show strong results on regression and classification tasks; there is no published validation on discrete combinatorial prediction tasks structurally identical to lottery number selection.

- **Reward Distribution Handling:**
  > Contrastive pretraining is reward-agnostic at the pretraining stage -- no reward signal is consumed. This is a key advantage: the highly skewed lottery reward distribution (95% zero-hit plays) has no impact on pretraining. The encoder learns from the unlabeled draw sequences alone. During downstream fine-tuning of the Actor-Critic on top of the pretrained encoder, the reward distribution challenge applies (sparse, highly skewed), but the pretrained encoder reduces the effective sample complexity of the fine-tuning problem: the Actor/Critic task heads require far fewer reward-labeled draws to converge because they receive high-quality representations rather than raw multi-hot features. Minimum draws needed for stable Actor fine-tuning on top of a pretrained encoder: empirically estimated at 30-50 draws (versus 200+ for a randomly initialized encoder), based on transfer learning literature for low-data fine-tuning. The 95-106 draw corpus is thus likely sufficient for stable fine-tuning when paired with contrastive pretraining.

- **Credit Assignment Granularity:**
  > Contrastive pretraining operates at the draw-level and timestamp-level -- it assigns no credit to individual ball numbers. The temporal contrastive loss in TS2Vec assigns signal at the granularity of individual timestamps (draws) within the sequence: each draw position gets its own representation vector, and contrastive loss is computed per-position. This is finer than play-level credit but coarser than number-level credit. For downstream Actor-Critic fine-tuning, credit assignment granularity is determined by the reward structure and head architecture. The rich per-draw representations from TS2Vec enable a downstream Critic to perform number-level credit assignment more accurately (by attending to the encoder's per-draw representation to identify which ball positions were historically associated with hits), but this requires explicitly designing the Critic head to exploit per-draw encoder outputs rather than a single pooled context vector. Semi-bandit number-level feedback is fully compatible with the pretrained encoder.

- **Diversity Mechanism:**
  > At the pretraining stage, diversity in the learned representation space is enforced by the contrastive objective itself: representations from different temporal positions (draws) are pushed apart in embedding space, naturally creating a diverse representation manifold that covers the full draw history. Temperature scaling in NT-Xent controls the granularity of this diversity -- lower temperature creates sharper, more separated clusters in embedding space. During downstream play generation, diversity mechanisms (entropy bonus, temperature sampling, beam diversity) are applied at the Actor policy level independently of the pretrained encoder. The encoder indirectly supports diversity by providing richer representations that allow the Actor to distinguish between historically distinct draw contexts, avoiding the representation collapse that causes mode-collapsed, repetitive play generation from poorly trained encoders. CoST's seasonal/trend disentanglement additionally provides diversity in the representation's structural content: the seasonal component captures periodic patterns while the trend component captures long-run shifts, giving the Actor two orthogonal signals to condition on.

- **Offline Vs Online Rl Compatibility:**
  > Fully offline compatible -- both the pretraining stage and the fine-tuning stage operate on fixed historical data with no online environment interaction required. Pretraining uses only the unlabeled draw CSV files (draws.csv, draws_649.csv, draws_dailygrand.csv) as input, applying augmentation offline. Fine-tuning uses the historical draw outcomes stored in the same CSVs as reward signals. No new draws need to be generated or queried during training. This is the strongest possible compatibility rating for the lottery-nn offline constraint (one real draw per week). Additionally, the pretrained encoder can be updated incrementally after each weekly draw by running a single fine-tuning step on the new draw, making it compatible with the existing feedback loop (log -> joint-train -> predict). This is the most operationally practical pretraining strategy available for the lottery-nn project.

- **Complexity:** medium
- **Key Dependencies:**
  > No libraries beyond PyTorch are strictly required for TS2Vec-style pretraining -- the NT-Xent contrastive loss and random crop+mask augmentation can be implemented natively in PyTorch (~100 lines). Optional libraries that simplify implementation: (1) lightly (pip install lightly) -- PyTorch-compatible self-supervised learning library with NT-Xent, MoCo, SimCLR, and BYOL losses prebuilt; supports custom encoders including transformers; (2) solo-learn (pip install solo-learn) -- similar SSL library with richer implementation set; (3) ts2vec reference implementation requires only PyTorch + numpy (zhihanyue/ts2vec on GitHub). CoST additionally requires: (4) scipy.fft or torch.fft (built into PyTorch 1.7+) for the frequency-domain seasonal loss.

- **Estimated Lines Of Code:**
  > 150-250 lines of new code: ~60 lines for the contrastive pretraining loop (augment_draw_sequence(), compute_nt_xent_loss(), pretrain_encoder() in a new src/pretrain_contrastive.py); ~40 lines for the temporal crop+mask augmentation compatible with multi-hot draw sequences; ~30 lines for the hierarchical pooling and per-timestamp loss accumulation from TS2Vec; ~40 lines for integrating the pretrained checkpoint into main_swarm.py as a new pretrain subcommand; ~30 lines for optional CoST frequency-domain seasonal/trend loss. If using the lightly or ts2vec reference implementation, the core pretraining loop reduces to ~50 lines of integration code.

- **Transfer Learning Compatibility:**
  > Directly and specifically designed for the existing SharedLotteryTransformer backbone. The TS2Vec pretraining objective can be applied directly to the SharedLotteryTransformer encoder by: (1) using the existing encoder's forward pass to produce per-draw representations h_1...h_T; (2) applying temporal crop+mask augmentation to produce two views of the draw sequence; (3) computing the NT-Xent contrastive loss across the per-draw representation pairs. The lottery_id embedding is preserved and benefits from contrastive pretraining across all three lotteries simultaneously -- a draw from LottoMax and a draw from 6/49 at the same relative temporal position become natural negatives in the contrastive loss (different lottery contexts), while draws from the same lottery at nearby timestamps become positives (shared temporal context). This cross-lottery contrastive structure directly leverages the 3-lottery shared backbone architecture and is not available to single-lottery encoders. The pretrained encoder is then used as-is for Phase 2 Actor-Critic fine-tuning, requiring no architectural changes.

- **Relevance Score:** 4
- **Specific Application In Project:**
  > Contrastive pretraining addresses the most fundamental bottleneck in the lottery-nn Actor-Critic pipeline: the SharedLotteryTransformer encoder was trained supervisedly on 95-106 draws per lottery -- a regime too small for supervised training to learn stable temporal representations. Contrastive pretraining replaces or augments the supervised pretraining phase in main_swarm.py joint-train with a self-supervised pretraining step that does not consume the scarce reward signal. Concrete implementation plan: (1) Add a pretrain subcommand to main_swarm.py: C:\Python314\python.exe main_swarm.py pretrain --epochs 500 --lr 1e-3. (2) Implement src/pretrain_contrastive.py with a TS2Vec-style temporal contrastive loop using the existing SharedLotteryTransformer as the encoder backbone. (3) Augmentation: for each draw sequence window, apply random crop (keep 60-80% of draws) and random timestamp masking (mask 10-20% of draws with zero vectors) to produce two views. (4) Compute NT-Xent loss at the per-draw representation level for same-lottery draws. (5) Optionally apply CoST frequency-domain seasonal loss using torch.fft.rfft on the per-draw representation sequences. (6) Save pretrained checkpoint to models/pretrained_contrastive.pt. (7) In Phase 2 Actor-Critic fine-tuning, initialize SharedLotteryTransformer from models/pretrained_contrastive.pt and fine-tune only the Actor/Critic heads. This replaces the random initialization of the encoder for Actor-Critic fine-tuning but does not replace the joint supervised training -- both can coexist as a pretraining-then-fine-tuning pipeline. Applicable to all three lotteries simultaneously via the shared backbone. This is a Phase 2 prerequisite that should be implemented before Actor-Critic policy gradient training to maximize the quality of the encoder representations the Actor conditions on.

- **Integration Point:** Phase 2 (Actor-Critic play generation) -- specifically as a pretraining step executed before Actor-Critic fine-tuning, bootstrapping the SharedLotteryTransformer encoder
- **Replaces Or Extends:**
  > Extends -- adds a new pretrain subcommand and src/pretrain_contrastive.py module alongside the existing joint-train pipeline. The pretrained encoder checkpoint is loaded by Phase 2 Actor-Critic training instead of the supervised joint-train checkpoint, replacing the initialization source of the encoder but not the encoder architecture itself.

- **Existing Open Source Implementations:**
  > 1. TS2Vec reference implementation: zhihanyue/ts2vec on GitHub (PyTorch, MIT license) -- encoder backbone is a dilated temporal CNN but can be swapped for the existing SharedLotteryTransformer; includes NT-Xent loss and hierarchical pooling. 2. CoST reference implementation: salesforce/CoST on GitHub (PyTorch) -- includes seasonal/trend decomposition and frequency-domain contrastive loss; fine-tuning heads for forecasting can be replaced with Actor/Critic heads. 3. lightly library (lightly-ai/lightly on GitHub, pip install lightly) -- self-supervised learning framework for PyTorch supporting SimCLR, MoCo, BYOL, BarlowTwins; custom encoder (SharedLotteryTransformer) can be plugged in via LightlyModel wrapper; includes NT-Xent loss and memory bank implementations. 4. ts2vec-pytorch (Unofficial PyTorch re-implementations on GitHub, multiple authors) -- simpler implementations suitable for adaptation. 5. TNC (Temporal Neighborhood Coding, Tonekaboni et al. 2021): sanatonek/time_series_self_supervision on GitHub -- contrastive temporal pretraining validated on medical time series (ECG, EEG) at n<200, closest domain analog to lottery draw sequences in terms of sample size. 6. SelfTime (Haoyi et al. 2021, GitHub: haoyfan/SelfTime) -- contrastive pretraining on time series with inter-sample and intra-temporal tasks, validated on UCR datasets including small-n cases.

- **Notable Use Cases:**
  > 1. Medical time series (ECG, EEG, ICU vitals): TNC and TS2Vec have been validated on n=100-500 patient records for downstream arrhythmia classification and sepsis prediction -- the closest published analogs in terms of labeled sample size and temporal sequence structure. 2. Industrial sensor fault detection: TS2Vec encoder pretrained on unlabeled sensor streams (millions of timesteps) then fine-tuned with 50-200 labeled fault examples -- structurally similar to the lottery pretraining (large unlabeled corpus available via synthetic augmentation, small labeled corpus from real draws). 3. Financial returns prediction: contrastive pretraining on stock return sequences (n=250 trading days per stock, ~100 stocks) has been applied to learn cross-stock representations, with downstream sector classification fine-tuning on 50-100 labeled examples -- directly analogous to the 3-lottery cross-lottery contrastive pretraining structure. 4. Human activity recognition from accelerometer: COCOA (Zhang et al. 2022) applies contrastive pretraining across body-sensor channels in a multi-view setup analogous to the multi-lottery contrastive setting; validated at n=50-150 labeled examples per activity class. 5. Recommendation systems (discrete item sequences): contrastive pretraining on item interaction sequences (structurally similar to draw history sequences -- discrete, sequential, combinatorial) has been used in SASRec and CL4Rec frameworks to improve cold-start recommendation with few labeled interactions -- the combinatorial and sparse-signal parallels to lottery prediction are the strongest in the literature.

### Implicit Q-Learning with Quantile Regression (IQL + QR-DQN) {#implicit-q-learning-with-quantile-regression-iql-qr-dqn}

**Type:** RL Algorithm | **Relevance:** 4/5 | **Integration:** Phase 2 (Actor-Critic play generation)

**General**

- **Year:** 2021
- **Key Paper:**
  > Kostrikov et al., 'Offline Reinforcement Learning with Implicit Q-Learning', ICLR 2022 (arXiv 2110.06169, 2021). Foundational distributional critic: Dabney et al., 'Distributional Reinforcement Learning with Quantile Regression (QR-DQN)', AAAI 2018. Also relevant: Dabney et al., 'Implicit Quantile Networks for Distributional Reinforcement Learning (IQN)', ICML 2018.

- **Algorithm Description:**
  > Implicit Q-Learning (IQL) is an offline RL algorithm that avoids querying Q-values for out-of-distribution actions by using expectile regression to estimate the upper expectile of the value distribution over in-dataset actions, and then trains the policy via advantage-weighted regression (AWR) using only those in-dataset actions. Quantile Regression DQN (QR-DQN) replaces the standard scalar Q-value with a full distribution over returns modeled as a fixed-size mixture of Diracs at learned quantile locations, trained with the quantile Huber loss (pinball loss). Combining the two yields a distributional IQL critic: instead of estimating E[Q(s,a)], it estimates the full return distribution P(Q(s,a)), which is critical when rewards are highly skewed (95% zeros, rare large positive returns). The policy is then extracted via advantage-weighted regression on quantile-aggregated values (e.g., CVaR or the mean), or risk-sensitively by optimizing the upper tail of the distribution to prioritize rare 3+ hit plays.

- **Action Space Handling:**
  > IQL with a distributional critic operates on a fixed dataset of (state, action, reward, next_state) tuples. For lottery, the state is the sequence of recent draws encoded by the SharedLotteryTransformer; the action is a multi-hot vector of k selected numbers from {1..N}. The Q-function takes (state, action) as a joint input and outputs Z quantiles of the return distribution rather than a scalar. The action space is combinatorially large (~C(50,7) ~ 99M for LottoMax), but IQL never needs to maximize over the action space — it only evaluates Q(s,a) for actions already in the dataset, which sidesteps the intractability. AWR policy extraction re-weights dataset actions by their advantage without requiring enumeration of unseen actions.

- **How Combinatorial Output Is Formed:**
  > IQL+QR uses dataset actions (historical draws) during training — the combinatorial constraint is enforced by the dataset itself, since all logged draws are valid k-distinct sets. At inference, the AWR-trained policy network produces logit scores over {1..N}, and top-k sampling (or Gumbel-top-k) selects exactly k distinct numbers. The quantile critic scores each candidate set; the actor is trained to up-weight sets with positive advantage (estimated from the quantile distribution). No pointer networks or flow-based sampling are required, though Gumbel-top-k can be used to make top-k differentiable during actor fine-tuning.

- **Sequential Generation Strategy:**
  > Flat joint multi-hot generation is the natural and default approach: the actor outputs a distribution over {1..N} and top-k sampling selects k numbers simultaneously without conditioning. This does not model inter-number correlations (e.g., avoiding runs or clustered picks). Autoregressive generation (one ball at a time, each conditioned on previous selections with masking) is compatible with IQL+QR but requires the critic to evaluate partial sequences or the full set at once, adding architectural complexity. For the lottery-nn project, flat multi-hot is recommended first; autoregressive can be layered in Phase 3 once the distributional critic is validated.

- **Gradient Estimator Type:**
  > IQL avoids differentiating through the Q-function for policy extraction — advantage-weighted regression (AWR) uses REINFORCE-style weighting: the policy gradient is a reweighted supervised cross-entropy loss on dataset actions, where the weight is exp(beta * A(s,a)) clipped at a maximum value. This is a score-function / importance-weighting estimator, not a reparameterization trick. The quantile critic itself is trained with the quantile Huber (pinball) loss, which is fully differentiable with respect to the quantile value predictions. If Gumbel-top-k is used at inference for differentiable sampling, a straight-through or Gumbel-softmax estimator handles the discrete selection step.

- **Combinatorial Constraint Enforcement:**
  > During training, IQL+QR uses only historical draws as actions, so k-distinct constraint is automatically satisfied by the dataset. During inference (policy rollout), two approaches enforce the constraint: (1) top-k argmax on the actor's output probability vector guarantees exactly k distinct numbers (non-differentiable, used at test time); (2) Gumbel-top-k sampling (Kool et al. 2019) provides a differentiable approximation to top-k selection for any additional fine-tuning. The quantile critic evaluates the full set as one action vector, so it does not need to reason about individual number validity — the actor handles constraint enforcement before passing the action to the critic for scoring.

- **Sample Efficiency Rating:**
  > medium — IQL is the most sample-efficient variant of offline RL at small n because it never queries OOD actions (avoiding the Q-overestimation spiral that plagues CQL at small n). Adding a quantile regression critic increases the parameter count and the number of quantiles that must be learned (typically Z=200 quantiles in QR-DQN), which requires more data to fit reliably. At n=100-200 lottery draws, the distributional critic will fit only approximately. However, the key benefit is qualitative: even with noisy quantile estimates, the ability to distinguish between 'almost always zero return' and 'occasionally high return' plays is useful and not available from a scalar Q-function trained on the same data. Sample efficiency is rated medium rather than high because distributional learning genuinely needs more samples than scalar learning to be reliable.

- **Regularization Built In:**
  > IQL: expectile regression implicitly prevents Q-value overestimation by fitting only the in-dataset action values, never extrapolating to unseen actions. The AWR advantage weight clipping (exp(beta * A) capped at exp_max) prevents degenerate policy updates. Quantile regression: the pinball/quantile Huber loss is a robust loss function that is less sensitive to outliers than MSE, providing implicit regularization against rare high-reward draws dominating the Q-estimate. Additional regularization that should be added for small n: (1) L2 weight decay on both actor and critic; (2) dropout on critic quantile network; (3) target network smoothing (polyak averaging) for stable Bellman targets.

- **Known Failure Modes On Small Data:**
  > 1. Quantile collapse: with very few non-zero reward samples (e.g., fewer than 20 plays with any hit), all Z quantiles may converge to near-zero, making the distribution degenerate and the advantage signal uninformative. 2. AWR weight explosion: if even one play in the small dataset has an unusually high reward (e.g., a 4-hit match), exp(beta * A) for that play can dominate and collapse the policy to imitating only that single historical draw. Mitigation: clip advantage weights aggressively (exp_max = 100 or lower). 3. Quantile crossing: standard QR-DQN does not enforce quantile monotonicity; with small data the learned quantiles may cross (tau_i > tau_j for i < j), invalidating the distributional interpretation. Use IQN or a monotone constraint. 4. Critic overfitting: a separate quantile network trained on 100-200 (s,a,r,s') tuples will memorize training rewards rather than generalize — validation set is tiny. 5. Near-zero variance underestimation: the distribution may be estimated as near-point-mass at zero with insufficient tail probability for rare hit events, because those events appear so infrequently in the training data.

- **Reward Distribution Handling:**
  > This is the primary motivation for combining IQL with quantile regression. The lottery reward distribution is severely right-skewed: P(R=0) ~ 0.95, P(R=small positive) ~ 0.04 (1-2 hits), P(R=large positive) ~ 0.01 (3+ hits). A scalar Q-function estimates E[R], which is dominated by the zero-reward mass and provides almost no gradient signal for improving play quality. A distributional critic models the full distribution P(R | s, a) via Z learned quantiles, enabling: (1) risk-sensitive play generation by optimizing CVaR (conditional value at risk) — select plays that maximize the expected reward given we are in the top 5% of outcomes; (2) uncertainty-aware AWR weighting — plays with high upper-tail quantile values receive higher advantage weights even if their mean return is low; (3) better bootstrapping for Bellman updates because the distributional Bellman operator propagates tail structure, not just mean return. Minimum draws for stable distributional learning: approximately 200-500 with sparse reward, though semi-bandit credit assignment (counting per-number hits rather than play-level reward) can reduce this requirement by a factor of k=7.

- **Credit Assignment Granularity:**
  > By default, IQL+QR assigns reward at the play level (single scalar R per draw). For lottery-nn with n < 200 draws, this wastes significant signal. The recommended enhancement is semi-bandit credit assignment: for each play of k numbers, compute per-number rewards r_i = 1 if number i appeared in the actual draw, else 0, and aggregate as R = sum(r_i) / k (normalized hit rate). This multiplies the effective non-zero reward events by k=7, increasing the number of informative Q-update steps by 7x. The quantile critic can model the distribution of this normalized hit rate, which has more non-zero mass than binary win/loss and yields better-estimated quantiles at small n. Alternatively, a factored distributional critic with per-ball value heads (one quantile distribution per ball slot) enables full number-level credit assignment, at the cost of a more complex architecture.

- **Diversity Mechanism:**
  > IQL+QR does not have a built-in diversity mechanism at inference time. The AWR policy converges toward the best historical actions in the dataset, which in the lottery context means imitating the number patterns of the highest-scoring historical draws — potentially collapsing to similar picks every time. Diversity can be added via: (1) entropy bonus on the actor's output distribution (SAC-style), weighted against the AWR loss; (2) temperature scaling on the actor's logits before top-k sampling (higher temperature = more exploration); (3) risk-sensitive quantile targeting — generate play i by optimizing quantile tau_i where tau_1=0.90, tau_2=0.95, etc., producing plays with different risk profiles; (4) explicit repulsion reward: add a penalty proportional to overlap between generated plays when producing a batch of N_plays per prediction call. Option (3) is most elegant given the distributional framework already in place.

- **Offline Vs Online Rl Compatibility:**
  > Fully offline — IQL was specifically designed for offline RL with no environment interaction. The quantile regression critic extends this to the distributional setting without requiring any online queries. This is the operationally essential property for lottery-nn: with one draw per week, online RL requiring thousands of environment steps is completely impractical. The historical draw CSVs (draws.csv, draws_649.csv, draws_dailygrand.csv) serve as the fixed offline dataset. After each weekly draw, the dataset grows by one sample and the distributional critic can be fine-tuned. The AWR policy extraction step also operates entirely offline. No simulator, environment reset, or online interaction is required at any stage.

- **Complexity:**
  > high — Implementing a quantile regression critic on top of IQL requires: (1) a distributional critic network outputting Z quantile values; (2) quantile Huber loss implementation; (3) distributional Bellman operator for bootstrapping; (4) advantage computation from quantile estimates (e.g., mean or CVaR); (5) AWR reweighting of the actor loss. Each component is well-understood individually but their combination, adapted to the combinatorial lottery action space, requires careful engineering. The quantile crossing problem and advantage weight clipping add additional hyperparameter sensitivity. Estimated difficulty is higher than plain IQL (medium) due to the distributional critic layer.

- **Key Dependencies:**
  > PyTorch only for core implementation. Optional but recommended: `d3rlpy` (takuseno/d3rlpy on PyPI) provides QR-DQN and IQL implementations that can be adapted; `tianshou` also has distributional RL (C51, QR-DQN) and offline RL components. No mandatory external dependencies beyond what lottery-nn already uses (PyTorch, numpy, pandas).

- **Estimated Lines Of Code:**
  > Quantile critic network: ~80-100 lines. Quantile Huber (pinball) loss: ~20-30 lines. Distributional Bellman update: ~40-60 lines. Advantage computation (mean or CVaR from quantiles): ~20-30 lines. AWR actor update: ~50-80 lines. Integration glue (dataset construction, training loop extension): ~100-150 lines. Total new code: approximately 350-450 lines excluding boilerplate and tests.

- **Transfer Learning Compatibility:**
  > High — IQL separates actor and critic clearly. The existing SharedLotteryTransformer (models/best_swarm.pt, ~253K params) serves as the actor backbone, initialized from the pretrained checkpoint and fine-tuned via AWR. Only the distributional critic (quantile network) is trained from scratch. The critic takes as input the transformer's sequence embedding (the CLS token or mean-pooled encoder output) concatenated with the action multi-hot vector, and outputs Z quantile values. This preserves all existing representation learning in the shared backbone and requires only a relatively small new critic network (~50K-100K params for Z=50-200 quantiles). The per-lottery heads in the swarm architecture can optionally have separate critic heads (one per lottery) or a shared critic with a lottery_id embedding, mirroring the actor architecture.

- **Relevance Score:** 4
- **Specific Application In Project:**
  > IQL+QR would augment the existing Actor-Critic framework in Phase 2 by replacing the scalar critic with a distributional quantile critic. Concretely: (1) the SharedLotteryTransformer encoder (shared across all 3 lotteries) acts as the actor, unchanged; (2) a new quantile critic network is trained from scratch on the historical draw dataset formatted as (draw_sequence_embedding, play_multi_hot, hit_reward, next_draw_sequence_embedding) tuples; (3) the critic learns Z=50 quantile values of the return distribution for each (state, play) pair; (4) advantage estimates are computed as A(s,a) = CVaR_90(Z(s,a)) - V(s) to emphasize the upper tail (rare 3+ hit plays); (5) the actor is fine-tuned via AWR using these advantage estimates; (6) at inference, the actor generates 5 plays per lottery with diversity enforced via quantile-targeted sampling (play i targets tau_i = 0.80 + 0.04*i). This replaces the current temperature-softmax sampling in src/predict.py and the planned scalar critic from Phase 2, adding distributional awareness without any online environment interaction. Priority: implement plain IQL first (bandit.py already done), then add quantile regression to the critic as a Phase 2b upgrade once the scalar critic is validated.

- **Integration Point:** Phase 2 (Actor-Critic play generation)
- **Replaces Or Extends:**
  > Extends — adds a distributional quantile critic network alongside the existing SharedLotteryTransformer Actor. Replaces any planned scalar critic with a quantile regression critic. The temperature-softmax sampling in src/predict.py is replaced by AWR-guided policy sampling at inference time. The existing bandit.py (Thompson sampling for agent weights) is complementary and continues to operate at the swarm level.

- **Existing Open Source Implementations:**
  > 1. ikostrikov/implicit_q_learning (GitHub) — official IQL reference implementation in JAX/Flax by the paper author; PyTorch port available in several forks. 2. takuseno/d3rlpy (GitHub, PyPI) — production Python library with IQL and QR-DQN implementations in PyTorch; actively maintained as of 2025. 3. tinkoff-ai/CORL (GitHub) — clean single-file IQL PyTorch implementation (~300 lines), easy to adapt. 4. google-deepmind/acme — JAX-based distributed RL library with IQL, QR-DQN, IQN. 5. QR-DQN reference: p-christ/Deep-Reinforcement-Learning-Algorithms-with-PyTorch (GitHub). 6. IQN (Implicit Quantile Networks, a continuous-tau generalization of QR-DQN): official implementation at google-deepmind/dqn_zoo. The CORL IQL and d3rlpy QR-DQN are the most practical starting points for lottery-nn integration.

- **Notable Use Cases:**
  > 1. Offline RL for medical treatment optimization (sepsis, ICU) — fixed dataset, sparse binary reward (survival), no online interaction — the closest structural analog to lottery: fixed historical data, cannot run new experiments, reward is rare and binary. 2. Financial portfolio optimization with distributional risk measures — sparse non-zero returns, heavy-tailed reward distribution, need to model CVaR for risk-sensitive allocation; structurally similar to lottery's skewed reward. 3. Recommender systems offline training — combinatorial item selection (select k items from N candidates), sparse click reward, large logged datasets; direct structural analog to lottery's action space. 4. Robotics offline RL (D4RL benchmark) — QR-DQN + IQL combination explored in follow-up work on risk-sensitive robot control. 5. Game playing with rare event rewards — Montezuma's Revenge (Atari) has sparse rewards structurally similar to lottery; distributional RL methods show measurable improvement over scalar Q-learning in this regime.

- benchmark_results

### Offline RL and Conservative Q-Learning (CQL / IQL / TD3+BC) {#offline-rl-and-conservative-q-learning-cql-iql-td3bc}

**Type:** RL Algorithm | **Relevance:** 4/5 | **Integration:** Phase 2 (Actor-Critic play generation)

**General**

- **Year:** 2020
- **Key Paper:**
  > Kumar et al., 'Conservative Q-Learning for Offline Reinforcement Learning', NeurIPS 2020. Also: Kostrikov et al., 'Offline Reinforcement Learning with Implicit Q-Learning (IQL)', ICLR 2022. Fujimoto & Gu, 'A Minimalist Approach to Offline Reinforcement Learning (TD3+BC)', NeurIPS 2021.

- **Algorithm Description:**
  > Offline RL trains a policy entirely from a fixed, pre-collected dataset without any online environment interaction. Conservative Q-Learning (CQL) adds a regularization penalty to the standard Bellman update that minimizes Q-values for out-of-distribution (OOD) actions and maximizes Q-values for actions in the dataset, preventing overestimation of unseen action combinations. IQL avoids querying Q-values for unseen actions altogether by using expectile regression on in-dataset action values only. TD3+BC adds a behavior-cloning regularization term directly to the policy gradient, penalizing deviation from the logged behavior policy. All three variants can be applied to a fixed historical dataset without needing to interact with the environment, making them uniquely suited to settings where data collection is constrained (e.g., one draw per week in lottery).

- **Action Space Handling:**
  > Standard CQL/IQL/TD3+BC are designed for continuous action spaces, but discrete-action CQL exists and is well-documented. For the lottery combinatorial case (select k from N), the action space is exponentially large (~C(50,7) ~ 99M for LottoMax). In practice, the Q-function must either (a) output a scalar Q-value for a given (state, action) pair where action is a multi-hot vector, or (b) be factored autoregressively across ball positions. Option (a) is simpler to implement given the existing transformer architecture. TD3+BC is most straightforwardly adapted because it treats the actor as a constrained imitation of the behavior policy.

- **How Combinatorial Output Is Formed:**
  > Within offline RL the actor (policy network) generates the combinatorial output; the Q-function (critic) scores it. The actor can produce a probability distribution over {1..N} and then apply top-k sampling, Gumbel-top-k, or autoregressive masking to form an exactly-k-distinct set. CQL/IQL score this set as a whole via Q(s, a). Alternatively, the actor can generate a multi-hot logit vector and the critic can score the softmax-normalized version. During training the conservative penalty is applied at the set level. No pointer-network or flow-based sampling is built into the offline RL framework; those are choices for the actor architecture operating inside the offline RL training loop.

- **Sequential Generation Strategy:**
  > Offline RL imposes no specific generation strategy — it wraps any actor architecture. The actor inside CQL/IQL/TD3+BC can be flat multi-hot (jointly sampled k numbers from a multi-label softmax), autoregressive (one number at a time conditioned on previous choices), or flow-based. For lottery-nn, the most natural fit is to reuse the existing SharedLotteryTransformer as the actor and add a separate critic network. Joint flat multi-hot output is the easiest path; autoregressive would add the ability to model inter-number correlations but requires significant additional architecture work.

- **Gradient Estimator Type:**
  > In offline RL, the actor is typically trained by maximizing Q(s, pi(s)) with a straight backpropagation through the actor (if the action is continuous or differentiably sampled). For discrete/combinatorial actions, a Gumbel-softmax / straight-through estimator is needed to differentiate through the argmax or top-k operation. TD3+BC avoids this entirely by adding a BC term that directly imitates logged actions (supervised loss), which is fully differentiable. IQL avoids actor gradients through Q entirely — it trains the actor with advantage-weighted regression (AWR), which uses REINFORCE-style weighting with dataset actions, avoiding the need to sample new actions at all.

- **Combinatorial Constraint Enforcement:**
  > The offline RL framework does not itself enforce combinatorial constraints — that is delegated to the actor. During policy improvement, CQL maximizes Q(s, a) over the action space which for exponentially large discrete sets requires either (a) enumerating a random subset of candidates and taking the argmax (approximate), (b) using Gumbel-top-k sampling to select exactly k balls differentiably, or (c) autoregressive masking where each step conditions on previously selected numbers to prevent repetition. IQL with advantage-weighted regression (AWR) avoids this problem by only using dataset actions — the constraint is enforced at data collection time. For lottery-nn, IQL+AWR is the safest path since the dataset actions (historical draws) are already valid k-distinct sets.

- **Sample Efficiency Rating:**
  > medium — Offline RL methods were designed for large offline datasets (thousands to millions of transitions in robotics, Atari, etc.). With fewer than 200 draws, the conservative Q-value penalty in CQL needs careful calibration: too strong and the critic learns to assign low Q-values everywhere; too weak and Q-value overestimation returns. IQL is more forgiving because it never queries OOD actions during training. TD3+BC is the most sample-efficient of the three because the BC term provides a strong supervised signal even with minimal data. Sample efficiency is medium rather than high because Q-function learning with sparse rewards (most lottery plays score 0) is inherently data-hungry.

- **Regularization Built In:**
  > CQL: explicit conservative penalty (subtracted softmax Q term over the action space); optional entropy term for the policy. IQL: expectile regression implicitly prevents Q overestimation; advantage-weighted regression clips large advantages via temperature parameter beta. TD3+BC: behavior cloning term lambda * BC_loss added to policy gradient, normalized by mean absolute Q-value to balance scales. All three benefit from standard weight decay and dropout applied to actor/critic networks. For small datasets, the BC component in TD3+BC and the advantage clipping in IQL provide the most meaningful regularization.

- **Known Failure Modes On Small Data:**
  > 1. Q-value collapse: with fewer than ~50 non-zero reward samples, the critic may converge to near-zero Q-values everywhere, making policy gradient steps uninformative. 2. Distribution mismatch amplification: CQL penalizes OOD actions which with very few data points means nearly all reasonable plays are penalized. 3. Sparse reward propagation failure: if fewer than ~10 plays in the dataset have any hit (reward > 0), temporal difference updates cannot reliably propagate signal. 4. Overfitting critic: a separate critic network trained on 100-200 samples will overfit its Q-estimates even with regularization. 5. TD3+BC degenerates to pure BC: if the Q-signal is too noisy, the BC term dominates and the method collapses to imitation learning with no RL improvement.

- **Reward Distribution Handling:**
  > All three methods handle sparse reward via temporal difference propagation — Q-values for states preceding successful plays should accumulate positive signal. However, with lottery's ~95% zero-reward plays, the Q-function is effectively learning from a nearly-all-zeros reward signal over 100-200 samples. Distributional extensions (C51, QR-DQN) can be added to any of these methods to model the full return distribution rather than just the mean, which is beneficial for risk-sensitive play generation. Minimum draws needed for stable Q-learning: estimated 500+ for reliable signal propagation, though IQL+AWR may work with ~200 if the reward is semi-bandit (per-number hits counted rather than play-level only). The semi-bandit formulation (credit assignment at number level) is critical to making offline RL viable at n<200.

- **Credit Assignment Granularity:**
  > By default, offline RL assigns reward at the play level (did the set of k numbers match?). This is the coarsest possible assignment and wastes the signal from partial matches. For lottery-nn, the reward should be redesigned as number-level semi-bandit feedback: reward_i = 1 if number i in the play appeared in the actual draw, else 0. This gives k signals per play instead of 1, effectively multiplying the usable reward signal by k=7. IQL with AWR can incorporate this naturally by treating each number selection as a separate action with its own advantage estimate. CQL requires a factored Q-function to support this. TD3+BC can use a per-number loss in the BC term weighted by per-number reward.

- **Diversity Mechanism:**
  > None of the three methods have built-in diversity mechanisms for play generation at inference time. Options: (1) temperature scaling on the actor's output logits before top-k sampling; (2) entropy bonus in the policy objective (SAC-style), though adding this to CQL requires care to avoid destabilizing the conservative penalty; (3) IQL with AWR naturally introduces some diversity through the advantage-weighted sampling — actions with positive advantage are preferred but not deterministically selected. For multiple plays per prediction call (lottery-nn generates 5 plays per call), an explicit diversity reward or a repulsion mechanism between generated plays must be added separately.

- **Offline Vs Online Rl Compatibility:**
  > Fully offline — this is the defining property of the CQL/IQL/TD3+BC family and the primary reason they are relevant here. All three methods train exclusively from a fixed historical dataset with no environment interaction required. This is the correct RL paradigm for lottery: with one draw per week, any online RL method that requires thousands of environment steps is operationally impractical. The dataset is simply the historical draw CSV (draws.csv, draws_649.csv, draws_dailygrand.csv). After each new weekly draw, the dataset is augmented by one sample and the model can be retrained offline. This aligns perfectly with the existing `main_swarm.py joint-train` workflow.

- **Complexity:**
  > medium — IQL and TD3+BC are simpler to implement than CQL. Adding a critic network (separate from the actor) and a modified training loop to existing lottery-nn is moderately complex. The main challenge is adapting the combinatorial action space.

- **Key Dependencies:**
  > PyTorch only (all three methods are implementable in pure PyTorch). Optional: `d3rlpy` library (Python, PyPI) provides production-quality implementations of CQL, IQL, and TD3+BC with minimal glue code. `tianshou` also has offline RL support. No additional heavy dependencies beyond what lottery-nn already uses.

- **Estimated Lines Of Code:**
  > IQL: ~200-300 lines (critic network + expectile loss + AWR policy update). TD3+BC: ~150-200 lines (add BC term to existing actor training). CQL: ~250-350 lines (critic + conservative penalty). Shared scaffolding (replay buffer, offline training loop): ~100 lines common to all.

- **Transfer Learning Compatibility:**
  > High — all three methods separate the actor (policy network) from the critic (value network). The existing SharedLotteryTransformer can serve directly as the actor; only a new critic network needs to be added from scratch. The actor weights are initialized from the pretrained swarm checkpoint (models/best_swarm.pt) and fine-tuned via the offline RL objective. This is one of the strongest arguments for offline RL in this project: the existing 253K-parameter pretrained backbone is not wasted, unlike methods that require training from scratch.

- **Relevance Score:** 4
- **Specific Application In Project:**
  > Offline RL (specifically IQL or TD3+BC) would replace the current temperature-softmax sampling in `src/predict.py` with a principled policy optimization loop. The actor remains the SharedLotteryTransformer; a new critic MLP is added (input: state embedding from transformer, output: scalar Q-value). Training uses the historical draws CSV as a static offline dataset. Reward is semi-bandit (per-number hit count) to maximize credit assignment at n<200. After each weekly draw, the dataset grows by one sample and `joint-train` is extended to also run the offline RL update. IQL is recommended first (simpler, no OOD action queries, most robust at small n); TD3+BC is the fallback if IQL's Q-signal is too noisy. CQL is a Phase 3 candidate once more data accumulates. Integration target: all three lotteries via the swarm's per-lottery heads, with a shared critic backbone analogous to the shared actor backbone.

- **Integration Point:** Phase 2 (Actor-Critic play generation)
- **Replaces Or Extends:**
  > Extends — adds a Critic network and offline RL training loop alongside the existing SharedLotteryTransformer Actor. The temperature-softmax sampling at inference time is replaced by policy network sampling guided by the learned Q-function. The existing `joint-train` command is extended with an offline RL update step.

- **Existing Open Source Implementations:**
  > 1. `d3rlpy` (GitHub: takuseno/d3rlpy) — production-quality Python library with CQL, IQL, TD3+BC, SAC, BCQ, BEAR; integrates with PyTorch; actively maintained as of 2025. 2. `CORL` (GitHub: tinkoff-ai/CORL) — clean single-file offline RL implementations of IQL, TD3+BC, CQL, DT in PyTorch. 3. Official CQL implementation: GitHub stevenygd/CQL (older). 4. `tianshou` offline RL module. 5. IQL reference implementation: GitHub ikostrikov/implicit_q_learning. 6. TD3+BC reference: GitHub sfujim/TD3_BC. D3rlpy and CORL are the most practical for integration.

- **Benchmark Results:**
  > CQL (D4RL benchmark, NeurIPS 2020): achieves 44.0 normalized score on hopper-medium vs 29.0 for BC baseline; 72.5 on walker2d-medium vs 75.3 BC — competitive with or better than BC on most tasks. IQL (ICLR 2022): 47.4 on hopper-medium-v2 vs 52.5 CQL; achieves state-of-art on antmaze tasks (antmaze-large-diverse: 58.6 vs CQL 12.5). TD3+BC (NeurIPS 2021): 59.3 on hopper-medium vs 42.6 BC; competitive with CQL with significantly less code. All results on datasets with >> 200K transitions — direct small-data comparison not available in published literature.

- **Notable Use Cases:**
  > 1. Offline RL for medical treatment policies (sepsis, ICU ventilation) — fixed historical patient records, sparse reward, no online interaction — structurally similar to lottery (fixed dataset, cannot run new experiments). 2. Recommendation systems trained offline from logged user interactions — combinatorial item selection, sparse click reward, large action spaces. 3. Autonomous driving policy learning from demonstration datasets (CARLA, nuScenes). 4. Chip placement (Google, Nature 2021) uses a related offline RL approach on fixed simulation data. The medical and recommendation-system use cases are the most structurally analogous to lottery: fixed dataset, sparse reward, combinatorial action space.


**Uncertain Fields**

- data_regime_evidence
