### Pointer Networks and Set Transformers {#pointer-networks-and-set-transformers}

**Type:** Architecture | **Relevance:** 4/5 | **Integration:** Phase 2 (Actor-Critic play generation)

**General**

- **Year:** 2015
- **Key Paper:**
  > Vinyals, O., Fortunato, M., Jaitly, N. (2015). Pointer Networks. NeurIPS 2015. | Lee, J., Lee, Y., Kim, J., Kosiorek, A., Choi, S., Teh, Y.W. (2019). Set Transformer: A Framework for Attention-based Permutation-Invariant Neural Networks. ICML 2019.

- **Algorithm Description:**
  > Pointer Networks (PtrNet) are a seq2seq architecture where the decoder at each step uses an attention mechanism to 'point' to one element of the input sequence, producing an output sequence drawn directly from the input positions rather than a fixed vocabulary. The pointer head computes scores e_i = v^T tanh(W1 * enc_i + W2 * dec_t) over all input positions and samples or argmaxes from softmax(e), naturally masking already-selected positions to enforce distinctness. Set Transformers extend the Transformer architecture to operate on sets: they use Induced Set Attention Blocks (ISAB) with m learnable inducing points to reduce per-element attention cost from O(n^2) to O(mn), and Pooling by Multihead Attention (PMA) to aggregate a set into a fixed-size representation. Together, these two architectures address the 'select k from N' combinatorial output problem from complementary directions: PtrNet from the autoregressive decoding angle, Set Transformer from the permutation-invariant encoding angle. In the lottery context PtrNet can decode a play of 7 numbers one at a time from the pool of 50 candidates; Set Transformer can encode the history of draws as an unordered set and produce a context vector for the policy head.

- **Action Space Handling:**
  > Pointer Networks handle the action space autoregressively: at each decoding step t (for t=1..k), the pointer head attends over all N input candidates and produces a probability distribution over them. Already-selected indices are masked (set to -inf before softmax) so sampling produces k distinct selections without additional post-processing. This implicitly factors the joint distribution as pi(a_1, ..., a_k) = prod_{t=1}^{k} pi(a_t | a_1, ..., a_{t-1}, input). Set Transformers do not directly define an action space sampling scheme; instead they produce permutation-invariant encoded representations of sets that can be consumed by any downstream policy head. When combined, the Set Transformer encodes the draw history into a context and the Pointer Network decodes a play from the candidate pool conditioned on that context, giving a structured autoregressive factorization of the combinatorial action space.

- **How Combinatorial Output Is Formed:**
  > In Pointer Networks the combinatorial output is formed by autoregressive pointer decoding: (1) encode the N candidate numbers (1..50) using an encoder (LSTM or Transformer); (2) at each step t, compute attention scores over all N encoder states conditioned on the current decoder state; (3) apply a masking vector m_t that zeros already-selected indices; (4) sample or argmax from the masked softmax to select the t-th number; (5) repeat for k steps, yielding exactly k distinct numbers from {1..N} with no post-hoc rejection needed. The constraint is enforced by construction via cumulative masking. In Set Transformers, the output set is not autoregressively decoded -- instead PMA (Pooling by Multihead Attention) aggregates the encoded set into k seed vectors that can be projected to k selections, but the distinctness constraint is not enforced natively and requires an additional sampling or top-k step. For lottery play generation, PtrNet is the more direct tool for combinatorial output formation.

- **Sequential Generation Strategy:**
  > Pointer Networks use fully autoregressive generation: each of the k numbers is generated one at a time, conditioned on all previously selected numbers via the accumulated decoder hidden state and masking. This captures inter-number correlations explicitly -- the network can learn that if 7 was selected, the probability of 14 increases (or decreases). The cost is O(k) forward passes through the decoder per play. Set Transformers use non-autoregressive (joint) encoding -- all elements of the input set are processed simultaneously with no ordering assumption. The PMA decoder can produce k output representations in a single pass, but they are not sequentially conditioned on each other, so inter-element correlations in the output set are modeled only implicitly through the attention mechanism. A hybrid architecture -- Set Transformer encoder feeding a Pointer Network decoder -- gives the best of both: permutation-invariant history encoding combined with auto-regressive distinctness-enforcing play generation.

- **Gradient Estimator Type:**
  > Pointer Networks were originally trained with REINFORCE (score-function estimator) in the original 2015 paper, treating the pointer selection at each step as a stochastic action. Subsequent work (Bello et al. 2016, Kool et al. 2019 Attention Model) trained them with REINFORCE + greedy rollout baseline, achieving substantially lower variance. When trained with teacher forcing (supervised imitation of ground-truth sequences), cross-entropy loss applies directly and no discrete gradient estimator is needed. For the RL use case in lottery-nn, the k pointer decisions form a chain of discrete actions; the joint log-probability is log pi(play) = sum_{t=1}^{k} log pi(a_t | prev), and REINFORCE is applied on this sum. Set Transformers are trained purely with continuous gradients (cross-entropy or MSE on set-level predictions) since their outputs are continuous representations -- no discrete sampling or gradient estimator is needed at the Set Transformer encoding stage.

- **Combinatorial Constraint Enforcement:**
  > Pointer Networks enforce the combinatorial constraint (exactly k distinct numbers from {1..N}) by autoregressive masking: at step t, any index already selected in steps 1..t-1 is masked to -inf before the softmax, making its selection probability exactly zero. This is an exact, differentiable-compatible enforcement requiring no rejection sampling and no post-hoc correction. The constraint is always satisfied by construction for any stochastic or greedy decoding strategy. Set Transformers do not natively enforce a k-subset constraint on their outputs; if PMA is used to produce k output vectors, they must be discretized with an additional top-k or Gumbel-top-k operation, which introduces the usual distinctness-vs-softmax tension. For hard constraint enforcement in the lottery context, the Pointer Network autoregressive masking approach is superior to anything achievable with a vanilla Set Transformer output head.

- **Regularization Built In:**
  > Pointer Networks have no built-in regularization beyond the structural constraint imposed by the attention mechanism. Standard regularization additions: dropout on encoder/decoder hidden states (common in practice), weight decay on pointer head parameters, entropy bonus on pointer attention distributions to prevent early collapse to deterministic selections. Set Transformers have layer normalization built into every ISAB and PMA block (inherited from Transformer architecture), which provides implicit regularization against feature scale explosion. The inducing-point bottleneck in ISAB (m << n) acts as an information bottleneck that regularizes the representation. Both architectures benefit from the standard Transformer regularization toolkit: dropout, weight decay, gradient clipping. No method-specific regularization is inherent beyond these structural properties.

- **Known Failure Modes On Small Data:**
  > 1. PtrNet encoder overfitting: with n < 200 training sequences the LSTM or Transformer encoder memorizes the training draws rather than learning generalizable patterns; validation loss rises after 10-20 epochs. 2. Pointer distribution collapse: the pointer attention can collapse to always selecting numbers in a fixed order (e.g., ascending), ignoring the input context; entropy regularization on pointer distributions prevents this. 3. Order dependence artifact: PtrNet is sensitive to the order in which the k ground-truth numbers are presented during supervised training; since lottery draws have no canonical ordering, training with multiple random permutations of each draw is required but multiplies data requirements. 4. Set Transformer inducing point starvation: with few training samples (n < 100), m inducing points in ISAB may not be meaningfully learned if m approaches n; setting m << n (e.g., m=4 for n=100) avoids this. 5. RL fine-tuning instability: RL fine-tuning of the PtrNet policy on top of supervised pretraining can catastrophically forget the imitation prior if the RL reward signal is extremely sparse (95% zero-hit plays).

- **Reward Distribution Handling:**
  > Pointer Networks with REINFORCE inherit the same sparse-reward problem as vanilla REINFORCE: ~95% zero-hit plays produce near-zero gradient signal. The key advantage of the PtrNet formulation is that the log-probability of a play decomposes as sum_{t=1}^{k} log pi(a_t | prev), so reward can be attributed at the per-pointer-step level. This enables a form of step-level credit assignment: if number a_t hits, apply higher weight to the gradient of log pi(a_t | prev) at step t. This is the semi-bandit feedback extension and is more natural in the PtrNet autoregressive framework than in flat multi-hot policies. For supervised pretraining the reward distribution is irrelevant -- the model trains on historical draw sequences as ground-truth labels. Distributional returns (QR-DQN style) are not supported natively and would require significant architectural modification. Minimum draws needed: for supervised pretraining, 50-100 draws may suffice with a pretrained backbone; for RL fine-tuning, 500+ evaluated plays are needed for stable REINFORCE convergence on sparse rewards.

- **Credit Assignment Granularity:**
  > Pointer Networks provide the finest-grained credit assignment available among architectures in this research set. Because each of the k selections is a separate stochastic decision with its own log-probability log pi(a_t | a_1..a_{t-1}), reward can be assigned differentially across the k pointer steps. If the play contains a number that hits, the gradient can be localized to the pointer step that selected that number: delta_theta propto r_t * grad_theta log pi(a_t | prev), where r_t = indicator(a_t in winning_draw). This is number-level credit assignment, the most signal-efficient possible given the semi-bandit feedback structure of lottery results. By contrast, flat multi-hot policies assign the same scalar reward to all N logits simultaneously. The PtrNet step-level credit assignment effectively converts the sparse play-level reward into denser per-step gradient signal, which is particularly valuable at n < 200.

- **Diversity Mechanism:**
  > Pointer Networks generate diverse plays naturally through stochastic sampling: at each pointer step, sampling from the softmax distribution (rather than argmax) introduces randomness, and different sampling runs yield different sequences. Temperature scaling of the pointer attention logits (z_t / T) controls diversity: T > 1 flattens the distribution for more exploration, T < 1 sharpens it for exploitation. The existing temperature parameter (T=1.2) in predict.py is directly analogous and can be applied to pointer logits without code changes. For multi-play diversity the sequential stochastic sampling of each play independently ensures some spread. Set Transformers do not have a built-in diversity mechanism for output sets. An explicit diversity reward (penalize pairwise Jaccard overlap between plays) or DPP-based sampling can be layered on top of either architecture. Entropy bonus on pointer attention distributions (beta * H(softmax(e_t))) is the most principled built-in diversity mechanism for PtrNet.

- **Offline Vs Online Rl Compatibility:**
  > Both architectures are compatible with fully offline training when used in supervised (imitation learning) mode: historical draws are used as ground-truth labels for the pointer decoder, and the model is trained via cross-entropy with no environment interaction required. This is the operationally correct mode for the lottery-nn project where draws occur weekly. For RL fine-tuning (REINFORCE on top of supervised pretraining), the same offline approximation used for flat policies applies -- historical draw outcomes serve as rewards for current-policy plays. This introduces importance-sampling bias (the plays being evaluated were generated by the current policy, not the data-collection policy) but is standard practice in offline RL fine-tuning (analogous to RLHF in language models). Set Transformers used purely as encoders are fully offline-compatible: they process the historical draw sequence and produce a context embedding without any RL or environment interaction. The supervised pretraining + offline RL fine-tuning pipeline is the recommended approach for lottery-nn, giving this architecture combination good offline compatibility.

- **Complexity:** high
- **Key Dependencies:**
  > No additional libraries required beyond PyTorch for a custom Pointer Network implementation (uses torch.nn.MultiheadAttention or manual attention). Set Transformer reference implementation (juho-lee/set_transformer on GitHub) is a standalone PyTorch module. Optional: torch_geometric for graph-based variants. No stable-baselines3, torchrl, or pyro required. The main complexity is implementation, not dependencies.

- **Estimated Lines Of Code:**
  > 300-450 lines of new code: ~150 lines for PtrNet decoder (encoder projection, pointer attention, masking loop, log-prob accumulation, sampling); ~100 lines for Set Transformer ISAB + PMA blocks if added as encoder replacement; ~80 lines for integration into main_swarm.py predict path and the RL fine-tuning loop; ~50 lines for supervised pretraining data pipeline (ordering draws as pointer targets with random permutation augmentation). Excludes boilerplate, tests, and checkpoint logic.

- **Transfer Learning Compatibility:**
  > High compatibility for the encoder side: the existing SharedLotteryTransformer already produces contextual embeddings from the draw history sequence, and its output can be used directly as the encoder states over which the Pointer Network decoder attends. Concretely, the transformer encoder outputs a sequence of hidden states h_1..h_T; the PtrNet pointer head computes attention scores over these states to select the next number. Only the pointer head (a small attention module, ~2K parameters) needs to be added on top of the frozen backbone. The shared encoder is NOT replaced -- this is a pure extension. For Set Transformer: its ISAB blocks could replace the transformer encoder blocks, but this would require retraining from scratch and is not recommended given the existing pretrained backbone. The preferred integration keeps the SharedLotteryTransformer backbone frozen and adds a lightweight pointer decoder head, making the approach maximally compatible with the existing Phase 1 investment.

- **Relevance Score:** 4
- **Specific Application In Project:**
  > Pointer Networks would be integrated as the play-generation decoder in Phase 2, replacing the current temperature-softmax top-k sampling in src/predict.py. Concretely: (1) the SharedLotteryTransformer encoder runs on the last SEQUENCE_LEN=20 draws and produces hidden states h_1..h_20; (2) a new PtrNet decoder head (pointer attention module in src/pointer_decoder.py) attends over a set of 50 candidate embeddings (one per number in the pool) conditioned on h_T (the final encoder state); (3) at each of 7 pointer steps, the head selects one number by masked softmax attention, accumulating log-probabilities for the REINFORCE gradient; (4) supervised pretraining trains the pointer decoder to reproduce historical draws in random-permutation order (all 7! orderings treated as equivalent); (5) RL fine-tuning applies per-step REINFORCE updates using indicator(selected_number in actual_draw) as the per-step reward signal; (6) applies to all three lotteries via the shared backbone with per-lottery pointer head parameters (small, ~6K params each). Set Transformer ISAB blocks are a lower-priority optional addition that could replace or augment the existing transformer encoder to better handle draws as unordered sets rather than temporal sequences.

- **Integration Point:** Phase 2 (Actor-Critic play generation)
- **Replaces Or Extends:**
  > Extends -- adds a Pointer Network decoder head (src/pointer_decoder.py) on top of the existing SharedLotteryTransformer encoder without replacing the backbone. Replaces the temperature-softmax Gumbel-top-k sampling in src/predict.py with autoregressive pointer decoding. The Set Transformer component, if added, would optionally replace the encoder blocks inside SharedLotteryTransformer (Backbone-level change), but this is not recommended in Phase 2.

- **Existing Open Source Implementations:**
  > 1. juho-lee/set_transformer (GitHub) -- official PyTorch implementation of Set Transformer with ISAB, SAB, and PMA blocks; clean and directly importable. 2. Pointer Networks: ptrblck/pt_pointernet (GitHub) -- PyTorch reimplementation of the original 2015 paper on convex hull and TSP tasks. 3. wouterkool/attention-learn-to-route (GitHub) -- Kool et al. 2019 Attention Model, a Transformer-based Pointer Network for TSP/VRP, trained with REINFORCE + greedy baseline; highest-quality PtrNet reference for combinatorial optimization. 4. google/or-tools combined with neural Pointer Networks in various academic repos. 5. Hugging Face Transformers library does not include Pointer Networks natively, but the cross-attention mechanism used in encoder-decoder models (e.g., T5) is functionally equivalent and can be repurposed. All implementations are available as of 2025 and are compatible with PyTorch 2.x.

- **Benchmark Results:**
  > Pointer Networks (Vinyals et al. 2015): on convex hull (n=50 points), 99.2% optimal; on TSP-10, 92% optimal tours with REINFORCE; on sorting (n=10), near-100% accuracy with supervised training. Kool et al. (2019) Attention Model on TSP-100: 5.24% optimality gap vs. Concorde, compared to 8.3% for REINFORCE baseline. Set Transformer (Lee et al. 2019): on maximum-likelihood clustering (k=4 Gaussians, n=200 points), ELBO 0.241 vs. 0.182 for LSTM baseline; on point cloud classification (ModelNet40), 90.3% accuracy. On amortized maximum likelihood estimation, Set Transformer outperforms DeepSets by 12-18% ELBO across all tasks tested. Neither architecture has been benchmarked on lottery number prediction or any task structurally equivalent to k-of-N combinatorial selection from a fixed pool with sparse binary reward and n < 200 training draws.

- **Notable Use Cases:**
  > 1. Traveling Salesman Problem (TSP) and Vehicle Routing (VRP): Pointer Networks are the canonical neural architecture for combinatorial route optimization -- selecting N cities in an order, which is structurally identical to selecting k numbers from a pool. 2. Sorting and convex hull: original PtrNet benchmark tasks, demonstrating k-from-N combinatorial output. 3. Drug candidate selection: molecular generative models use PtrNet-style attention to select atoms from a candidate set, closely analogous to selecting lottery numbers from {1..50}. 4. Recommendation system slate generation: selecting k items from a product catalog of N, matching the play-generation task structure exactly (Ie et al., SlateQ 2019). 5. Set Transformer for multi-instance learning: classifying bags of instances (pathology patches, point clouds) where the input is an unordered set -- analogous to treating the history of draws as an unordered set of observations. 6. Neural combinatorial optimization in operations research: scheduling, bin packing, and set cover problems all use PtrNet variants as the policy network in Actor-Critic RL pipelines.

### Self-Play and Quality-Diversity for Diverse Play Generation {#self-play-and-quality-diversity-for-diverse-play-generation}

**Type:** Technique | **Relevance:** 4/5 | **Integration:** Phase 2 (Actor-Critic play generation)

**General**

- **Year:** 2023
- **Key Paper:**
  > Felten, F., Talbi, E.-G., & Danoy, G. (2023). Multi-Objective Reinforcement Learning with Quality-Diversity. arXiv:2306.14467. Also foundational: Mouret, J.-B., & Clune, J. (2015). Illuminating search spaces by mapping elites. arXiv:1504.04909.

- **Algorithm Description:**
  > Quality-Diversity (QD) methods, most notably MAP-Elites, maintain a structured archive of solutions (plays) that are both high-performing (quality) and behaviorally diverse (coverage across a user-defined feature space). In the RL formulation (QD-RL), multiple actor heads or population members are each rewarded for two objectives: (1) maximizing cumulative return (hit quality) and (2) occupying a unique behavioral cell in a discretized behavior descriptor space (e.g., number-range distribution, parity pattern, hot-cold balance). Self-play extends this by having actor heads compete or collaborate — each head is evaluated relative to the outputs of other heads, with diversity bonuses penalizing overlap between simultaneously generated plays. At each generation, new solutions are produced by mutating archive elites, evaluated for fitness and behavior descriptor, then inserted into the archive if they outperform the current occupant of their cell or fill an empty cell. The result is a large, diverse population of non-dominated solutions rather than a single best policy.

- **Action Space Handling:**
  > QD methods treat the combinatorial action space (choose k from N) via population-level diversity rather than per-step diversity. Each individual actor in the population uses a standard discrete policy (softmax over N logits, with autoregressive masking or Gumbel-top-k for constraint enforcement). Diversity is imposed at the population level: the archive structure forces different actors to occupy different regions of behavior space. Self-play variants add pairwise diversity rewards: actor i receives a penalty proportional to the set-intersection overlap with actors j != i, pushing them apart in number space. This is orthogonal to and composable with any single-actor action-space mechanism (autoregressive, flat multi-hot, pointer network).

- **How Combinatorial Output Is Formed:**
  > Each actor head in the population generates a play using the same k-of-N sampling mechanism as a standard actor — typically autoregressive masking (select number 1 from N logits, mask it, select number 2 from remaining N-1, etc.) or Gumbel-top-k joint sampling. The QD/self-play layer operates above this: (1) M actor heads run in parallel, each producing one play per forward pass; (2) a pairwise diversity matrix is computed (Jaccard distance or set intersection count between all M*M pairs); (3) each actor's reward is augmented with a diversity bonus proportional to its uniqueness score; (4) actors that collapse to the same play are penalized and their parameters are perturbed (mutation) or re-initialized. The archive (MAP-Elites grid) bins plays by behavioral descriptors (e.g., mean number value, number of odd/even balls) and retains the highest-reward play per bin.

- **Sequential Generation Strategy:**
  > QD and self-play operate at the population level and are agnostic to the within-actor generation strategy. Each individual actor can use joint (flat multi-hot), autoregressive (one number at a time with masking), or flow-based generation. For the lottery-nn project, autoregressive masking is recommended within each actor head, as it guarantees exactly k distinct numbers without post-hoc filtering. The QD diversity mechanism acts on the output plays (sets of k numbers) rather than on the generation trajectory, so it composes cleanly with any sequential strategy. Inter-number correlations can be modeled within each actor's autoregressive pass; inter-play diversity is enforced by the QD population mechanism.

- **Gradient Estimator Type:**
  > Within each actor, standard REINFORCE (score function estimator) or its variance-reduced variants (RLOO, REINFORCE with leave-one-out baseline) are used. The diversity bonus is typically non-differentiable (set intersection is discrete), so it enters the reward signal rather than the gradient directly — no additional estimator is needed if the base policy uses REINFORCE. When differentiable diversity is desired, a soft Jaccard similarity (computed via soft top-k relaxations such as NeuralSort or Gumbel-top-k) can be included in the loss, enabling straight-through or reparameterization gradients for the diversity term. In MAP-Elites specifically, gradient information is not used at all — evolution (mutation + selection) drives search, making it gradient-free. Gradient-based QD variants (DQD, CMA-MEGA) use the gradient of a surrogate fitness function to guide archive exploration.

- **Combinatorial Constraint Enforcement:**
  > Each actor enforces the k-of-N constraint independently, using the same mechanisms available to single-actor policies: (1) Autoregressive masking — most common in practice, masks already-selected numbers at each of k steps, guaranteeing distinct selection. (2) Gumbel-top-k — joint sampling without replacement in one forward pass. (3) Rejection sampling with replacement — simplest but biased. The QD/self-play layer adds no new constraint complexity — it operates on completed, constraint-satisfying plays. The archive behavioral descriptor computation (e.g., binning by mean ball value) also operates on valid plays only, so constraint enforcement is fully decoupled from diversity enforcement.

- **Sample Efficiency Rating:**
  > medium. QD methods are more sample-efficient than single-policy RL in the sense that the archive retains all good solutions found during training — no solution is discarded due to policy updates (unlike on-policy PPO). However, MAP-Elites in its classic form is evolutionary and requires many fitness evaluations to fill the archive, which is expensive when each evaluation requires a real lottery draw. In the offline setting (100-200 historical draws), QD methods can be adapted by: (1) using the historical draws as a fixed evaluator (scoring each generated play against past draws for hit count); (2) training M actor heads jointly on the fixed dataset with a diversity augmented reward. The multi-actor diversity bonus is computed cheaply from generated plays without requiring new environment interactions. Sample efficiency is medium — better than naive random search, competitive with single-actor REINFORCE on small data, but not as theoretically grounded as offline RL methods with conservative value estimation.

- **Regularization Built In:**
  > Several regularization mechanisms are inherent: (1) Archive-based selection pressure — only the best solution per behavioral cell survives, preventing the population from collapsing to a single attractor. (2) Diversity reward — the explicit penalty for inter-actor overlap acts as a regularizer against degenerate generation. (3) Entropy bonus within each actor — standard entropy regularization prevents any single actor from becoming deterministic. (4) Population-level implicit regularization — maintaining M diverse actors makes it unlikely that all M overfit identically to the small training set; the diversity pressure forces different actors to explore different regions, effectively acting as an ensemble regularizer. (5) Mutation/perturbation in evolutionary QD variants adds noise that prevents premature convergence.

- **Known Failure Modes On Small Data:**
  > Key failure modes on n < 200: (1) Archive underfilling — with sparse reward (95% zero-hit plays), very few solutions achieve non-zero fitness scores, leaving most archive cells empty and the diversity mechanism ineffective. (2) Behavioral descriptor collapse — if the descriptors are not well-chosen, many plays map to the same cell regardless of actual numerical diversity (e.g., using only mean ball value bins all moderate-range plays to the same cell). (3) Diversity without quality — QD can produce many diverse but equally poor plays; on small data, the quality signal is too weak to guide the diversity-quality tradeoff. (4) M-actor overhead on small datasets — training M=5-10 actor heads on 100-200 draws can lead to each actor overfitting its diversity niche rather than finding genuinely better predictions. (5) Self-play instability — if actor heads are trained adversarially (each trying to avoid the others' outputs), the training dynamics can become non-stationary and diverge on small data.

- **Credit Assignment Granularity:**
  > Standard QD methods assign reward at the play level (how many numbers matched the draw). Number-level (semi-bandit) feedback can be incorporated by using per-number hit indicators to define the behavioral descriptor rather than the reward — for example, a behavioral descriptor could be (number of low-range hits, number of high-range hits), turning per-number feedback into a structured diversity signal. This is a creative extension not standard in the QD literature but well-suited to the lottery domain. Within each actor, the REINFORCE gradient uses play-level reward by default; reward decomposition into per-number signals requires architectural changes (M separate binary critics, one per number) that are feasible but add complexity.

- **Diversity Mechanism:**
  > Diversity is the defining feature of QD methods and operates through multiple complementary mechanisms: (1) MAP-Elites archive — the behavior space (e.g., fraction of odd numbers, mean ball value, hot/cold ball balance) is discretized into a grid; only one solution occupies each cell, forcing the population to spread across the space. (2) Explicit diversity reward in self-play variants — each actor i receives a reward bonus proportional to the minimum pairwise set distance to all other actors: bonus_i = min_{j != i} (1 - |S_i intersect S_j| / k), where S_i and S_j are the sets of numbers in plays i and j. (3) Novelty search as surrogate fitness — in novelty-based QD, actors are rewarded purely for producing plays that differ from all previously seen plays, with quality only used as a secondary selection criterion. (4) Population replacement policy — solutions are only replaced by strictly better solutions in their cell, ensuring diversity is maintained across generations even if overall quality decreases.

- **Offline Vs Online Rl Compatibility:**
  > QD methods are partially compatible with offline training but were originally designed for online evolutionary search. Key considerations: (1) The archive quality signal requires evaluating generated plays against an environment (a draw) — in offline mode, generated plays can be scored against historical draws (semi-bandit feedback), computing the hit count for each historical draw and averaging. This is a valid offline adaptation. (2) The evolutionary mutation operators (perturbing actor network weights) do not require environment interaction, only fitness evaluation — compatible with offline historical data. (3) Self-play diversity bonuses are computed purely from generated plays (set intersection), requiring zero environment interaction — fully offline compatible. (4) Gradient-based QD (DQD, CMA-MEGA) requires gradient of fitness w.r.t. policy parameters — compatible with offline RL if a differentiable surrogate fitness (learned critic) is used. Verdict: QD diversity mechanisms are offline-compatible; quality signal requires offline evaluation against historical draws, which is feasible. More offline-friendly than PPO but less principled than conservative offline RL methods.

- **Complexity:** high
- **Key Dependencies:**
  > PyTorch (already present). Optional: qdax (https://github.com/adaptive-intelligent-robotics/QDax) — JAX-based QD library, not PyTorch-native but provides MAP-Elites reference. ribs (https://github.com/icaros-usc/pyribs) — Python library for MAP-Elites and QD algorithms, framework-agnostic, PyTorch-compatible for neural policy evaluation. No mandatory new dependencies if implementing core self-play diversity reward from scratch — only requires standard PyTorch tensor operations for set intersection computation.

- **Estimated Lines Of Code:**
  > 400-600 lines of new code: M-actor ensemble wrapper (~80 lines), pairwise set-intersection diversity reward computation (~60 lines), archive (MAP-Elites grid) data structure with behavioral descriptor binning (~120 lines), diversity-augmented training loop integrating with existing REINFORCE or bandit (~100 lines), behavioral descriptor extraction from generated plays (~60 lines), population management (archive insert/replace logic, ~80 lines), evaluation utilities comparing archive coverage metrics (~60 lines). Excludes boilerplate. Simpler self-play-only variant (no archive, just pairwise diversity reward) requires ~200 lines.

- **Transfer Learning Compatibility:**
  > High. The existing SharedLotteryTransformer encoder serves as the shared backbone for all M actor heads — each head is a small linear layer on top of the shared encoder output, initialized identically and then diversified through training. The lottery_id embedding already handles per-lottery differentiation, so the same multi-actor architecture applies to all three lotteries simultaneously. The pretrained encoder provides a strong initialization that allows actor heads to start from a meaningful representation rather than random features, significantly accelerating archive filling. Transfer is especially beneficial here: the shared encoder has already learned temporal draw patterns across all three lotteries, so diversity pressure at the actor-head level will produce plays that are both meaningfully diverse and grounded in real statistical patterns.

- **Relevance Score:** 4
- **Specific Application In Project:**
  > QD self-play would be used in Phase 2 as the primary diversity mechanism for play generation across all three lotteries. Concrete implementation: (1) Replace the current single-actor temperature-softmax sampling in src/predict.py with an M=5 actor ensemble (one actor head per desired play per call). (2) Each actor head is a 2-layer MLP on top of the SharedLotteryTransformer encoder output, outputting N logits (N=50 for LottoMax/6-49, N=49 for Daily Grand); autoregressive masking samples exactly k numbers. (3) After generating M plays per lottery per round, compute a pairwise diversity matrix (set intersection counts) and augment each actor's training reward: reward_i = hit_count_i / k + lambda * diversity_bonus_i, where lambda=0.3 is a tunable weight. (4) A lightweight MAP-Elites archive bins plays by two behavioral descriptors: (a) fraction of numbers in the low third of the pool (1-17 for LottoMax), (b) count of numbers in the top third (34-50). This 3x3 grid forces coverage of low/mid/high number regions. (5) After each real draw, all M actors are updated using REINFORCE with the diversity-augmented reward; the archive is updated with the current best plays per cell. (6) The bandit.py Thompson sampling selects which archive cell to draw predictions from at inference time. This replaces temperature-softmax in predict.py and extends bandit.py with archive-aware cell selection.

- **Integration Point:** Phase 2 (Actor-Critic play generation)
- **Replaces Or Extends:**
  > Extends temperature-softmax sampling in src/predict.py by replacing single-actor generation with an M-actor ensemble with pairwise diversity reward. Extends bandit.py with archive-aware Thompson sampling over behavioral descriptor cells. Does not replace the SharedLotteryTransformer backbone — adds actor heads on top of the existing encoder.

- **Benchmark Results:**
  > MAP-Elites (Mouret & Clune 2015): originally demonstrated on robot locomotion morphology search — archives of 13,000+ diverse locomotion strategies found vs. single-solution optimization finding 1. QD-RL (Colas et al. 2020, Nilsson & Cully 2021): applied to continuous control (HalfCheetah, Ant locomotion) — QD-RL fills 60-80% of behavioral archive cells vs. 5-15% for pure reward maximization, with no statistically significant quality degradation vs. best single-policy RL. Felten et al. 2023 QD-MORL: achieves 2-3x improvement in hypervolume coverage of the Pareto front on MO-MuJoCo benchmarks vs. standard MORL baselines. Self-play diversity bonus: Jaderberg et al. (2019) Capture the Flag — population-based self-play with diversity reward produces qualitatively more diverse strategies than single-agent training; direct metric comparison not combinatorial. No published benchmark on discrete combinatorial k-of-N outputs with n < 200 samples.

- **Notable Use Cases:**
  > 1. Robot morphology and locomotion search (original MAP-Elites application) — find 1000s of diverse locomotion strategies across terrain/body-shape behavior space; structural similarity: combinatorial discrete choices (joint angles, morphology), archive-based diversity. 2. Game AI diversity (OpenAI Five, AlphaStar, Capture the Flag) — self-play populations produce diverse strategies resistant to exploitation; structural similarity: multiple solution diversity, competitive quality-diversity tradeoff. 3. Drug/molecule design with QD — maintaining diverse chemical scaffolds while maximizing binding affinity; structural similarity: discrete combinatorial selection (atoms, bonds), sparse reward (assay results), small validated set. 4. Combinatorial optimization with QD (Vassiliades & Mouret 2018, QD for TSP) — MAP-Elites applied to vehicle routing to find diverse high-quality route families; directly analogous combinatorial structure. 5. Recommendation slate diversity — self-play diversity rewards for generating non-redundant item sets in recommender systems; directly analogous k-of-N combinatorial structure with explicit diversity requirement.


**Uncertain Fields**

- data_regime_evidence
- convergence_guarantees_small_n
- existing_open_source_implementations

### Set-Valued Action Spaces in Reinforcement Learning {#set-valued-action-spaces-in-reinforcement-learning}

**Type:** Theory | **Relevance:** 4/5 | **Integration:** Phase 2 (Actor-Critic play generation) + Bandit (extend bandit.py with semi-bandit feedback)

**General**

- **Year:** 2013
- **Key Paper:**
  > Chen, W., Wang, Y., & Yuan, Y. (2013). Combinatorial Multi-Armed Bandit: General Framework and Applications. ICML 2013. Secondary: Lattimore, T., & Szepesvari, C. (2020). Bandit Algorithms (Cambridge University Press) — Chapter 22: Combinatorial Bandits. Also foundational: Cesa-Bianchi, N., & Lugosi, G. (2012). Combinatorial Bandits. Journal of Computer and System Sciences, 78(5), 1404-1422.

- **Algorithm Description:**
  > Set-valued action spaces formalize the RL problem where the agent must select a subset S of size k from a ground set of N items at each decision step, rather than a single scalar action. The action space has combinatorial size C(N, k) — for N=50, k=7 this is ~99 million possible actions, making tabular methods infeasible. The formal framework distinguishes three sub-problems: (1) Combinatorial bandits — a one-step version where a subset is selected and a reward observed, without state transitions; the key variants are semi-bandit feedback (reward observed per selected item) vs. bandit feedback (reward observed for the whole set). (2) Top-k selection — a special case where the goal is to identify the k highest-expected-reward items, studied as a pure exploration problem with PAC-style sample complexity bounds. (3) Action space factorization — decomposing the joint subset selection into a product of simpler distributions, enabling tractable policy gradient methods. The unifying theoretical insight is that the expected reward of a subset decomposes under linearity assumptions (CMAB-T model), enabling efficient algorithms that avoid enumeration.

- **Action Space Handling:**
  > Three principal approaches exist for representing and sampling from set-valued action spaces: (1) Flat enumeration — enumerate all C(N,k) subsets explicitly; exact but computationally infeasible for N > 20, k > 3. (2) Factored independent sampling — assign a score/logit to each item independently, then select the top-k or sample without replacement; tractable in O(N log k) but cannot model inter-item correlations within the subset. (3) Autoregressive factorization — model the joint probability of a subset as a product of conditionals: P(S) = P(s1) * P(s2 | s1) * ... * P(sk | s1,...,s_{k-1}); expressive but requires k sequential forward passes and k-fold credit assignment. The combinatorial bandit literature primarily uses the factored independent model (items are assumed to be independent given context), while deep RL approaches increasingly use autoregressive factorization (pointer networks, transformer decoders) to capture within-subset correlations.

- **How Combinatorial Output Is Formed:**
  > The canonical mechanisms for generating a k-subset from {1..N} are: (1) Score-and-select (greedy top-k) — a network produces N scores, and the top-k by score are taken deterministically; non-differentiable, used in inference. (2) Gumbel-top-k sampling — add i.i.d. Gumbel noise to N logits and take top-k; produces an exact sample from the Plackett-Luce distribution without replacement; differentiable via soft-top-k relaxation. (3) Autoregressive masking — a recurrent or transformer decoder selects items one at a time, masking previously selected items; pointer networks (Vinyals et al. 2015) are the canonical architecture. (4) Determinantal Point Processes (DPPs) — a probabilistic model where P(S) is proportional to det(L_S) for a kernel matrix L; naturally models negative correlations (diversity) within the selected subset; sampling is O(Nk^2). (5) Action set embedding — embed each candidate subset (or a factored representation of it) and train a critic/Q-function on subset embeddings; avoids enumeration but requires generalization across exponentially many subsets.

- **Sequential Generation Strategy:**
  > The literature identifies three generation strategies with distinct tradeoffs: (1) Joint (flat) — all k items are selected simultaneously from N independent logits using top-k or Gumbel-top-k; O(N) computation per play; cannot model within-subset correlations; probability model is Plackett-Luce (or Bernoulli multi-hot with post-hoc k-constraint); standard for combinatorial bandits. (2) Autoregressive — items are generated sequentially, each conditioned on previously selected items via masking or embedding; k forward passes required; captures co-occurrence structure; pointer networks and set transformers use this strategy; dominant in combinatorial optimization RL literature. (3) Energy/flow-based — a global energy function over subsets is defined and subsets are sampled via MCMC or a learned flow (GFlowNets); exact modeling of full joint distribution over subsets at cost of O(Nk) or O(N^2) computation; GFlowNets are the leading 2023-2024 representative of this approach for combinatorial generation tasks.

- **Gradient Estimator Type:**
  > Several gradient estimators are applicable to set-valued action spaces, ordered by variance and bias: (1) REINFORCE (score function / likelihood-ratio) — applicable to any differentiable policy, unbiased but high variance; variance grows with k (k independent log-probability terms each contributing noise). (2) RLOO (Leave-One-Out baseline) — a per-sample baseline using other samples in the batch; reduces REINFORCE variance by a factor of (B-1)/B for batch size B; recommended for combinatorial outputs with small datasets. (3) Gumbel reparameterization — for Plackett-Luce / Gumbel-top-k policies; low variance reparameterization gradient; requires differentiable reward or surrogate. (4) Straight-through estimator — applied to hard top-k; high bias, low variance; common for binary subset selection when differentiability is needed cheaply. (5) Stochastic perturbation (SPSA / ES) — black-box gradient estimation via N-point perturbation; applicable when reward is non-differentiable; scales poorly with N. For the combinatorial bandit setting specifically, the semi-bandit gradient (CUCB, CombLinUCB) avoids gradient estimation entirely by using upper confidence bounds on per-item expected rewards.

- **Combinatorial Constraint Enforcement:**
  > The formally studied mechanisms for enforcing exactly k distinct items from {1..N} are: (1) Hard top-k (argmax-based) — exactly k distinct items are selected by construction; non-differentiable; no chance of duplicates; standard in combinatorial bandit algorithms. (2) Without-replacement sampling (Gumbel-top-k, Plackett-Luce) — probabilistic selection of exactly k distinct items; differentiable via soft relaxation; no post-hoc filtering needed. (3) Autoregressive masking — at each of k steps, previously selected items have their logits set to -inf before softmax; exactly k distinct items by construction; differentiable through each step. (4) Constrained decoding with feasibility oracle — a general approach using Lagrangian relaxation or projection to enforce subset constraints; applicable to arbitrary combinatorial constraints beyond simple size k. (5) Post-hoc rejection sampling — sample with replacement then reject duplicates; simple but introduces bias, especially when k/N is large (e.g., k=7, N=50 means 14% of the pool selected, making duplicate collisions common). Theoretical analysis shows autoregressive masking is the only approach that simultaneously guarantees exact k, is differentiable, and can model correlations.

- **Sample Efficiency Rating:**
  > medium-to-high for combinatorial bandits (with semi-bandit feedback); low for full combinatorial RL. Combinatorial bandit algorithms (CUCB, CombLinUCB, Thompson sampling for linear payoffs) achieve regret bounds of O(k * sqrt(N * T)) under the linear payoff assumption — this scales favorably because only N parameters (one per item) are estimated rather than C(N,k). With semi-bandit feedback (reward observed per selected item), each draw yields up to k signal observations rather than 1, giving a k-fold effective sample size improvement. Concretely: with N=50 items, k=7, and 100 historical draws, semi-bandit feedback yields up to 700 item-level reward observations — sufficient for Thompson sampling on per-item Bernoulli distributions to converge to reasonable posteriors. This is the strongest theoretical argument for using the combinatorial bandit framework over deep RL for this application. Full combinatorial RL (with state transitions and a learned policy network) loses this favorable scaling and reverts to standard RL sample complexity.

- **Regularization Built In:**
  > Regularization in set-valued action space methods varies by approach: (1) Combinatorial bandits (CUCB, Thompson sampling) — UCB confidence bounds and Thompson posterior widths act as implicit regularization; no overfitting in the traditional sense since these are non-parametric models. (2) Plackett-Luce policy gradient — entropy of the Plackett-Luce distribution over k-subsets can be computed in closed form and added as a bonus; the Gumbel temperature acts as an inverse regularization parameter (higher temperature = more entropic policy). (3) Autoregressive policies (pointer networks) — standard neural network regularization applies (dropout, weight decay, label smoothing on the sequential selections); additionally, the masking constraint prevents policy from converging to degenerate subsets with repeated items. (4) DPP-based selection — the determinant kernel in DPPs provides built-in diversity regularization (high probability subsets are those with maximally diverse items), which can be interpreted as a structural inductive bias against mode collapse. (5) Linear combinatorial bandits (CombLinUCB) — L2 regularization on the feature weight vector (ridge regression) is standard; provides generalization across items sharing features.

- **Known Failure Modes On Small Data:**
  > Documented failure modes for set-valued action spaces with n < 200: (1) Non-linear payoff violation — combinatorial bandit algorithms (CUCB, CombLinUCB) assume the expected reward of a subset decomposes as a sum/function of per-item rewards; lottery payoffs are highly non-linear (jackpot requires matching all k), and this assumption fails badly, causing theoretical guarantees to collapse. (2) Curse of combinations — methods that attempt to learn over the full subset space (Q-learning on subsets, value-based RL) face C(N,k) states and cannot generalize with n < 200 samples; any method using a lookup table over subsets will completely overfit. (3) Semi-bandit feedback mismatch — in a standard lottery draw, feedback is semi-bandit (we know which of our selected numbers matched) but the underlying ball distribution does not change between draws; some algorithms assume an adversarial or stochastic bandit model that does not match the lottery's fixed but unknown draw distribution. (4) Mode collapse in autoregressive policies — with sparse reward and fewer than 200 episodes, autoregressive policies (pointer networks) frequently collapse to outputting the same k-subset repeatedly; entropy regularization is insufficient without enough reward signal. (5) Overconfident posterior in Thompson sampling — with only 100 draws, the Beta/Dirichlet posterior over ball frequencies is heavily influenced by the prior choice; a flat (uninformative) prior can lead to either too-confident estimates early or too-diffuse estimates never converging.

- **Data Regime Evidence:**
  > **minimum_training_samples_tested**: Combinatorial bandit papers (Chen et al. 2013, CUCB) prove regret bounds that hold for any T but do not specify a minimum T for the algorithm to outperform random selection. Empirically, CombLinUCB with semi-bandit feedback on N=50 items has been validated in recommendation system experiments with as few as 50-200 rounds (equivalent to draws) when items have rich feature representations (Qin et al. 2014, Cascading Bandits). Without features (pure bandit, Bernoulli rewards per item), the minimum T for meaningful convergence in N=50 is approximately 200-500 rounds per simulation study. For deep RL methods (pointer networks, set transformer + PPO), no published result demonstrates effective learning with fewer than 1000 training episodes on combinatorial tasks. The lottery-nn scenario (100-200 draws, no item features beyond historical frequency) falls at the boundary of the favorable regime for combinatorial bandits and entirely outside the validated regime for deep combinatorial RL.; **performance_degradation_curve**: [uncertain] — No published degradation curve exists for combinatorial bandit or combinatorial RL methods specifically in the 50-200 sample range with lottery-like sparse, non-linear payoffs. The general bandit theory predicts O(k * sqrt(N * T)) cumulative regret, which improves monotonically with T; at T=100, N=50, k=7 this gives regret ~O(470) relative to the optimal policy — dominated by noise in the small-T regime. Empirical curves for CUCB and Thompson sampling on recommendation tasks with N=50, k=5 show convergence to near-optimal selection around T=300-500 rounds under Bernoulli reward assumptions.; **comparison_baseline**: Chen et al. 2013 compare CUCB to UCB1 (single-item bandit ignoring combinatorial structure) and random selection; CUCB achieves lower regret per item-observation under the linear payoff model. Qin et al. 2014 (Cascading Bandits) show combinatorial UCB variants match or exceed greedy heuristics at T=200-500 on click-through-rate data (N=50, k=5). No paper directly compares combinatorial bandit vs. combinatorial RL in the n<200 regime; this gap in the literature is a known limitation acknowledged in recent survey papers (Lattimore & Szepesvari 2020, Chapter 22).

- **Reward Distribution Handling:**
  > Set-valued action space theory addresses sparse and skewed rewards differently across its sub-frameworks: (1) Combinatorial bandits — designed for repeated selection with observed reward; handle sparse rewards by construction (UCB exploration ensures all items are tried; low-reward items are deprioritized, not ignored). However, jackpot-only rewards (binary win/loss) with p ~ 1e-7 are effectively never observed in 100 draws, making the bandit framework degenerate. Semi-bandit feedback (per-ball hit/miss) with reward = number_of_hits is the only practically viable reward signal — this transforms the problem from jackpot prediction to frequency/hot-number learning. (2) Deep combinatorial RL — reward shaping is essential; raw binary reward (jackpot won?) provides zero gradient signal. Shaped rewards (hit_count / k, or marginal gain per matched number) make the problem tractable at the cost of incentive misalignment. (3) Distributional approaches — distributional RL (IQN, C51) can model the full distribution of set-level rewards rather than just the expectation; this is theoretically attractive for lottery where the reward distribution is highly multimodal (nearly always 0, sometimes 1-7). Minimum draws for stable combinatorial RL: 500+ for semi-bandit feedback; effectively infinite for jackpot-only binary feedback without reward shaping.

- **Credit Assignment Granularity:**
  > This is the theoretically most important field for the lottery application. Set-valued action space theory distinguishes: (1) Full-bandit feedback — only the total reward for the selected subset is observed; gradient signal is a single scalar per draw; hardest credit assignment problem; sample complexity O(C(N,k)) in worst case. (2) Semi-bandit feedback — the reward for each selected item is observed individually (for lottery: which numbers matched); reduces sample complexity from O(C(N,k)) to O(N) because per-item estimates can be maintained; enables combinatorial UCB and Thompson sampling algorithms with provable O(sqrt(NT)) regret vs. O(sqrt(C(N,k)*T)) for full-bandit. (3) Cascading feedback — a special semi-bandit variant where items are examined sequentially until one fails; not applicable to lottery. (4) Number-level credit in deep RL — when using policy gradient on a factored (independent) policy, the gradient for each item's logit is proportional to whether that item was selected times the total reward; this achieves number-level credit assignment implicitly when the policy is factored. True number-level credit (directly measuring item i's marginal contribution to subset reward) requires a Shapley value computation or a separate critic per item. For lottery-nn: using hit_count as reward with semi-bandit feedback (was ball i selected AND did it appear in the draw?) provides number-level credit and is the single most important design decision for sample efficiency.

- **Diversity Mechanism:**
  > Diversity in set-valued action spaces is both a practical concern (generating different plays) and a formal optimality requirement in some frameworks: (1) Determinantal Point Processes (DPPs) — formally model repulsion between selected items via the determinant kernel; high-probability subsets are maximally diverse by mathematical construction; used in recommendation systems and summarization for explicit diversity. (2) Plackett-Luce / Gumbel-top-k — diversity arises from stochastic Gumbel perturbation; independent perturbations across items mean different plays are generated at each forward pass without explicit diversity reward; the temperature parameter controls entropy. (3) Submodular reward shaping — adding a submodular diversity penalty (e.g., coverage reward) to the bandit reward encourages exploration of different k-subsets; studied in combinatorial bandit literature for coverage objectives. (4) Population-based methods — maintaining a population of diverse policies (evolutionary strategies, Quality-Diversity algorithms) that collectively cover different regions of the subset space. (5) Entropy regularization — maximizing H(pi) over the distribution of k-subsets; for Plackett-Luce this is tractable; for autoregressive models this requires summing over all k sequential steps. For lottery-nn: Gumbel-top-k stochastic sampling provides sufficient diversity for generating distinct plays; DPPs provide structural diversity (selecting historically uncorrelated balls); entropy bonus prevents collapse to a single dominant play.

- **Offline Vs Online Rl Compatibility:**
  > This is the critical axis for the lottery application (one real draw per week). Analysis by sub-framework: (1) Combinatorial bandits — inherently online (each UCB update requires a new observed reward); can be adapted for offline by treating historical draws as a fixed dataset and computing posterior estimates (Thompson sampling with Beta priors over ball frequencies); this is valid and efficient — each historical draw contributes k semi-bandit observations; fully offline operation is possible and theoretically grounded. (2) Linear combinatorial bandits (CombLinUCB) — offline training is straightforward: fit a ridge regression on historical (context, reward) pairs, then apply UCB exploration strategy; this is the standard offline contextual bandit approach. (3) Deep combinatorial RL — fundamentally online; offline adaptation requires importance weighting (per-decision importance sampling for factored policies), offline RL techniques (CQL, IQL), or behavior cloning pre-training; all add implementation complexity. (4) Top-k identification — a pure exploration problem; offline data from historical draws can directly inform which k balls have highest historical frequency; this is statistically valid (maximum likelihood estimate of ball probabilities from historical draws). Verdict: combinatorial bandit approaches (Thompson sampling, CombLinUCB) are fully compatible with offline training on historical draws; deep combinatorial RL requires explicit offline RL adaptation to work without online interaction.

- **Complexity:** medium
- **Key Dependencies:**
  > For combinatorial bandit approaches: NumPy/SciPy only (Thompson sampling is 20 lines of numpy; CombLinUCB requires ridge regression from scipy.linalg). For DPP-based methods: dppy library (pip install dppy) for efficient k-DPP sampling; alternatively torch-based DPP implementations. For action space factorization in deep RL: PyTorch only (factored Bernoulli heads or autoregressive transformer decoder). For Plackett-Luce / Gumbel-top-k: PyTorch only (no external dependency). For full combinatorial RL: torchrl (Meta's RL library) provides CompositeActionSpec for structured action spaces. Existing bandit.py in lottery-nn is the natural integration point — Thompson sampling for combinatorial bandits extends it with ~80 lines of additional code.

- **Estimated Lines Of Code:**
  > 60-150 lines depending on chosen sub-method: (a) Thompson sampling combinatorial bandit with semi-bandit feedback: ~60-80 lines — extend existing bandit.py with per-ball Beta posteriors, k-subset selection via posterior sampling, and semi-bandit reward update; (b) CombLinUCB with ball feature vectors: ~100-120 lines — ridge regression fit, UCB score computation, greedy top-k selection; (c) Factored independent policy with Gumbel-top-k (deep RL): ~50-80 additional lines over existing predict.py — replace temperature-softmax sampling with Gumbel-top-k primitive; (d) Full autoregressive policy with masking: ~200-300 lines — new decoder module, sequential masking loop, k-step advantage estimation; (e) DPP-based diverse subset sampling: ~80-100 lines — kernel matrix construction from ball logits, k-DPP sampling call via dppy.

- **Transfer Learning Compatibility:**
  > Excellent for all deep learning sub-methods. The existing SharedLotteryTransformer produces per-ball logits (main_logits: [batch, N]) which are exactly the input required by Gumbel-top-k, autoregressive masking, or factored Bernoulli policies. No architectural changes to the backbone are required. For combinatorial bandit methods (Thompson sampling, CombLinUCB), transfer is indirect: the transformer's logits can serve as informative priors for the bandit's per-ball score estimates, or the backbone can be frozen and only the bandit posterior maintained on top. This hybrid (pretrained neural backbone + combinatorial bandit on top of its outputs) is the most practical and transfer-compatible integration pattern for the lottery-nn project, combining the backbone's sequence modeling capability with the bandit's theoretical sample-efficiency guarantees.

- **Relevance Score:** 4
- **Specific Application In Project:**
  > Set-valued action space theory provides the formal foundation for the entire Phase 2 Actor-Critic play generation system and the existing bandit.py. Concrete applications: (1) Theoretical justification for Thompson sampling in bandit.py — the existing Thompson sampling implementation is a combinatorial bandit with semi-bandit feedback; understanding the CMAB-T framework clarifies when its guarantees hold (linear payoff assumption: number of hits = sum of per-ball hit indicators) and when they break (jackpot-only reward). (2) Semi-bandit feedback implementation — the most impactful code change is converting the current play-level reward (hit_count) into per-ball semi-bandit rewards: for each play, record which of the k selected balls matched the draw; update each matched ball's Beta posterior separately. This is a 20-line change to bandit.py that provides a k-fold improvement in effective sample size. (3) Gumbel-top-k integration — replace the current rejection-sampling loop in predict.py with a Gumbel-top-k forward pass over the transformer's logits; this is Phase 2's core Actor mechanism, grounded in the Plackett-Luce theory of set-valued actions. (4) DPP diversity sampling — optionally replace naive entropy-temperature diversity with DPP-based selection of diverse plays from the posterior distribution, using historical ball co-occurrence as the diversity kernel. Applies to all three lotteries (LottoMax, 6/49, Daily Grand) since all share the same SharedLotteryTransformer backbone.

- **Integration Point:** Phase 2 (Actor-Critic play generation) + Bandit (extend bandit.py with semi-bandit feedback)
- **Replaces Or Extends:**
  > Extends bandit.py with semi-bandit (per-ball) reward updates and combinatorial posterior sampling. Extends predict.py by grounding the current temperature-softmax sampling in Plackett-Luce theory and replacing the rejection-sampling duplicate-avoidance loop with Gumbel-top-k. Does not replace the SharedLotteryTransformer backbone.

- **Benchmark Results:**
  > Key empirical results from the combinatorial bandit and set-valued RL literature: (1) CUCB (Chen et al. 2013, ICML): achieves O(k * sqrt(N*T*log T)) regret on synthetic combinatorial problems; outperforms naive UCB1 (treating each subset as an arm) by factor of C(N,k) / N in sample complexity. (2) CombLinUCB / LinUCB for slates (Qin et al. 2014): on Yahoo! news recommendation (N=271 articles, k=5 slots), semi-bandit LinUCB achieves CTR 6-8% above random selection within 200 rounds, comparable to full-bandit methods needing 1000+ rounds. (3) Cascading Bandits (Kveton et al. 2015, AISTATS): KL-UCB variant achieves near-optimal regret on click-through data with N=50, k=5 within 500 rounds. (4) DPP for diverse recommendation (Chen et al. 2018, KDD): DPP-based set selection improves diversity metrics by 30-50% vs. greedy top-k while maintaining >90% of relevance score. (5) Pointer networks for combinatorial RL (Bello et al. 2016, ICLR): REINFORCE-trained pointer network solves TSP-50 to within 1-2% of optimal using 1M training instances — not directly applicable to small-data regime. (6) No benchmark exists for any of these methods on lottery-specific tasks (N~50, k~7, sparse non-linear reward, n<200 draws).

- **Notable Use Cases:**
  > Structurally similar real-world applications of set-valued action space methods: (1) Slate recommendation systems — Netflix, YouTube, and Spotify select k=5-20 items from N=10k-1M candidates; semi-bandit feedback (user clicks on individual items) enables efficient combinatorial bandit learning; this is the closest structural analog to lottery play generation at the algorithm level, though N and T are much larger. (2) Clinical trial arm selection — selecting k treatment combinations from N possible interventions; small sample sizes (n<200 patients), non-linear (interaction) payoffs, semi-bandit feedback (per-treatment outcome observed); the non-linear payoff challenge matches the lottery jackpot structure. (3) Portfolio optimization — selecting k assets from N candidates to maximize risk-adjusted return; subset constraints (budget, sector limits), sparse reward (annual return), and correlation structure between selected assets — structurally very close to lottery, studied under submodular maximization and combinatorial bandit frameworks. (4) Drug combination screening — selecting k compounds from N candidates; combinatorial synergy effects, expensive reward observations (~cost-per-experiment analogous to cost-per-draw), small dataset regime; active area connecting combinatorial bandits to Bayesian optimization. (5) Feature subset selection in AutoML — selecting k features from N to maximize validation accuracy; offline dataset, combinatorial structure; Thompson sampling and greedy submodular approaches are standard.

### Soft Actor-Critic for Discrete Action Spaces (SAC-Discrete) {#soft-actor-critic-for-discrete-action-spaces-sac-discrete}

**Type:** RL Algorithm | **Relevance:** 4/5 | **Integration:** Phase 2 (Actor-Critic play generation)

**General**

- **Year:** 2019
- **Key Paper:** Christodoulou, P. (2019). Soft Actor-Critic for Discrete Action Spaces. arXiv:1910.07207. Derived from Haarnoja et al., SAC: Off-Policy Maximum Entropy Deep RL with a Stochastic Actor, ICML 2018.
- **Algorithm Description:**
  > SAC-Discrete extends the continuous Soft Actor-Critic algorithm to finite discrete action spaces. The Actor outputs a categorical probability distribution over all actions via a softmax head; the two Q-Critics (to reduce overestimation bias) receive only the state and return Q-values for every action simultaneously in a single forward pass, avoiding the need to enumerate transitions. The policy is trained to maximise a weighted combination of expected return and policy entropy H(pi), with the entropy coefficient alpha tuned automatically via a dual-gradient-descent objective targeting a minimum entropy threshold. The target entropy is typically set to -log(1/|A|) * 0.98. Off-policy replay buffer training makes it highly sample-efficient compared to on-policy methods.

- **Action Space Handling:**
  > Fully discrete flat action space. The Actor outputs a softmax distribution over |A| actions; the Critics output |A| Q-values per state, making evaluation O(1) rather than O(|A|) queries. Actions are sampled from the categorical distribution during training and chosen greedily (or via temperature) at inference. The method does not natively handle structured or combinatorial action spaces without adaptation.

- **How Combinatorial Output Is Formed:**
  > In the vanilla formulation SAC-Discrete does not produce combinatorial outputs natively. To select k distinct numbers from {1..N} it must be adapted: the most straightforward extension is autoregressive masking, where the Actor is queried k times sequentially; after each selection the chosen number is masked to zero probability (via -inf logit) before the next sample. An alternative is to treat the entire k-subset as a single atomic action (|A| = C(N,k)), which is intractable for N=50, k=7. The autoregressive-masked variant is practical and retains entropy regularization at each step.

- **Sequential Generation Strategy:**
  > When adapted for combinatorial output via autoregressive masking, numbers are generated one at a time conditioned on previously selected numbers (autoregressive). Inter-number correlations can be modeled if the Actor's input at step t includes the already-selected set (e.g., as a multi-hot mask appended to the state). In the unadapted flat form, generation is joint/flat (single pass, no correlation modeling).

- **Gradient Estimator Type:**
  > No discrete gradient estimator is needed for the Actor because the expected Q-value is computed as a closed-form dot product between the softmax probabilities and the Q-vector: E_a[Q(s,a)] = pi(s)^T Q(s). This is a simple sum, fully differentiable through the softmax without REINFORCE or Gumbel-softmax. This is the key computational advantage of SAC-Discrete over policy-gradient methods.

- **Combinatorial Constraint Enforcement:**
  > Autoregressive masking: after selecting number i at step t, the Actor's logit for i is set to -inf before softmax at step t+1, guaranteeing distinctness. This is applied at both training-time (when generating transitions) and inference-time. No rejection sampling loop is needed. The k constraint (select exactly k) is enforced by running exactly k autoregressive steps.

- **Sample Efficiency Rating:**
  > Medium-high. SAC-Discrete is off-policy and uses an experience replay buffer, making it far more sample-efficient than on-policy methods (PPO, A2C). Entropy regularization prevents premature convergence and helps exploration on sparse data. However, the Q-function needs meaningful return signal to stabilize, and with fewer than 100 training episodes it may take many epochs before the Critics converge. Rated medium (not high) because the reward signal in the lottery domain is very sparse and near-random, limiting how much the Q-function can learn even with good replay efficiency.

- **Regularization Built In:**
  > Maximum entropy objective: the policy is penalized for low entropy at every update step, preventing mode collapse and encouraging exploration. Automatic entropy tuning (alpha dual update) adapts the regularization strength dynamically. Double-Q Critics reduce overestimation. Polyak-averaged target networks provide stable bootstrapping targets. No explicit dropout or weight decay in the base algorithm, but they can be added to network layers.

- **Known Failure Modes On Small Data:**
  > Q-function divergence: with very few transitions in the replay buffer, bootstrapped TD targets are noisy and can cause Q-value explosion, especially early in training. Entropy collapse: if alpha decays too fast, the policy can collapse to a near-deterministic mapping before the Q-function has learned anything useful. Reward sparsity amplification: in the lottery domain almost all plays score 0 hits; the Critic may converge to a near-zero Q-function for all actions, making the entropy bonus the only gradient signal (essentially random play). Overestimation bias: even with double-Q, small batches from a sparse-reward buffer can exhibit high variance in Q-targets.

- **Reward Distribution Handling:**
  > SAC-Discrete can work with sparse, skewed rewards but requires careful design. The lottery reward distribution (~95% zero-hit, ~4% 1-2 hits, rare 3+ hits) means most replay buffer transitions carry zero reward. Distributional extensions (C51, QR-DQN style) are not built-in but can be grafted onto the Critic. The entropy bonus provides a non-zero gradient signal even when all rewards are zero, which is the primary mechanism keeping training alive in sparse-reward regimes. The minimum draws needed for stable Critic learning is approximately 500-1000 transitions in practice (achievable by replaying 100 draws many times). Semi-bandit (number-level) reward signal is not natively supported but can be incorporated by shaping the reward to count individual number hits.

- **Credit Assignment Granularity:**
  > By default, SAC-Discrete assigns credit at the play level (set-level): the reward is +k for k number hits per play. For number-level credit assignment, the reward function must be shaped manually (e.g., reward = hits / k). The autoregressive extension enables partial credit: each number selection step can receive a sub-reward based on whether that number appeared in the draw, implementing semi-bandit feedback and dramatically improving credit assignment granularity on small datasets.

- **Diversity Mechanism:**
  > The maximum entropy objective directly encourages the policy distribution to remain broad. High entropy => diverse plays across draws. The temperature parameter alpha controls the diversity-exploitation tradeoff and is tuned automatically. At prediction time, diversity across multiple plays is achieved by sampling multiple times from the stochastic policy. There is no explicit inter-play diversity reward by default; plays could still be similar if the policy distribution is multimodal with narrow peaks. An explicit diversity bonus (e.g., Hamming distance between generated plays) can be added to the reward.

- **Offline Vs Online Rl Compatibility:**
  > SAC-Discrete is an off-policy algorithm and can be trained on a fixed historical replay buffer containing all past draws, making it compatible with the offline setting (one draw per week). The replay buffer is simply pre-populated with all historical draw observations. However, without new environment interaction the Q-function may exhibit distributional shift artifacts (overestimated Q-values for out-of-distribution actions), analogous to offline RL pathologies. Conservative regularization (CQL penalty) can be added to mitigate this. Overall it is more offline-compatible than on-policy methods (PPO/A2C) but less robust than purpose-built offline RL algorithms (CQL, IQL).

- **Complexity:**
  > Medium. The base SAC-Discrete is straightforward to implement in PyTorch (~300-400 lines). Integrating with the existing SharedLotteryTransformer backbone and adapting for autoregressive combinatorial output adds complexity. Automatic alpha tuning and double-Q target networks are standard boilerplate.

- **Key Dependencies:** PyTorch only for core algorithm. Optional: torchrl (provides SAC-Discrete components and replay buffer utilities). No mandatory external RL library required.
- **Estimated Lines Of Code:**
  > 350-500 lines: Actor head with autoregressive masking (~80 lines), double-Q Critic head (~60 lines), replay buffer (~80 lines), SAC-Discrete training loop with alpha auto-tuning (~150 lines), integration with existing SharedLotteryTransformer encoder (~50 lines), prediction wrapper (~40 lines).

- **Transfer Learning Compatibility:**
  > High compatibility. The SharedLotteryTransformer encoder produces a state embedding that can be directly fed into the SAC-Discrete Actor and Critic heads without modification. The Actor and Critic are implemented as lightweight MLP heads attached to the frozen or fine-tuned encoder output. The lottery_id embedding already differentiates lotteries, so a single Actor/Critic pair can serve all three lotteries or separate heads can be used per lottery. Fine-tuning the encoder jointly with the Actor/Critic is also feasible.

- **Relevance Score:** 4
- **Specific Application In Project:**
  > In Phase 2, SAC-Discrete replaces the current temperature-softmax sampling in predict.py. The SharedLotteryTransformer encoder output (state) feeds into two lightweight MLP Q-Critics and one Actor with autoregressive masking. The replay buffer is pre-loaded with all historical draws (draws.csv, draws_649.csv, draws_dailygrand.csv) encoded as (state, action, reward, next_state) tuples where action = the play that was generated, reward = number of hits against the actual draw. The Actor generates plays by selecting 7 numbers (LottoMax) autoregressively with masking. After each new draw is logged via main_swarm.py log, the SAC-Discrete loop runs N update steps on the expanded buffer. Thompson sampling bandit weights in bandit.py can be updated using the per-play hit scores as before. This addresses the current limitation that predict.py has no feedback loop connecting historical hit scores back to play generation.

- **Integration Point:** Phase 2 (Actor-Critic play generation)
- **Replaces Or Extends:**
  > Replaces temperature-softmax sampling in src/predict.py. Extends the system by adding a Critic (Q-function) component alongside the existing Actor (transformer encoder + softmax head). The encoder backbone is reused, not replaced.

- **Existing Open Source Implementations:**
  > 1. Original author implementation: github.com/p-christ/Deep-Reinforcement-Learning-Algorithms-with-PyTorch (SAC_Discrete agent). 2. torchrl library (PyTorch, Meta): includes SACLoss and discrete policy components. 3. stable-baselines3 does NOT include SAC-Discrete natively (only continuous SAC). 4. CleanRL: cleanrl/sac_atari.py provides a clean single-file implementation for Atari discrete environments. 5. Ray RLlib: supports SAC with discrete action spaces via config.

- **Benchmark Results:**
  > Christodoulou (2019): SAC-Discrete matches or exceeds DQN, DDQN, and Dueling DQN on 6 Atari games (Pong, Breakout, Space Invaders, etc.) with substantially fewer environment steps, demonstrating superior sample efficiency. Entropy regularization prevents the policy collapse observed in DQN variants on sparse-reward Atari levels. No published results on combinatorial discrete action spaces or lottery-style prediction tasks.

- **Notable Use Cases:**
  > 1. Discrete robot control (joint angle discretized). 2. Game playing with discrete moves (Atari, board games). 3. Recommendation systems with discrete item selection (structurally similar to lottery: select k items from N, sparse implicit reward). 4. Discrete molecular design (select atoms/bonds sequentially -- analogous to selecting numbers autoregressively). 5. Network routing with discrete path selection. None published specifically for lottery number prediction.
