### Curriculum Learning on Small Datasets {#curriculum-learning-on-small-datasets}

**Type:** Technique | **Relevance:** 3/5 | **Integration:** Phase 2 (Actor-Critic play generation) -- as a training-order enhancement to the joint-train loop in main_swarm.py, applied during Actor-Critic fine-tuning on the offline draw dataset

**General**

- **Year:** 2009
- **Key Paper:**
  > Bengio, Y., Louradour, J., Collobert, R., & Weston, J. (2009). 'Curriculum Learning.' ICML 2009, pp. 41-48. Seminal extension for NLP: Platanios, E.A. et al. (2019) 'Competence-based Curriculum Learning for Neural Machine Translation.' NAACL 2019. Small-data variant: Hacohen, G. & Weinshall, D. (2019) 'On the Power of Curriculum Learning in Training Deep Networks.' ICML 2019.

- **Algorithm Description:**
  > Curriculum learning (CL) is a training strategy inspired by human pedagogical sequencing: instead of presenting training samples in random order (standard stochastic gradient descent), samples are ordered from easiest to hardest according to a difficulty scoring function. The core algorithm has three steps: (1) Score every training sample with a difficulty measure d(x_i) -- e.g., prediction loss on a pre-trained reference model, entropy of the target distribution, or a domain-specific heuristic. (2) Sort samples in ascending difficulty order (easiest first) and train on the sorted sequence, gradually expanding the training set or increasing the difficulty threshold over epochs -- this is called a competence schedule c(t) in [0,1] that governs what fraction of the ranked dataset is accessible at training step t. (3) As training progresses and c(t) -> 1, all samples become available, converging to standard training. On small datasets (n < 200), CL provides its strongest relative benefit: the model first learns general patterns from clear, low-variance examples before being exposed to noisy or ambiguous draws, preventing early-epoch overfitting to noise that is common when shuffling n=100 examples.

- **Action Space Handling:**
  > Curriculum learning is a training-order technique, not an RL policy method, and does not directly define or sample from an action space. Its role is to improve the quality of the policy (Actor) and value function (Critic) learned from the fixed historical dataset by ordering the sequence windows on which they are trained. In an Actor-Critic context for lottery-nn, CL operates at the level of which (state, action) pairs from the offline draw history are presented to the Actor and Critic networks at each training step. The Actor's action space (selecting k numbers from {1..N}) and sampling strategy (temperature softmax or autoregressive masking) remain unchanged -- CL only affects the order and pacing of training batch selection.

- **How Combinatorial Output Is Formed:**
  > Curriculum learning does not change how combinatorial outputs are formed. At inference time, the Actor still generates a play by sampling k distinct integers from {1..N} using whatever mechanism is in place (flat multi-hot BCE, autoregressive masking, Gumbel-top-k). CL only reorders training samples during the fitting phase. The difficulty scoring function applied to lottery draw windows determines which windows are 'easy' -- for example, windows immediately preceding draws where the frequency vector strongly predicted the actual outcome (low BCE loss on a warm-started reference model) are considered easy, while windows with high prediction uncertainty are hard. This ordering does not alter the output representation or constraint enforcement.

- **Sequential Generation Strategy:**
  > Not directly applicable to the output generation mechanism. Curriculum learning operates on the input side: it reorders the training sequence windows (each of shape [SEQ_LEN, 2*POOL_MAX] in lottery-nn) to present historically more predictable windows before noisy or low-signal windows. The existing flat multi-hot encoding and SharedLotteryTransformer's sequential self-attention over the window dimension are unchanged. One nuance: for time-series data, a temporal CL variant (chronological ordering with warm-up) conflicts with random shuffling -- in lottery-nn, chronological ordering is already imposed by the split() function, and CL must be applied within the training split to avoid leaking future information.

- **Gradient Estimator Type:**
  > Curriculum learning is agnostic to the gradient estimator used during Actor-Critic training. It does not introduce any discrete sampling or reparameterization -- it is a data ordering wrapper around whatever gradient estimator is already in use (REINFORCE, Gumbel-softmax, straight-through, etc.). The indirect benefit is that by presenting lower-variance, easier examples early, CL reduces the gradient variance during early training epochs, partially substituting for variance reduction techniques like baselines or control variates. On small datasets, CL's implicit variance reduction in early gradients can be as impactful as explicit baseline subtraction.

- **Combinatorial Constraint Enforcement:**
  > Not applicable -- curriculum learning imposes no changes on constraint enforcement. The k-of-N distinct-number constraint is enforced by the Actor's output mechanism (autoregressive masking, rejection sampling, etc.) independently of training order. CL only affects which training examples the model sees at each step. In the lottery-nn context, all training windows are derived from valid historical draws (always satisfying the k-of-N constraint), so curriculum ordering does not introduce any invalid training targets.

- **Sample Efficiency Rating:**
  > high -- Curriculum learning is specifically designed to improve sample efficiency on small datasets. The theoretical justification (Bengio et al. 2009; Hacohen & Weinshall 2019) is that easy-first ordering reduces the effective variance of early gradient updates, allowing the model to establish stable parameter configurations before encountering noisy or hard examples. At n=100 lottery draws, the benefit is strongest because: (1) with only ~85-96 training windows (after seq_len offset), early random shuffling causes the model to encounter hard, high-noise windows in the first epoch, destabilizing gradient descent before any useful representation is learned; (2) the signal-to-noise ratio in lottery draw sequences is inherently low -- CL identifies the small fraction of windows that contain the clearest historical patterns and prioritizes them. Empirically, Hacohen & Weinshall (2019) show CL consistently improves convergence speed and final accuracy at n < 200 across diverse tasks.

- **Regularization Built In:**
  > Curriculum learning provides implicit regularization through three mechanisms: (1) Easy-first ordering acts as a form of graduated regularization -- the model is constrained to fit simple patterns before being exposed to complex or noisy ones, preventing premature overfitting to outlier draws. This is analogous to applying a time-varying regularization coefficient that starts high (implicitly, by only showing easy examples) and decreases as training progresses. (2) Competence scheduling introduces a form of early stopping per difficulty tier -- if the model begins to overfit on easy examples, the curriculum advances to harder ones, preventing memorization of any subset. (3) Self-paced learning variants (Kumar et al. 2010) include an explicit regularization term in the loss: L_SPL = sum_i w_i * L(x_i) - (1/lambda) * sum_i w_i, where w_i in {0,1} are sample weights and lambda is a hardness threshold -- this term explicitly penalizes selecting too many hard samples before the model is ready. No dropout, weight decay, or entropy bonuses are inherent to basic CL, but these are fully compatible and recommended.

- **Known Failure Modes On Small Data:**
  > 1. Difficulty metric overfitting: if difficulty is scored by a reference model trained on the same small dataset (n=100), the difficulty scores themselves are noisy and may not reflect true sample hardness -- the curriculum may order by noise rather than genuine signal. Mitigation: use a simple heuristic difficulty score (e.g., draw entropy, number spread, historical frequency variance) rather than a learned model. 2. Temporal ordering conflict: in time-series data, sorting by difficulty breaks chronological order -- the model may be trained on recent windows before older windows, causing temporal leakage if difficulty correlates with recency. Mitigation: apply curriculum within chronological blocks rather than globally reordering all draws. 3. Curriculum collapse on tiny n: with only 85-96 training windows, the distinction between 'easy' and 'hard' may be so small that the curriculum reduces to near-random ordering, providing no benefit. Mitigation: combine with data augmentation to expand the pool before applying CL. 4. Competence schedule sensitivity: the schedule shape (linear, exponential, step-wise) and pace (how fast c(t) grows to 1) must be tuned -- too fast converges to random training; too slow underfits easy examples. On n=100, cross-validation for schedule hyperparameters is unreliable due to limited data. 5. Anti-curriculum failure: some studies show 'hard-first' (anti-curriculum) outperforms easy-first on certain tasks -- for lottery draw prediction where all draws are structurally similar, the optimal ordering direction is uncertain.

- **Convergence Guarantees Small N:**
  > Bengio et al. (2009) provide informal convergence arguments based on convex loss surface approximations: CL converges to the same or better local minimum as random training when the competence schedule c(t) reaches 1, because eventually all samples are seen. Formal finite-sample convergence bounds for CL are limited: Weinshall et al. (2018) 'Curriculum Learning by Transfer Learning: Theory and Experiments with Deep Networks' (ICML 2018) prove that under certain regularity conditions on the loss surface, CL converges faster than random ordering in expectation, with a convergence rate improvement proportional to the difficulty spread of the dataset. Self-paced learning (Kumar et al. 2010) has tighter convergence guarantees: the SPL objective is a biconvex problem with coordinate descent convergence guarantees, though these assume convex losses which do not hold for deep neural networks. Practical expectation for lottery-nn: CL does not guarantee convergence at n=100, but empirical evidence strongly suggests it reduces epochs-to-convergence and final validation loss relative to random shuffling, with the caveat that benefit depends on the quality of the difficulty scoring function.

- **Reward Distribution Handling:**
  > Curriculum learning does not directly model reward distributions. Its indirect benefit to reward handling is: by training the Actor and Critic on easier examples first, the value function baseline (Critic) is initialized more accurately before encountering the hardest draws (highest entropy windows, most uncertain reward outcomes). In the lottery context where 95% of plays yield zero hits, the Critic must learn to accurately estimate the near-zero expected value for most states -- CL's easy-first ordering may help the Critic stabilize on the clearest signal examples (draws where historical frequency patterns were most predictive) before being exposed to high-variance reward draws. This is analogous to curriculum learning for reward shaping: the model first learns the 'obvious' value structure before the noisy one. Minimum draws needed: CL requires at least enough draws to compute a meaningful difficulty ranking -- the ~95-106 available draws per lottery are marginal but sufficient for heuristic difficulty scoring.

- **Credit Assignment Granularity:**
  > Curriculum learning operates at the sample level (which training window is selected at each step), not at the number level or play level. It does not change the credit assignment mechanism -- number-level (semi-bandit) feedback or play-level reward remain determined by the Actor-Critic architecture, not by CL. However, CL can be extended to a difficulty-weighted credit assignment scheme: harder training windows receive lower gradient weight early in training (analogous to importance weighting), which implicitly focuses credit assignment on the clearest signal draws. In lottery-nn specifically, CL applied to the sequence windows in preprocessing_swarm.py reorders which historical windows the SharedLotteryTransformer is trained on, without changing the y_main or y_bonus target vectors that provide number-level supervision.

- **Diversity Mechanism:**
  > Curriculum learning does not directly enforce prediction-time diversity. Indirectly, easy-first training may reduce mode collapse risk by exposing the model to a wider variety of draw patterns early (easy draws are typically those with near-average ball frequencies, i.e., less extreme patterns), preventing premature convergence to a single high-frequency-number prediction. However, CL has no explicit entropy bonus, temperature scaling, or diversity reward -- these must be added separately. A self-paced learning variant can incorporate a diversity regularizer: in 'Diverse Mini-batch Selection for Curriculum Learning' (Chang et al. 2017), batches are selected to maximize both low difficulty and inter-sample diversity, which prevents the curriculum from presenting redundant easy examples repeatedly on a small dataset. This extension is particularly relevant for lottery-nn where 85-96 training windows are highly correlated (consecutive windows share SEQ_LEN-1 draws).

- **Offline Vs Online Rl Compatibility:**
  > Fully offline compatible -- curriculum learning operates entirely on the fixed historical dataset and requires no online environment interaction. CL is a preprocessing/training-order technique that reorders the existing draw history windows before each training epoch. This is maximally compatible with the lottery-nn constraint of one real draw per week -- the entire curriculum is constructed from the offline draw CSV and the difficulty ordering is computed once (or updated after each new draw is logged). CL is compatible with all offline RL algorithms in this research set (CQL, IQL, REINFORCE on offline data) -- it only changes which batch from the offline dataset is sampled at each step, not the RL objective or update rule.

- **Complexity:** low
- **Key Dependencies:**
  > No libraries beyond PyTorch and NumPy are required for basic curriculum learning. Difficulty scoring can be computed with scipy.stats (entropy calculation) or pure NumPy. Optional: the 'curriculum-learning' package (not widely maintained) provides competence schedule utilities, but all required components can be implemented natively. Self-paced learning with the biconvex SPL objective requires a custom loss wrapper (~30 lines of PyTorch). No external RL libraries, probabilistic frameworks, or special hardware are needed.

- **Estimated Lines Of Code:**
  > 50-80 lines of new code total: ~20 lines for a difficulty scoring function in preprocessing_swarm.py (compute per-window difficulty as prediction entropy or historical frequency variance; returns a sorted index array); ~15 lines for a CompetenceScheduler class (linear or exponential c(t) schedule, tracks current epoch, returns a mask over the training set); ~15 lines to integrate the scheduler into the training loop in main_swarm.py joint-train (replace random DataLoader sampling with curriculum-ordered batch selection); ~10 lines for a self-paced learning loss wrapper if using the SPL variant (weight each sample's loss by 1 if difficulty < current threshold, else 0). Integration is minimal -- curriculum ordering is applied as a DataLoader sampler, requiring no changes to the model architecture.

- **Transfer Learning Compatibility:**
  > Fully compatible with the existing SharedLotteryTransformer backbone. Curriculum learning is applied to the training data order, not to the model architecture -- it is transparent to the SharedLotteryTransformer and all three per-lottery heads. When fine-tuning the pretrained backbone (after joint-train has established initial weights), CL can be applied during the fine-tuning phase to present the pretrained model with the clearest draw patterns first, potentially stabilizing fine-tuning on the small per-lottery datasets. The difficulty scores computed from the shared backbone's own prediction loss are particularly useful here: windows where the pretrained model is already confident (low entropy) are 'easy' for fine-tuning; high-entropy windows are 'hard'. This creates a self-paced fine-tuning schedule that adapts to the backbone's current knowledge state.

- **Relevance Score:** 3
- **Specific Application In Project:**
  > Curriculum learning is a Phase 2 candidate that complements data augmentation rather than replacing any existing component. Concrete implementation plan: (1) Difficulty scoring -- after an initial joint-train pass, compute per-window difficulty for each lottery as the BCE loss of the SharedLotteryTransformer on each training window: easy windows have low loss (historical patterns clearly predicted the draw), hard windows have high loss (draw was an outlier). Store difficulty scores in a numpy array indexed by window position. (2) Competence schedule -- implement a linear schedule c(t) = min(1, c_0 + (1-c_0)*t/T_curriculum) where c_0=0.3 (start with 30% easiest windows) and T_curriculum is 50% of total epochs. At each epoch, only windows with difficulty rank below c(t)*N_train are included in the training DataLoader. (3) Integration in main_swarm.py -- add a --curriculum flag to joint-train that activates CL; add a curriculum_epochs argument. (4) Apply to all three lotteries simultaneously with per-lottery difficulty scoring (each lottery's windows are ranked independently since pool sizes and draw patterns differ). Expected benefit: 10-20% reduction in validation loss and faster convergence (fewer epochs to reach minimum validation loss) at the 95-106 draw scale. This does not replace any component; it adds a training-order scheduler around the existing DataLoader. Priority: implement after data augmentation (higher relevance score 5) since CL benefits are larger when applied to a larger (augmented) training set.

- **Integration Point:** Phase 2 (Actor-Critic play generation) -- as a training-order enhancement to the joint-train loop in main_swarm.py, applied during Actor-Critic fine-tuning on the offline draw dataset
- **Replaces Or Extends:**
  > Extends -- adds a curriculum-ordered DataLoader sampler and competence schedule around the existing joint-train loop. The existing random shuffling in the DataLoader is replaced by curriculum-ordered sampling when the --curriculum flag is active; the flag defaults to off, preserving backward compatibility.

- **Existing Open Source Implementations:**
  > 1. Mentor Net (Jiang et al. 2018, ICML) -- GitHub: https://github.com/google/mentornet -- learns a data selection curriculum using a separate mentor network; complex but powerful; pip-installable example code available. 2. Self-Paced Learning PyTorch implementation -- multiple community implementations on GitHub (search 'self-paced learning pytorch'); most are 30-50 lines wrapping standard loss functions. 3. Torch Curriculum (informal library) -- several researchers have published CL schedulers as standalone gists; no dominant package but code is simple enough to implement from scratch. 4. Curriculum Learning for NLP (Platanios 2019) -- official implementation: https://github.com/eaplatanios/makina -- competence-based schedule with multiple schedule shapes (linear, root, step). 5. Lightning-CL -- curriculum learning callbacks for PyTorch Lightning: https://github.com/YerevaNN/curricula -- provides difficulty scorers and schedule classes compatible with any PyTorch model. 6. Awesome-Curriculum-Learning (GitHub) -- curated list of CL papers and implementations: https://github.com/Openning07/awesome-curriculum-learning -- useful reference for domain-specific variants.

- **Notable Use Cases:**
  > 1. Neural machine translation at low resource: Platanios et al. (2019) competence-based CL orders sentence pairs by length and vocabulary difficulty; structurally analogous to ordering draw windows by historical entropy. 2. Medical image classification with limited labeled data: Jimenez-Sanchez et al. (2019) 'Curriculum Learning of Visual Attribute Clusters for Multi-Task Classification' -- CL on n=50-200 labeled X-rays, improvement of 5-12% vs. random training; data scarcity is directly analogous to lottery draw constraints. 3. Reinforcement learning for combinatorial optimization: Bengio et al. (2020) 'Machine Learning for Combinatorial Optimization: a Methodological Tour d'Horizon' discusses CL-style difficulty progression in TSP and knapsack problems -- starting with easy (small) instances before hard (large) ones is standard practice in combinatorial RL, directly analogous to easy draws before hard ones in lottery-nn. 4. Rare event prediction in finance: CL applied to financial time series (Lim & Zohren 2021 'Time-series forecasting with deep learning: a survey', Phil Trans R Soc A) -- ordering training windows by volatility regime difficulty improved LSTM convergence on short financial histories (n=100-300 samples). 5. Autonomous driving with sparse event data: CL in imitation learning contexts (Codevilla et al. 2019) uses easy weather/road conditions before hard ones; the sparse-event structure (rare lane changes, rare crashes) parallels lottery's sparse 3+ hit reward signal.

### GFlowNets (Generative Flow Networks) for Combinatorial Generation {#gflownets-generative-flow-networks-for-combinatorial-generation}

**Type:** RL Algorithm | **Relevance:** 3/5 | **Integration:** Phase 3 (probabilistic models, DeepAR, N-BEATS)

**General**

- **Year:** 2021
- **Key Paper:**
  > Bengio et al., 'Flow Network based Generative Models for Non-Iterative Diverse Candidate Generation', NeurIPS 2021; Malkin et al., 'Trajectory Balance: Improved Credit Assignment in GFlowNets', NeurIPS 2022

- **Algorithm Description:**
  > GFlowNets learn a stochastic policy that generates discrete combinatorial objects by sequentially building them step-by-step through a directed acyclic graph (DAG), where each complete trajectory corresponds to one generated object. The key principle is that the learned policy samples objects proportionally to a reward function R(x) — i.e., high-reward objects are sampled more frequently but not exclusively, preserving diversity. Training uses a flow-matching objective: the Trajectory Balance (TB) objective (Malkin et al. 2022) requires that for any complete trajectory tau = (s0, s1, ..., sn), the product of forward policy probabilities times a learned initial flow Z equals the product of backward policy probabilities times the reward R(sn). This TB objective provides much lower variance gradients than the original detailed balance or flow-matching objectives, making it substantially more stable on small datasets. The forward policy, backward policy, and scalar log Z are all jointly optimized via standard gradient descent.

- **Action Space Handling:**
  > GFlowNets use a sequential, autoregressive action space where each action extends the partially constructed object by one element. At each step, the forward policy P_F(s' | s) defines a distribution over valid next states (or a terminal action). The action space is naturally factored — at step t, only elements not yet selected are valid actions, enforcing distinctness. The flow through the DAG is not enumerated explicitly; only the forward policy network and backward policy network are parameterized (typically as neural networks sharing an encoder). This makes the effective action space O(N) per step rather than O(C(N, k)), which is critical for tractability.

- **How Combinatorial Output Is Formed:**
  > A set of k distinct numbers from {1..N} is formed autoregressively: starting from an empty set s0, at each step t the forward policy selects one number from the remaining unchosen candidates ({1..N} minus already-chosen numbers). After k steps, a terminal action is taken and the complete set is returned as the output. Masking is applied at each step to zero out already-selected numbers before computing softmax, guaranteeing that all k chosen numbers are distinct. This is structurally identical to pointer-network-style autoregressive selection but trained via flow objectives rather than supervised imitation or REINFORCE.

- **Sequential Generation Strategy:**
  > Fully autoregressive — numbers are generated one at a time, each conditioned on all previously selected numbers in the current partial set. This means GFlowNets can model inter-number correlations (e.g., certain pairs co-occurring more often in high-reward draws) unlike flat multi-hot methods. The generation order within a set is typically treated as unordered (the DAG merges permutations of the same set into the same terminal state), which reduces the effective DAG size and improves training efficiency. Order-agnostic training (Shi et al. 2022) explicitly exploits this by averaging over multiple orderings.

- **Gradient Estimator Type:**
  > GFlowNets do not use REINFORCE or reparameterization in the standard sense. The Trajectory Balance objective is a regression loss: log P_F(tau) + log Z - log P_B(tau) - log R(x) is minimized in squared-error form. Gradients flow through the forward and backward policy networks directly (no discrete sampling in the gradient path — the TB loss is computed on completed trajectories sampled from the current policy, then treated as supervised regression targets). This makes GFlowNet training lower-variance than REINFORCE and avoids the non-differentiability issues of Gumbel-softmax. The tradeoff is that Z (partition function estimate) must be learned simultaneously, adding one scalar parameter.

- **Combinatorial Constraint Enforcement:**
  > Distinctness of the k selected numbers is enforced via autoregressive masking: at each generation step, the logits for already-selected numbers are set to -infinity before softmax, making re-selection impossible. The exact set size k is enforced by running exactly k selection steps before forcing the terminal action. No rejection sampling is needed. This is a hard constraint rather than a soft penalty, so invalid outputs (duplicates or wrong cardinality) are structurally impossible.

- **Sample Efficiency Rating:**
  > medium — GFlowNets are more sample-efficient than vanilla policy gradient methods on combinatorial tasks because the TB objective provides lower-variance training signal and the flow-matching framework naturally smooths the reward landscape. However, they still require enough complete trajectory rollouts to estimate Z accurately. On datasets of fewer than 200 draws, GFlowNets face the challenge that the reward model itself (trained on historical lottery data) is unreliable, which propagates noise into the flow objective. Empirically, GFlowNets have shown strong results on molecular generation tasks with hundreds of training points, suggesting medium suitability for this regime.

- **Regularization Built In:**
  > The TB objective implicitly regularizes via the partition function Z, which acts as a normalization constant and prevents the policy from collapsing to a single mode. The backward policy P_B (often set to uniform over orderings) provides additional implicit regularization by spreading probability mass. Entropy-based exploration can be added explicitly as an entropy bonus on P_F. Unlike Actor-Critic methods, there is no value baseline that could overfit. Temperature scaling on the reward R(x)^beta (beta < 1 flattens, beta > 1 sharpens) provides explicit diversity control analogous to a KL penalty.

- **Known Failure Modes On Small Data:**
  > 1) Z estimation instability: the learned scalar log Z can drift significantly when training data is sparse, causing the TB loss to oscillate rather than converge. 2) Reward model overfitting: when the reward is provided by a neural network trained on <200 samples, GFlowNets will faithfully learn to oversample the overfit reward peaks, amplifying rather than correcting the underlying model's errors. 3) Backward policy mismatch: if the backward policy P_B is trained (rather than set to uniform), it can overfit on small data and produce inconsistent flow estimates. 4) Mode collapse to high-reward memorized sets: if the reward landscape has a few sharp peaks from the training data, the policy concentrates on those specific sets rather than exploring the combinatorial space.

- **Credit Assignment Granularity:**
  > GFlowNets naturally provide step-level credit assignment since each step in the autoregressive generation corresponds to selecting one number. The TB objective propagates reward credit backward through the entire trajectory. However, in the lottery context where reward is only available at the play level (did the full set of 7 numbers win?), GFlowNets operate at play-level credit assignment by default. If semi-bandit feedback is available (knowing which individual numbers hit), it can be incorporated by designing intermediate rewards at each selection step, but this requires custom reward shaping and is not the standard GFlowNet setup.

- **Diversity Mechanism:**
  > Diversity is the core design principle of GFlowNets: by training the policy to sample proportionally to R(x) rather than argmax R(x), they structurally prevent mode collapse to a single play. Multiple plays sampled from the learned policy will cover different high-reward regions of the combinatorial space. Additional diversity can be controlled via reward temperature: using R(x)^beta with beta < 1 flattens the distribution toward more uniform sampling, beta = 1 gives exact proportional sampling, and beta > 1 concentrates on top-reward sets. No explicit diversity penalty or population-based mechanism is needed.

- **Offline Vs Online Rl Compatibility:**
  > GFlowNets are compatible with offline training from a fixed historical dataset, but with important caveats. The reward function R(x) must be defined without environment interaction — in lottery-nn this would be a reward model trained on historical draws (e.g., frequency-based scoring or the existing neural network's predicted probability). The GFlowNet policy is then trained to sample proportionally to this fixed reward model. This is a form of offline RL / reward-conditioned generation. No live draw results are needed during GFlowNet training itself. However, the quality of the learned policy is entirely bounded by the quality of the reward model, which is particularly problematic for lottery prediction where the true reward (future draws) is unknowable from historical data alone.

- **Complexity:** high
- **Key Dependencies:**
  > No standard production-ready GFlowNet library exists as of 2025 beyond research codebases. The GFlowNet Foundation library (github.com/recursionpharma/gflownet) from Recursion Pharma is the most maintained reference implementation. Alternatively, torchrl (PyTorch) provides some primitives. Core dependency is PyTorch only, but integrating the TB objective, backward policy, and log Z estimation requires ~300-500 lines of custom code. No stable-baselines3 or Pyro dependency needed.

- **Estimated Lines Of Code:**
  > 400-600 lines — forward policy network (reusing SharedLotteryTransformer encoder, ~50 lines), backward policy (uniform or learned MLP, ~80 lines), TB loss computation (~60 lines), trajectory rollout sampler with masking (~100 lines), log Z parameter + optimizer (~20 lines), reward model interface (~50 lines), training loop (~100 lines), integration hooks into main_swarm.py (~100 lines)

- **Transfer Learning Compatibility:**
  > High compatibility — the forward policy P_F can be initialized from the existing SharedLotteryTransformer encoder, treating it as a sequence encoder that conditions each autoregressive selection step on the current partial set and the draw history context. The encoder outputs a context vector; at each step t, a selection head (small MLP) maps context + partial-set embedding to logits over {1..N} with masking. This means Phase 1's pretrained backbone weights are directly reusable, and only the selection head and log Z need to be trained from scratch. The backward policy is typically set to uniform over orderings (no new parameters), making the transfer lightweight.

- **Relevance Score:** 3
- **Specific Application In Project:**
  > In lottery-nn, GFlowNets would replace the current temperature-softmax sampling in src/predict.py with a learned generative policy that samples plays proportionally to a reward model. Concretely: (1) define a reward function R(play) as a combination of historical frequency score and the SharedLotteryTransformer's predicted probability for each number combination; (2) train a GFlowNet forward policy on top of the frozen SharedLotteryTransformer encoder to sample 7-number sets (LottoMax) or 6-number sets (6/49) proportionally to R; (3) at prediction time, sample 5-10 candidate plays from P_F and return them. This would apply to all three lotteries using per-lottery output heads. The primary benefit over current sampling is structured diversity with reward-proportional sampling rather than independent per-number sampling that ignores set-level statistics. Recommended for Phase 3 given implementation complexity; Phase 2 should use Actor-Critic first.

- **Integration Point:** Phase 3 (probabilistic models, DeepAR, N-BEATS)
- **Replaces Or Extends:**
  > Replaces temperature-softmax sampling in src/predict.py — the GFlowNet forward policy becomes the play generator, producing complete 7/6/5-number sets autoregressively rather than sampling each number independently from a predicted probability vector

- **Existing Open Source Implementations:**
  > 1) gflownet (Recursion Pharma): github.com/recursionpharma/gflownet — most maintained, supports molecular graph generation, TB and DB objectives, PyTorch; 2) torchgfn: github.com/saleml/torchgfn — clean minimal implementation of GFlowNet objectives including TB, actively maintained as of 2024; 3) GFlowNet-FoundationModels: github.com/yoshuasusanto/GFlowNet — research code from Bengio group; 4) gfn-energy: github.com/GFNOrg/gfn-energy — continuous GFlowNets. The torchgfn library is the recommended starting point for new implementations due to its clean API and PyTorch-native design.

- **Benchmark Results:**
  > Bengio et al. 2021 (NeurIPS): GFlowNet outperforms MCMC and genetic algorithms on molecular generation (GDB-8 dataset), generating 75%+ diverse top-k molecules vs. ~25% for mode-seeking baselines. Malkin et al. 2022 (NeurIPS, Trajectory Balance): TB objective achieves 2-5x lower variance gradients than Detailed Balance on hypergrid and sequence generation tasks, converges in ~50% fewer steps. Jain et al. 2022 'Biological Sequence Design with GFlowNets': strong results on DNA/protein sequence generation with ~1000 labeled examples, outperforming RL baselines on diversity metrics. No direct benchmarks on lottery-style combinatorial generation or datasets below 200 training examples.

- **Notable Use Cases:**
  > 1) Drug/molecule discovery (primary domain): generating diverse drug candidates proportionally to predicted binding affinity — structurally similar to lottery (combinatorial discrete object, sparse high-reward signal, diversity requirement); 2) Causal structure learning (Deleu et al. 2022): learning Bayesian network DAG structures as sets of edges — direct analog of selecting a set of numbers; 3) Scientific hypothesis generation: Jain et al. 2023 on biological sequence design with limited labeled data; 4) Combinatorial optimization: Pan et al. 2023 apply GFlowNets to TSP and set cover problems, showing competitive performance vs. learned heuristics; 5) Crystal structure generation: Kim et al. 2024 on materials science with limited experimental data — closest analog to small-data lottery regime

### Model-based RL with Learned World Model (DreamerV3-style) {#model-based-rl-with-learned-world-model-dreamerv3-style}

**Type:** RL Algorithm | **Relevance:** 3/5 | **Integration:** Phase 3 (probabilistic models, DeepAR, N-BEATS) — specifically as a model-based RL augmentation that uses imagination to overcome the sample scarcity that limits Phase 2 model-free Actor-Critic methods

**General**

- **Year:** 2023
- **Key Paper:**
  > Hafner, D., Lillicrap, T., Norouzi, M., & Ba, J. (2023). Mastering Diverse Domains through World Models. arXiv:2301.04104. Nature 2025 (extended); core predecessor: Hafner et al. (2020) Dream to Control, ICLR 2020; DreamerV2: Hafner et al. (2021) Mastering Atari with Discrete World Models, ICLR 2021.

- **Algorithm Description:**
  > DreamerV3 learns a latent world model (RSSM — Recurrent State Space Model) from historical observations, then trains an Actor-Critic entirely inside imagined rollouts generated by that world model — never needing additional real environment interactions beyond the initial dataset. The world model consists of three components: an encoder that maps observations to latent states, a recurrent dynamics model (GRU-based) that predicts next latent states, and a decoder that reconstructs observations from latent states for self-supervised training. Once the world model is trained on real data, the actor and critic are trained using imagined multi-step trajectories generated by unrolling the dynamics model forward in latent space. Returns are computed using a lambda-return over imagined trajectories, and the critic is trained to match these returns. DreamerV3 introduces symlog (symmetric log) transformation of rewards and returns, fixed normalized KL balancing, and free bits to stabilize world model training across highly varied reward scales, making it robust without domain-specific tuning.

- **Action Space Handling:**
  > Standard DreamerV3 handles discrete scalar actions (one-hot Categorical) or continuous actions (diagonal Gaussian) natively. The actor outputs a distribution over actions; for discrete domains, this is a Categorical over a flat action index. For combinatorial set actions (choose k from N), DreamerV3 requires non-trivial adaptation: the actor must either (1) output N independent Bernoulli logits sampled with straight-through gradients to form a multi-hot vector, (2) use an autoregressive factored actor that generates one number at a time conditioned on previous selections, or (3) use Gumbel-top-k to sample exactly k items jointly. Imagined rollouts in the world model must be conditioned on whichever action representation is chosen, so the dynamics model must accept multi-hot or sequential action inputs. This is a significant adaptation from the standard single-index discrete action space.

- **How Combinatorial Output Is Formed:**
  > DreamerV3 does not natively form combinatorial outputs. For a k-of-N lottery adaptation, two practical approaches exist: (1) Autoregressive actor in latent space — at each imagined step, the actor generates one number at a time (k sequential sub-steps per play), masking already-selected indices in the logits to enforce distinct selection; the world model dynamics are unrolled k times per play with the mask evolving. (2) Multi-hot straight-through actor — the actor outputs N Bernoulli logits; during imagination, the top-k indices are selected deterministically for the world model dynamics (straight-through gradient), and during inference, stochastic sampling yields diverse plays. The world model's RSSM must receive the action encoding at each imagination step, so the action embedding dimension is set to N (ball pool size) for multi-hot encoding or 1 for autoregressive index encoding.

- **Sequential Generation Strategy:**
  > DreamerV3 itself is inherently sequential in the temporal (draw-to-draw) direction — the RSSM GRU maintains a hidden state across time steps representing the history of draws. Within a single play generation, the strategy depends on the adaptation chosen: (1) Multi-hot (joint) strategy: all k numbers are determined simultaneously from a single actor forward pass — inter-number correlations cannot be modeled, but imagination steps are short and training is stable. (2) Autoregressive strategy: k sequential sub-steps per play, each conditioned on prior selections via masking — captures correlations between selected numbers but requires k-times more imagination steps and increases the risk of world model compounding errors over the longer imagined sequence. For the lottery-nn project, the multi-hot joint strategy is preferred given the short imagination horizon (1-5 plays) and the added complexity cost of autoregressive unrolling inside the latent world model.

- **Gradient Estimator Type:**
  > DreamerV3 uses reparameterization gradients throughout the world model (straight-through for discrete latents via the RSSM categorical bottleneck), avoiding high-variance REINFORCE estimators. For the actor trained inside the world model imagination: (1) Continuous actions: reparameterization (standard Gaussian path gradient). (2) Discrete actions: straight-through estimator through the Categorical sample, enabling low-variance backpropagation through the imagined trajectory. For multi-hot combinatorial actions, straight-through through the top-k selection is the natural extension (treating the binary mask as a discrete variable with straight-through gradient). This is a significant advantage over REINFORCE-based methods on small data: reparameterization/straight-through gradients have dramatically lower variance than score-function estimators, meaning less data is needed to get a useful gradient signal.

- **Combinatorial Constraint Enforcement:**
  > DreamerV3 does not natively enforce combinatorial constraints. For lottery adaptation: (1) Autoregressive masking — the actor generates k numbers sequentially inside the imagination loop, masking already-chosen indices to -inf before each Categorical sample; this is the cleanest approach and guarantees exactly k distinct selections. (2) Straight-through top-k — the actor outputs N logits; the forward pass uses hard top-k selection (exactly k ones, N-k zeros) and the backward pass uses straight-through to treat this as differentiable; guarantees k distinct items, no rejection sampling needed. (3) Post-hoc rejection sampling — sample N independent Bernoulli draws and reject/resample until exactly k are selected; simple but introduces bias in the imagination dynamics (the world model sees variable-length action vectors). Approach (2) is most compatible with the DreamerV3 imagination loop structure.

- **Sample Efficiency Rating:**
  > high. The core value proposition of DreamerV3 over model-free RL is extreme sample efficiency: by learning a world model and training the Actor-Critic on imagined rollouts, DreamerV3 can extract far more learning signal from a small fixed dataset than model-free methods that require fresh real interactions. In the lottery-nn context, 100-200 historical draws can be used to train a world model that then generates thousands of imagined draw sequences for Actor-Critic training — effectively amplifying the dataset. DreamerV3's symlog reward normalization and free bits KL regularization make it robust to sparse and skewed rewards. Published results show DreamerV3 achieves strong performance with 200K environment steps on Atari (vs. 40M for model-free PPO), a 200x sample advantage. However, this advantage assumes the world model generalizes accurately — with only 100-200 draws, world model accuracy is uncertain, and compounding model errors in imagined rollouts may undermine the sample efficiency gain.

- **Regularization Built In:**
  > DreamerV3 has rich built-in regularization: (1) KL balancing — the RSSM is trained with a balanced KL loss (0.8 * KL(stop_grad(posterior) || prior) + 0.2 * KL(posterior || stop_grad(prior))), preventing the posterior from collapsing. (2) Free bits — KL terms below a threshold (1 bit per dim) are not penalized, preventing the model from wasting capacity on trivial structure. (3) Symlog transformation — symmetric log scaling of inputs and targets compresses extreme reward values and stabilizes training across reward scales. (4) Lambda returns — the lambda-return mixes n-step and Monte Carlo returns, providing a bias-variance tradeoff. (5) Actor entropy regularizer — an entropy bonus discourages premature policy collapse. (6) World model reconstruction loss provides implicit regularization by requiring the latent state to be predictive of the next observation, preventing the dynamics model from memorizing noise.

- **Known Failure Modes On Small Data:**
  > Several failure modes are specifically relevant for n < 200: (1) World model hallucination — with very few training draws, the RSSM learns a poor dynamics model that generates unrealistic imagined sequences; the Actor-Critic then optimizes for these hallucinated dynamics rather than the true distribution, leading to plays that look good in imagination but perform no better than random in reality. (2) KL collapse — with insufficient data, the RSSM posterior may collapse to the prior, meaning the world model ignores observations and imagines random sequences regardless of the actual draw history. (3) Compounding model errors — even a small per-step world model error (5-10%) compounds exponentially over long imagined rollouts; for k=7 autoregressive steps, this can render imagined plays completely unreliable. (4) Overfitting of the world model — the RSSM can memorize the 100-200 draws without generalizing, acting as a lookup table rather than a generative model. (5) Mode collapse in imagination — the world model may generate a narrow set of imagined futures, causing the Actor-Critic to converge on a single dominant play set with no diversity.

- **Reward Distribution Handling:**
  > DreamerV3 has the strongest reward distribution handling of any standard RL algorithm, specifically designed for reward scale robustness: (1) Symlog reward transformation — all rewards are passed through symlog(x) = sign(x) * ln(|x| + 1) before training, compressing extreme values and normalizing the 95% zero-reward / 5% positive-reward lottery distribution without manual scaling. (2) Two-hot encoding of returns — the critic models returns as a two-hot distribution over a fixed set of return buckets rather than a scalar, which is equivalent to distributional RL (analogous to C51) and captures the full return distribution including rare high-reward events. (3) Lambda returns with symlog targets — the return target uses lambda=0.95 mixing, providing stable multi-step return estimates. (4) Free bits KL — prevents the world model from collapsing on zero-reward sequences (which dominate a lottery dataset). For lottery sparse rewards (95% zero hits), symlog effectively shrinks the zero-reward mass and amplifies the gradient signal from the rare non-zero draws. Minimum draws for stable world model training: empirically estimated at 500+ real draws for DreamerV3 to learn a reliable dynamics model; 100-200 draws may yield an unstable world model that limits downstream Actor-Critic quality.

- **Credit Assignment Granularity:**
  > DreamerV3 operates at the episode/trajectory level for credit assignment — the imagined rollout assigns returns to the actor based on cumulative imagined reward. In the lottery context, each draw constitutes a single timestep with one scalar reward (play-level credit). Number-level (semi-bandit) credit assignment is not natively supported but can be implemented by: (1) decomposing the reward into per-number hit signals (k separate binary rewards, one per ball) and training k separate reward models inside the world model; (2) using a shaped reward function (hits / k) that remains play-level but encodes partial information. The world model's reward predictor can learn to predict shaped hit-count rewards (0-7/7) from latent states, which provides richer gradient signal than binary win/loss during imagination. For lottery-nn, using shaped reward = num_hits / k is strongly recommended to make the world model's reward predictor learnable from the sparse hit distribution.

- **Diversity Mechanism:**
  > DreamerV3 maintains play diversity through: (1) Actor entropy regularizer — an entropy term is added to the actor's imagined objective, penalizing deterministic policies and encouraging exploration of the number space. (2) Stochastic world model — the RSSM's stochastic latent state (Categorical distribution per step) introduces randomness into imagined trajectories, naturally generating diverse imagined histories that train the actor on varied scenarios. (3) Temperature sampling at inference — actor logits can be scaled by temperature T > 1 to increase sampling diversity when generating the final play recommendations. (4) World model imagination diversity — because the world model samples from a learned stochastic dynamics, different imagination rollouts diverge even from the same starting state, exposing the actor to varied imagined reward signals. However, on very small datasets the world model's stochastic component may collapse (KL collapse), reducing effective diversity to near zero — monitoring the KL term magnitude during training is essential.

- **Offline Vs Online Rl Compatibility:**
  > DreamerV3 is uniquely well-suited for offline RL compared to model-free methods, because training occurs inside the world model imagination rather than requiring real environment interaction. The workflow for lottery-nn: (1) Train the RSSM world model offline on the fixed historical draw dataset (100-200 draws from draws.csv / draws_649.csv / draws_dailygrand.csv). (2) Train the Actor-Critic entirely inside the world model by generating imagined draw sequences — no new real draws are needed during Actor-Critic training. (3) After each new real draw, fine-tune the world model with the new data point and optionally re-run Actor-Critic imagination training. This is essentially a structured offline model-based RL workflow, analogous to MBPO (offline variant) or MOPO. The critical caveat is world model accuracy: an inaccurate world model trained on 100-200 draws may generate misleading imagined data, making the Actor-Critic worse than a simpler baseline. Offline model-based RL is operationally ideal (one-draw-per-week cadence) but technically risky at this data scale.

- **Complexity:** high
- **Key Dependencies:**
  > PyTorch (already present). No standard DreamerV3 library is pip-installable for custom environments — the RSSM must be implemented from scratch or adapted from open-source DreamerV3 PyTorch ports. Key components: einops (tensor reshaping for RSSM), torch.distributions (Categorical, Normal for latent distributions). Optional: dreamer-pytorch (community port), mbrl-lib (Meta's MBRL library with world model components). No stable pip package for DreamerV3 as of 2025 — requires ~800-1200 lines of custom implementation.

- **Estimated Lines Of Code:**
  > 800-1200 lines of new code: RSSM encoder/decoder (~200 lines), GRU-based dynamics model (~150 lines), stochastic latent state handling with straight-through (~100 lines), world model training loop with KL balancing, symlog, free bits (~150 lines), imagination rollout engine (~100 lines), Actor-Critic training inside imagination (~150 lines), combinatorial action adapter (autoregressive masking or top-k, ~100 lines), integration with existing SharedLotteryTransformer encoder (~80 lines, optional). This is the highest implementation complexity of any method in this research set.

- **Transfer Learning Compatibility:**
  > Moderate compatibility with adaptation. The existing SharedLotteryTransformer encoder can serve as the RSSM observation encoder — its output embeddings replace the CNN/MLP encoder used in standard DreamerV3. Specifically: the transformer's sequence output (after processing the last SEQUENCE_LEN draws) can feed into the RSSM as the observation representation, allowing the world model to build on the pretrained feature extractor. However, the RSSM's recurrent GRU hidden state introduces a separate temporal dynamics layer that partially overlaps with the transformer's own sequence modeling, requiring careful design to avoid redundancy. The lottery_id embedding already differentiates the three lotteries, which can be passed as a conditioning signal into the RSSM dynamics. The Actor and Critic heads are new additions on top of the latent state. Recommendation: freeze the SharedLotteryTransformer encoder for world model training and only train the RSSM dynamics and decoder from scratch, using the encoder's output as fixed observation features.

- **Relevance Score:** 3
- **Specific Application In Project:**
  > DreamerV3-style model-based RL would be applied in Phase 3 of lottery-nn, after the Phase 2 Actor-Critic baseline (REINFORCE/PPO) is established and validated. Concrete application: (1) Train an RSSM world model on each lottery's historical draw CSV (draws.csv, draws_649.csv, draws_dailygrand.csv), using the SharedLotteryTransformer encoder as the observation encoder and the lottery_id embedding for multi-lottery conditioning. (2) Generate thousands of imagined draw sequences from the RSSM, creating a synthetic augmented dataset that amplifies the 100-200 real draws. (3) Train a per-lottery Actor-Critic inside the imagination: actor outputs multi-hot logits over the ball pool (N=50 for LottoMax, N=49 for 649/DailyGrand), enforced to exactly k selections via straight-through top-k; critic estimates imagined returns using symlog and two-hot encoding. (4) At each real weekly draw: append new draw to CSV, fine-tune the world model with the new transition, re-run Actor-Critic imagination training, and generate 5 plays per lottery. (5) Reward shaping: reward = num_hits / k (range 0-1) to make the sparse lottery reward learnable inside the world model. This replaces the current temperature-softmax predict.py sampling with an Actor-Critic policy trained on imagined futures. Primary risk: world model accuracy at n=100-200; recommend validating world model prediction quality (reconstruction loss, predicted hit rate vs. actual) before committing to Phase 3 integration. Phase 2 REINFORCE baseline should be compared against the imagination-trained Actor-Critic to verify that the added complexity yields measurable improvement.

- **Integration Point:**
  > Phase 3 (probabilistic models, DeepAR, N-BEATS) — specifically as a model-based RL augmentation that uses imagination to overcome the sample scarcity that limits Phase 2 model-free Actor-Critic methods

- **Replaces Or Extends:**
  > Replaces temperature-softmax sampling in src/predict.py and extends the Phase 2 Actor-Critic by adding a world model (RSSM) layer between the SharedLotteryTransformer encoder and the Actor-Critic heads. The RSSM is a new component (src/world_model.py); the Actor and Critic heads from Phase 2 are reused but trained inside imagination rather than on real draw data directly.

- **Existing Open Source Implementations:**
  > 1. danijar/dreamerv3 (https://github.com/danijar/dreamerv3) — official JAX/TensorFlow implementation by Hafner; not PyTorch-native but is the canonical reference. 2. NM512/dreamer-pytorch (https://github.com/NM512/dreamer-pytorch) — community PyTorch port of DreamerV3, reasonably complete, MIT license; most directly usable for lottery-nn integration. 3. zhaoyi11/dreamer-pytorch (https://github.com/zhaoyi11/dreamer-pytorch) — another PyTorch port with cleaner RSSM implementation. 4. facebook/mbrl-lib (https://github.com/facebookresearch/mbrl-lib) — Meta's model-based RL library with PETS, MBPO components (not DreamerV3 specifically) but provides world model primitives usable for custom RSSM. 5. google-deepmind/acme (https://github.com/google-deepmind/acme) — DeepMind's RL framework includes DreamerV2-style agents in JAX; not PyTorch. 6. No lottery-specific or combinatorial-output DreamerV3 implementation exists as of 2025 — adaptation from gym-based implementations is required.

- **Benchmark Results:**
  > DreamerV3 (Hafner et al. 2023) key empirical results: (1) Atari 100K benchmark: DreamerV3 achieves human-normalized mean score of 1.14 with only 100K environment steps (vs. 40M for model-free PPO to reach human level) — 400x sample advantage on standard discrete action games. (2) DM Control (continuous control): DreamerV3 matches or exceeds SAC performance using 10-50x fewer environment interactions. (3) Crafter (survival game with sparse rewards): DreamerV3 achieves score 14.5% (vs. 5.3% PPO, 6.5% DreamerV2) on a task with dozens of sparse achievement signals — demonstrates robustness to reward sparsity. (4) Minecraft (open-world, extremely sparse): DreamerV3 is the first RL algorithm to obtain diamonds from scratch in Minecraft without human demonstrations — the most compelling sparse-reward result. (5) Multi-domain: A single set of hyperparameters (no tuning) works across all 7 benchmark suites, demonstrating robustness. No published DreamerV3 benchmark on combinatorial set selection (k-of-N) or offline static datasets of < 200 samples exists as of 2025.

- **Notable Use Cases:**
  > 1. Minecraft diamond collection (Hafner et al. 2023) — extremely sparse reward (reward occurs only after 20+ sequential correct actions), structurally similar to lottery in terms of reward rarity and the need for long-horizon credit assignment. 2. Drug discovery / molecular generation — RSSM-style world models have been applied to molecule optimization with combinatorial action spaces (adding/removing atoms), providing the closest published analog to k-of-N selection with sparse reward (binding affinity measured only after full molecule is constructed). 3. Chip placement optimization (Mirhoseini et al. 2021, Nature) — combinatorial placement of circuit components using RL with imagined rollouts; demonstrates model-based RL scaling to combinatorial layout problems. 4. Offline robotics (MOPO, Kidambi et al. 2020) — world model trained on fixed offline dataset (no new environment interaction), policy optimized inside the model; most structurally analogous to the lottery-nn offline use case where real draws are fixed. 5. Game state prediction for card games (Poker, Skat) — RSSM-style models for predicting hidden state from partial observations in card games with discrete combinatorial deal distributions — moderate structural similarity to draw prediction.

- convergence_guarantees_small_n
