### Multi-objective RL for Coverage and Hit-Rate Tradeoff (Pareto-Conditioned Networks + MORL-Baselines) {#multi-objective-rl-for-coverage-and-hit-rate-tradeoff-pareto-conditioned-networks-morl-baselines}

**Type:** RL Algorithm | **Relevance:** 3/5 | **Integration:** Phase 2 (Actor-Critic play generation)

**General**

- **Year:** 2023
- **Key Paper:**
  > Felten, F., Alegre, L. N., Nowe, A., Bazzan, A. L. C., Talbi, E.-G., Danoy, G., & da Silva, B. C. (2023). A Practical Guide to Multi-Objective Reinforcement Learning and Planning. Autonomous Agents and Multi-Agent Systems (JAAMAS), 37(1), 1-59. Also: Reymond, M., & Nowe, A. (2019). Pareto-Conditioned Networks. AAAI Workshop on Reinforcement Learning in Games.

- **Algorithm Description:**
  > Multi-objective RL (MORL) replaces the single scalar reward with a vector reward r = [r1, r2, ..., rK] where each component captures a distinct objective — in the lottery context, r1 = expected hit count (exploitation) and r2 = play diversity or coverage (exploration). Instead of collapsing these into a weighted sum, MORL algorithms learn a Pareto front of non-dominated policies, each representing a different tradeoff between objectives. Pareto-Conditioned Networks (PCN) extend this by training a single neural network conditioned on a desired Pareto point (preference vector w) at inference time, allowing a single model to express the full front of tradeoff policies without retraining. MORL-Baselines (Felten et al. 2023) provides a unified library implementing Envelope Q-learning, PGMORL, GPI-LS, CAPQL, and PCN under a common API. The agent learns to optimize multiple objectives simultaneously, producing a set of solutions on the Pareto front rather than a single policy — the user selects the operating point (e.g., maximize hits while maintaining coverage >= threshold) at deployment time.

- **Action Space Handling:**
  > MORL algorithms handle the action space identically to their single-objective counterparts — the action space itself is unchanged; only the reward signal becomes a vector. For combinatorial lottery play generation, the MORL policy outputs N logits over the ball pool (N=50 for LottoMax, N=49 for 6/49) and samples k distinct numbers via autoregressive masking or Gumbel-top-k. The multi-objective component operates entirely in the reward space and in how the critic (value function) is defined — the critic estimates a vector of Q-values Q(s,a) = [Q1(s,a), Q2(s,a)] for each objective, and the policy is optimized against a scalarized or envelope-based combination of these vector Q-values. PCN conditions the actor on the preference vector w by concatenating it with the state embedding before the policy head, effectively giving the actor explicit control over the hit-diversity tradeoff at inference time.

- **How Combinatorial Output Is Formed:**
  > The combinatorial k-of-N output mechanism is decoupled from the multi-objective reward structure. Within MORL, play generation uses the same techniques as standard discrete RL: (1) autoregressive masking — at each of k steps, the policy outputs logits over {1..N}, previously selected numbers are masked to -inf, and one number is sampled; repeated k times to form a complete play; (2) Gumbel-top-k — a joint differentiable sampling of exactly k items without replacement in one pass; (3) flat multi-hot with post-hoc top-k enforcement. For multi-play diversity (the second MORL objective), multiple plays are sampled independently from the same conditional policy, then a diversity reward is computed as the average pairwise Hamming distance or set-cover metric across the batch of plays. The preference weight w applied to the critic controls how aggressively the policy trades off between concentrating on high-probability numbers (hitting) and spreading across underrepresented numbers (coverage).

- **Sequential Generation Strategy:**
  > MORL does not prescribe a specific generation strategy — it is agnostic to whether actions are generated jointly or autoregressively. For the lottery application: (1) Joint (flat multi-hot): all k numbers are determined simultaneously from N logits; fast but cannot model correlations between numbers within a play. (2) Autoregressive: k steps, each conditioned on previous selections via masking; models within-play correlations and guarantees distinct numbers. (3) Play-level generation with diversity as a second objective: each play is generated autoregressively, then diversity is evaluated across the batch of plays, providing a cross-play reward signal that complements the within-play hit-count objective. The MORL framework naturally supports multi-play batch reward computation because the vector reward can include a batch-level diversity term computed after all plays in a ticket are generated.

- **Gradient Estimator Type:**
  > Gradient estimation in MORL depends on the underlying algorithm: (1) PCN uses a regression-based supervised loss — the network predicts which Pareto front point corresponds to the desired (return, preference) target, trained via mean-squared error on the return vector, avoiding REINFORCE variance entirely. (2) Envelope Q-learning (Envelope DQN, Yang et al. 2019) extends DQN with a vector Q-function, using Bellman updates with vectorized targets — no policy gradient, TD-error based. (3) PGMORL and GPI-LS use multi-objective policy gradient, which is a weighted REINFORCE estimator where the weight vector w selects the scalarization; variance is reduced via baselines analogous to standard actor-critic. (4) CAPQL (Continuous Action Pareto Q-learning) uses SAC-style reparameterization for continuous actions. For the discrete lottery setting, PCN with MSE regression or Envelope Q-learning are the most applicable, both avoiding the high-variance REINFORCE estimator.

- **Combinatorial Constraint Enforcement:**
  > Constraint enforcement (exactly k distinct numbers from {1..N}) is handled identically to single-objective RL — the multi-objective reward structure does not add new constraint mechanisms. Recommended approaches for lottery-nn: (1) Autoregressive masking — mask logits of already-selected numbers at each of k sampling steps; guarantees distinct selection with zero constraint violation; compatible with all MORL algorithms. (2) Gumbel-top-k — sample k items jointly without replacement using the Gumbel-max trick; differentiable end-to-end, supports backpropagation through the sampling step. (3) Post-hoc filtering — sample from N Bernoulli heads, keep top-k by probability; simple but biased. For PCN specifically, the autoregressive masking approach is preferred because the network is conditioned on the preference vector w at each step, allowing the preference to influence which numbers are selected at each autoregressive position rather than only at the final output.

- **Sample Efficiency Rating:**
  > low-to-medium. MORL algorithms generally inherit the sample efficiency of their single-objective base (e.g., PCN is based on offline regression, Envelope Q-learning inherits DQN's replay buffer). The multi-objective component adds overhead: (1) the critic must estimate a vector of values rather than a scalar, increasing model capacity needed; (2) diverse Pareto front coverage requires sampling from multiple preference vectors during training, effectively multiplying the number of distinct training scenarios. For n < 200 draws: PCN is the most sample-efficient MORL approach because it frames the problem as supervised regression on a dataset of (state, return-vector, preference) triples — with 100-200 historical draws, this dataset is small but usable. Standard MORL-RL algorithms (PGMORL, Envelope DQN) require online interaction or large offline replay buffers and perform poorly at n < 200. Rating: low for online MORL methods; medium for PCN used offline as a conditioned regression model.

- **Regularization Built In:**
  > MORL methods inherit regularization from their base algorithm plus add MORL-specific mechanisms: (1) Preference conditioning as implicit regularization — conditioning on a preference vector w forces the policy to generalize across the Pareto front rather than memorizing a single tradeoff, acting as a form of data augmentation that improves generalization on small datasets. (2) PCN regression loss — MSE on return predictions is a strongly regularized objective compared to policy gradient methods; the regression framing naturally prevents the divergence typical of actor-critic on small data. (3) Diversity objective as regularizer — the coverage/diversity reward (second objective) explicitly penalizes mode collapse toward a single play, providing the equivalent of an entropy bonus but with a more semantically meaningful objective. (4) Pareto front coverage — training over uniformly sampled preference vectors ensures the model does not overfit to any single weighting of objectives. (5) Standard regularization from base algorithm: weight decay, dropout, entropy bonus (for policy gradient variants).

- **Known Failure Modes On Small Data:**
  > Documented failure modes for MORL on n < 200: (1) Pareto front collapse — with insufficient data, all preference vectors w converge to the same policy because the value network cannot distinguish between Pareto-optimal tradeoffs; the front degenerates to a single point. (2) Reward vector estimation noise — estimating Q-values for multiple objectives simultaneously from sparse rewards amplifies noise; with 95% zero-hit draws, both the hit-count Q-value and the diversity Q-value are noisy, causing unstable training. (3) Preference overfitting — with few training samples, the preference-conditioned network may memorize which preference vector saw which draw outcomes, failing to generalize to novel preference values at inference. (4) Incoherent Pareto fronts — the trained Pareto front may be non-convex or contain dominated solutions due to approximation errors from small data, making the preference-to-policy mapping unreliable. (5) Diversity reward instability — the diversity reward (e.g., Hamming distance between plays) has high variance when plays are sampled from a policy that is still converging, creating a noisy second reward signal that compounds instability.

- **Reward Distribution Handling:**
  > MORL addresses reward distribution challenges differently per objective: (1) Hit-count objective (r1): inherits the sparsity problem of standard RL — ~95% of plays yield r1=0, causing Q1(s,a) estimates to concentrate near zero with high variance. Reward shaping (r1 = hits/k, range 0.0-1.0) helps; distributional extensions (IQN-style vector Q-function) can model the full return distribution per objective. (2) Diversity objective (r2): the coverage/diversity reward can be computed deterministically from the set of generated plays (average pairwise Hamming distance, entropy of number frequency), giving a dense, low-variance reward signal that stabilizes training even when r1 is sparse. This is a key advantage of MORL over single-objective RL: r2 provides a learning signal on every update even when r1=0. (3) Minimum draws needed for stable MORL: approximately 200-500 for simple PCN-style regression; 1000+ for policy-gradient MORL (PGMORL, Envelope DQN). (4) The two-objective formulation (hit-count + diversity) partially solves the sparsity problem because diversity reward is dense.

- **Credit Assignment Granularity:**
  > MORL does not inherently change credit assignment granularity — it depends on the base algorithm. For the lottery application with MORL: (1) Hit-count objective (r1): best implemented with number-level (semi-bandit) credit — each number in a play that matches the actual draw contributes +1/k to r1, providing k independent learning signals per draw rather than one. This is straightforward to implement with play-level MORL. (2) Diversity objective (r2): naturally operates at play-batch level — r2 is computed across all plays in a ticket (e.g., 3 plays per ticket), measuring how well the set of plays covers different regions of the number space. This batch-level credit is unique to MORL and not available in single-objective RL without explicit diversity reward. (3) Combining both: PCN receives the vector reward [r1, r2] per ticket, allowing the network to learn both number-level hit patterns and batch-level diversity patterns from the same 100-200 historical draws.

- **Diversity Mechanism:**
  > Diversity is a first-class objective in MORL rather than a regularization afterthought: (1) Explicit diversity reward (r2) — computed as the mean pairwise Hamming distance between plays in a ticket (e.g., 3 plays of k=7 numbers): higher distance = more diverse coverage. This makes diversity directly optimizable and measurable. (2) Pareto front diversity — by training over the full range of preference vectors w = [w1, w2] with w1 + w2 = 1, the PCN learns policies ranging from pure hit-maximization (w = [1, 0]) to pure diversity-maximization (w = [0, 1]), with all intermediate tradeoffs available at inference by selecting w. (3) Set-cover metric as r2 — alternatively, r2 can be defined as the fraction of the ball pool {1..N} covered by at least one play in the ticket, incentivizing plays to collectively cover different number regions. (4) Population-based diversity (PGMORL) — maintains multiple policies simultaneously, each optimized for a different Pareto weight, with explicit population diversity maintained via evolutionary selection. The explicit diversity objective eliminates the need for entropy coefficient tuning, which is a major practical advantage over single-objective methods.

- **Offline Vs Online Rl Compatibility:**
  > MORL algorithms vary widely in offline compatibility: (1) PCN (best for lottery-nn) — can be trained fully offline as a supervised regression problem on historical draws; requires only a static dataset of (draw_history, preference_vector, achievable_returns) triples. Offline compatible with no environment interaction. (2) Envelope Q-learning — offline compatible with a replay buffer, analogous to offline DQN; requires careful handling of distributional shift when the behavior policy (historical plays) differs from the learned policy. (3) PGMORL — fundamentally online; requires iterative policy rollouts to evolve the population of Pareto-optimal policies. Not directly applicable to weekly draw cycle without extensive synthetic augmentation. (4) GPI-LS (Generalized Policy Improvement with Linear Scalarization) — can operate offline over a fixed set of policy evaluations, making it partially compatible. Verdict: PCN is the only MORL-Baselines algorithm that is straightforwardly applicable to the offline, fixed-dataset (100-200 draws) lottery setting. The combination of offline compatibility and preference conditioning makes PCN the recommended MORL approach for lottery-nn Phase 2.

- **Complexity:** high
- **Key Dependencies:**
  > morl-baselines (pip install morl-baselines, https://github.com/LucasAlegre/morl-baselines), PyTorch (already present), numpy, gymnasium (for environment interface), scipy (for Pareto front computation and hypervolume indicator). PCN specifically requires only PyTorch and numpy — it is the lightest MORL-Baselines algorithm. Full MORL-Baselines install pulls in stable-baselines3 and wandb as optional dependencies.

- **Estimated Lines Of Code:**
  > 400-600 lines of new code for PCN integration: preference-conditioned actor head (60 lines), vector critic with 2-objective Q-function (80 lines), diversity reward computation across plays (50 lines), Pareto front sampling and preference vector scheduler (80 lines), offline PCN training loop with historical draw replay (120 lines), multi-objective evaluation and hypervolume logging (80 lines), inference-time preference selection and play generation (60 lines). Excludes MORL-Baselines library code itself. Full PGMORL integration would require 800-1000 additional lines for population management.

- **Transfer Learning Compatibility:**
  > High compatibility for PCN; medium for other MORL algorithms. The existing SharedLotteryTransformer encoder is ideal as the backbone: (1) PCN adds a preference vector concatenation layer before the actor head — the encoder output (embedding) is concatenated with the 2D preference vector w and passed to the actor and critic heads. Only the heads require new parameters; the pretrained encoder is reused intact. (2) The lottery_id embedding already handles per-lottery differentiation, so MORL per-lottery objective definitions (e.g., LottoMax has 7-number plays so diversity reward is defined differently from 5-number Daily Grand) are naturally handled. (3) The pretrained backbone provides a strong feature initialization that reduces the data needed for MORL regression training, which is critical at n < 200. (4) For PGMORL: the population of policies can all share the pretrained encoder backbone, with separate lightweight actor heads per policy, reducing total parameter count and training instability.

- **Relevance Score:** 3
- **Specific Application In Project:**
  > MORL would be used in Phase 2 to solve the specific tension between two conflicting goals in lottery play generation: (1) maximizing expected hits (concentrate plays on highest-frequency number subsets) and (2) maximizing coverage (spread plays across underrepresented numbers to hedge against model uncertainty). Concrete implementation: (a) Define r1 = number of hits per play (0-7 for LottoMax), normalized to [0,1]. (b) Define r2 = mean pairwise Hamming distance across the 3 plays in a ticket (coverage metric), normalized to [0,1]. (c) Train a preference-conditioned PCN actor that takes [encoder_output | preference_vector_w] and generates plays whose expected (r1, r2) tradeoff matches w. (d) At inference time, expose a --coverage parameter (0.0=pure hit focus, 1.0=pure diversity) that sets w and generates tickets along the Pareto front. (e) Use MORL-Baselines GPI-LS to identify the knee point of the Pareto front (best single operating point balancing both objectives) as the default. This replaces the current temperature parameter in src/predict.py with a semantically meaningful coverage-vs-hits knob. All three lotteries share the encoder; per-lottery Pareto fronts differ only in head dimensions and play size. Phase 2 timeline: implement after basic Actor-Critic (REINFORCE-with-baseline) is working.

- **Integration Point:** Phase 2 (Actor-Critic play generation)
- **Replaces Or Extends:**
  > Extends existing temperature-softmax sampling in src/predict.py — the MORL preference vector w replaces the single temperature parameter, decomposing the implicit diversity-vs-exploitation tradeoff into an explicit two-objective optimization. The SharedLotteryTransformer backbone is extended with a preference-conditioned actor head and a 2-objective critic head; the encoder itself is unchanged.

- **Existing Open Source Implementations:**
  > 1. MORL-Baselines (https://github.com/LucasAlegre/morl-baselines) — official implementation by Felten et al. 2023, includes PCN, GPI-LS, Envelope Q-learning, PGMORL, CAPQL under a unified Gymnasium-compatible API; pip install morl-baselines; actively maintained as of 2025. 2. PCN reference implementation (https://github.com/mathieu-reymond/pareto-conditioned-networks) — Reymond & Nowe original PCN code, simpler but less integrated. 3. pymoo (https://pymoo.org) — not RL-specific but provides Pareto front computation utilities (NSGA-II, hypervolume indicator) used for evaluating MORL policies. 4. multi-objective-rl (https://github.com/RunzheYang/MORL) — Yang et al. 2019 Envelope Q-learning for MORL, PyTorch-based. 5. No lottery-specific MORL reference implementation exists; the combinatorial action adaptation (autoregressive masking for k-of-N) must be implemented from scratch on top of any of the above.

- **Benchmark Results:**
  > MORL-Baselines (Felten et al. 2023) benchmarks: (1) MO-MuJoCo (continuous control, 2-4 objectives): PCN achieves 85-95% of the hypervolume of the true Pareto front on MO-HalfCheetah and MO-Hopper with 1M environment steps; GPI-LS outperforms PGMORL on most tasks. (2) Deep Sea Treasure (discrete action, sparse reward): PCN achieves near-optimal Pareto front coverage with 50K steps; Envelope Q-learning converges but is slower. (3) MO-Reacher (continuous, 2 objectives): GPI-LS achieves the highest hypervolume indicator (0.91 vs 0.78 for PGMORL). (4) All benchmarks use online interaction with 50K-1M environment steps — no small offline dataset benchmarks. (5) Pareto front quality (hypervolume indicator): PCN typically achieves 80-95% of optimal hypervolume on 2-objective tasks; degrades to 60-70% on 3-4 objectives. (6) No direct combinatorial discrete set-output benchmarks exist in the MORL-Baselines paper.

- **Notable Use Cases:**
  > 1. Multi-objective recommendation systems — slate generation (select k items from N candidates) with competing objectives of relevance and diversity; structurally identical to lottery play generation (k-of-N, diversity vs. hit rate). Industrial recommendation at YouTube, Netflix uses MORL-style preference weighting to balance click-through rate vs. watch-time vs. content diversity. 2. Portfolio optimization — select k assets from N candidates to maximize expected return while minimizing variance; combinatorial structure with two competing objectives directly analogous to lottery coverage-vs-hits. 3. Drug discovery — select k molecular fragments from a pool to maximize binding affinity while maintaining structural diversity; sparse reward, combinatorial action space, small datasets (high structural similarity to lottery application). 4. Multi-objective combinatorial optimization (MOCO) — MORL applied to Traveling Salesman with time and distance objectives; autoregressive pointer networks with preference conditioning; same architecture pattern applicable to lottery play generation. 5. Clinical trial design — select k patients for treatment from N candidates, balancing efficacy (hit rate analog) and population coverage (diversity analog); small dataset regime (100-500 patients) with direct methodological parallels.

### N-BEATS: Neural Basis Expansion Analysis for Interpretable Time Series Forecasting {#n-beats-neural-basis-expansion-analysis-for-interpretable-time-series-forecasting}

**Type:** Architecture | **Relevance:** 3/5 | **Integration:** Phase 3 (probabilistic models, DeepAR, N-BEATS) / Backbone (replace SharedLotteryTransformer)

**General**

- **Year:** 2020
- **Key Paper:** Oreshkin, B. N., Carpov, D., Chapados, N., & Bengio, Y. (2020). N-BEATS: Neural Basis Expansion Analysis for Interpretable Time Series Forecasting. ICLR 2020 (Spotlight). arXiv:1905.10437.
- **Algorithm Description:**
  > N-BEATS is a pure deep-learning architecture for univariate time series forecasting that requires no feature engineering, no exogenous variables, and no domain-specific components. Its core building block is a stack of fully connected residual blocks organized into two doubly-residual stacks: a Trend stack and a Seasonality stack (interpretable variant) or generic polynomial basis stacks (generic variant). Each block receives a backcast window of historical values, produces a backcast (reconstruction of the input window) and a forecast (prediction of future values) by expanding learned basis functions. The backcast is subtracted from the block input before passing to the next block, implementing a hierarchical decomposition of the time series. This doubly-residual design allows very deep networks (30+ layers) to train stably without vanishing gradients. The architecture is entirely MLP-based — no recurrence, no attention, no convolutions — making it fast, parallelizable, and interpretable through its basis expansion structure.

- **Action Space Handling:**
  > N-BEATS is not an RL algorithm and does not interact with an action space. It is a supervised time series forecasting architecture that maps a fixed-length historical window (backcast) to a fixed-length forecast horizon. In the lottery-nn context, it would replace the SharedLotteryTransformer as the backbone encoder that maps historical draw sequences to a latent representation or directly to output logits over ball numbers. There is no sampling policy, importance ratio, or exploration-exploitation tradeoff built into N-BEATS itself — those concerns belong to a downstream actor layer that consumes its output.

- **Sample Efficiency Rating:**
  > medium — N-BEATS was designed for short time series with limited history and was shown to outperform statistical methods (ETS, ARIMA, Theta) on M4 competition data which includes series with as few as 42 observations for yearly data and 60+ for quarterly. The M4 dataset is the primary evidence base for N-BEATS performance on short series. Compared to transformer-based models (like the existing SharedLotteryTransformer), N-BEATS has fewer parameters per layer and its doubly-residual structure provides strong inductive bias (trend and seasonality decomposition) that acts as implicit regularization, reducing the number of samples needed to learn. However, N-BEATS was validated on proper time series (continuous-valued, smooth), not on discrete combinatorial draws where the signal-to-noise ratio is near zero. On truly random lottery draws, no architecture (N-BEATS included) can extract predictive signal — the sample efficiency advantage disappears when the underlying process has no structure to exploit.

- **Regularization Built In:**
  > N-BEATS has several structural regularization mechanisms: (1) Doubly-residual connections — each block can only modify the residual remaining after previous blocks have been accounted for, limiting each block's capacity and preventing co-adaptation. (2) Basis expansion constraint — the interpretable variant forces the forecast to be a linear combination of polynomial trend basis functions or Fourier seasonality basis functions, strongly limiting the function class and acting as a hard structural prior. (3) Shared basis functions across blocks within a stack — weight sharing reduces effective parameter count. (4) The generic variant uses a learned (unconstrained) basis but still decomposes the series hierarchically, which implicitly regularizes by forcing each stack to explain only the residual. Standard dropout and weight decay can be added as additional regularizers. For lottery-nn's 100-200 draw dataset, the interpretable variant's basis constraints provide the most meaningful regularization against overfitting.

- **Known Failure Modes On Small Data:**
  > 1. Trend/seasonality basis mismatch: The interpretable N-BEATS variant assumes the time series decomposes into polynomial trends and Fourier seasonality — lottery draws are i.i.d. random, violating both assumptions. The basis expansion will fit spurious patterns rather than real signal. 2. Overfitting to backcast: with fewer than 200 draws and large MLP blocks (default 512 hidden units x 4 layers per block x 30+ blocks), N-BEATS has far more parameters than data points, requiring aggressive regularization or architectural reduction. 3. Backcast reconstruction dominates: N-BEATS jointly optimizes backcast (reconstruction) and forecast losses; on small datasets the model may allocate most capacity to perfect backcast reconstruction (memorization) at the expense of forecast quality. 4. Lack of cross-series learning: standard N-BEATS is univariate — each lottery's draw sequence is modeled independently, losing the shared encoder benefit that the current SharedLotteryTransformer provides across 3 lotteries. N-BEATS-X (multivariate variant) would be needed to retain cross-lottery feature sharing.

- **Credit Assignment Granularity:**
  > Not applicable to N-BEATS as a standalone architecture — it is supervised, not RL-based. N-BEATS produces ball-level output logits (one logit per possible ball number), which inherently provides ball-level granularity in its predictions. In a downstream Actor-Critic use, credit assignment would be determined by the RL algorithm consuming N-BEATS outputs, not by N-BEATS itself. If N-BEATS is trained with a per-ball binary cross-entropy loss (did ball i appear in this draw?), it implicitly operates at number-level granularity, which is the most informative level for lottery prediction. This is structurally equivalent to the semi-bandit feedback formulation and is preferable to play-level loss.

- **Diversity Mechanism:**
  > N-BEATS is a deterministic architecture — given the same input sequence, it always produces the same output. It has no built-in diversity mechanism. Diversity in play generation must be introduced by the downstream sampling layer: (1) temperature scaling of N-BEATS output logits before Gumbel-top-k sampling; (2) dropout at inference time (MC dropout), sampling multiple stochastic forward passes through N-BEATS to produce an ensemble of logit vectors, each yielding a different play; (3) noise injection into the N-BEATS input or intermediate layers; (4) training multiple independent N-BEATS heads (one per desired play), encouraging diversity via repulsion loss. Of these, MC dropout is the most natural — it adds diversity without architectural changes and provides uncertainty estimates for each ball's predicted probability.

- **Offline Vs Online Rl Compatibility:**
  > N-BEATS is a fully offline supervised learning architecture — it has no concept of online interaction, episodes, or environment stepping. It trains entirely from a fixed historical dataset (the draw CSV files), which is precisely the lottery-nn data setup. This makes it operationally compatible with the weekly-draw cycle: after each new draw, the draw is appended to the CSV, and N-BEATS is retrained or fine-tuned from the existing checkpoint. No online RL interaction is required. If N-BEATS is later embedded in an Actor-Critic system (Phase 3), the RL component's offline vs. online compatibility question applies to the RL wrapper, not to N-BEATS itself. As a pure backbone architecture, N-BEATS is fully offline-compatible and fits the existing `joint-train` workflow.

- **Complexity:**
  > medium — N-BEATS is architecturally simpler than the existing SharedLotteryTransformer (no attention, no positional encoding, pure MLP), but integrating it as a drop-in replacement requires adapting the multi-lottery shared encoder design, since standard N-BEATS is univariate. Adding lottery_id conditioning and per-lottery output heads analogous to the current swarm design is additional work.

- **Key Dependencies:**
  > PyTorch only for custom implementation. Production-quality implementations available in: `neuralforecast` (Nixtla, pip install neuralforecast) which provides N-BEATS and N-BEATS-X with PyTorch backend; `darts` (pip install darts) which includes N-BEATS with optional GPU support. No mandatory new dependencies beyond PyTorch if implementing from scratch (~300 lines). neuralforecast or darts would simplify integration at the cost of a new library dependency.

- **Estimated Lines Of Code:**
  > 300-450 lines for a from-scratch PyTorch implementation: N-BEATS Block class (~80 lines), Stack class (~40 lines), full N-BEATS model with configurable stacks (~100 lines), multi-lottery adaptation with lottery_id embedding (~60 lines), per-lottery output heads (~50 lines), integration with existing preprocessing_swarm.py and joint-train loop (~100 lines). If using neuralforecast/darts: ~150-200 lines of integration glue code only.

- **Relevance Score:** 3
- **Specific Application In Project:**
  > N-BEATS is a Phase 3 backbone candidate to replace or augment the SharedLotteryTransformer in `src/model_swarm.py`. Concrete application: (1) Replace `SharedLotteryTransformer` with an `NBEATSLotteryModel` that takes a sequence of multi-hot draw vectors (shape: seq_len x POOL_MAX) and outputs ball logits (shape: POOL_MAX) per lottery. The model uses a generic N-BEATS stack (not interpretable, since lottery draws lack trend/seasonality) with the backcast window set to SEQUENCE_LEN=20 draws. (2) Add a lottery_id embedding broadcast-added to the input, preserving the multi-lottery joint training paradigm. (3) Per-lottery output heads (same as current swarm): main_logits and bonus_logits with natural sizes (LottoMax: 50/50, 6/49: 49/49, Daily Grand: 49/7). (4) The joint-train loop in `main_swarm.py` remains unchanged; only `model_swarm.py` is swapped. (5) Evaluate N-BEATS backbone vs. SharedLotteryTransformer on validation hit rate to decide whether to keep it. Primary motivation: N-BEATS was designed for short series and has strong inductive bias; it may generalize better than the transformer on the 100-200 draw dataset. Primary risk: lottery draws have no temporal structure for N-BEATS to exploit, and the interpretable variant's trend/seasonality basis is inappropriate for i.i.d. data.

- **Integration Point:** Phase 3 (probabilistic models, DeepAR, N-BEATS) / Backbone (replace SharedLotteryTransformer)
- **Replaces Or Extends:**
  > Replaces — N-BEATS would replace the `SharedLotteryTransformer` in `src/model_swarm.py` as the sequence-to-logits backbone. The per-lottery output heads, lottery_id embedding, joint training loop, and checkpoint format would remain, but the transformer encoder blocks would be replaced by N-BEATS doubly-residual MLP stacks. Could alternatively extend as a parallel ensemble branch alongside the transformer.

- **Existing Open Source Implementations:**
  > 1. `neuralforecast` (Nixtla, https://github.com/Nixtla/neuralforecast) — production N-BEATS and N-BEATS-X in PyTorch with GPU support, pip installable, actively maintained as of 2025; most practical for integration. 2. `darts` (https://github.com/unit8co/darts) — includes N-BEATS with a scikit-learn-compatible API; less flexible for custom architectures but good for prototyping. 3. Original N-BEATS implementation (https://github.com/ServiceNow/N-BEATS) — reference PyTorch implementation from the paper authors (ServiceNow Research), covers both generic and interpretable variants. 4. `pytorch-forecasting` (https://github.com/jdb78/pytorch-forecasting) — N-BEATS included with Lightning training loop. 5. GluonTS (Amazon, https://github.com/awslabs/gluonts) — MXNet/PyTorch N-BEATS implementation. The ServiceNow reference and neuralforecast are the most useful starting points.

- **Notable Use Cases:**
  > 1. M4 time series competition (2018): N-BEATS won the pure ML track — 100K diverse time series including finance, economics, demographics; series as short as 13-42 observations for yearly/quarterly frequency. Most structurally relevant in terms of short series length. 2. Retail demand forecasting (Walmart M5, Kaggle): N-BEATS applied to hierarchical store-item sales forecasting with intermittent demand (sparse non-zero values) — somewhat analogous to sparse lottery signal. 3. Energy consumption forecasting: Short-horizon electricity load prediction where N-BEATS outperforms LSTM and transformer baselines on individual customer series (50-200 data points per series). 4. Financial return prediction: Experimental applications to stock return forecasting on daily data — structurally similar (low SNR, near-random series) to lottery draws; results show N-BEATS performs no better than naive baselines when autocorrelation is absent, which is a cautionary signal for lottery application. 5. Medical time series (patient vital signs): N-BEATS applied to ICU monitoring with short individual patient histories (~100 timesteps); shows advantage over LSTM at very short series lengths.

### PAC-Bayes Generalization Bounds for Small-Sample RL {#pac-bayes-generalization-bounds-for-small-sample-rl}

**Type:** Theory | **Relevance:** 3/5 | **Integration:** Phase 2 (Actor-Critic play generation)

**General**

- **Year:** 2022
- **Key Paper:**
  > Haddouche & Guedj, 'Online-to-PAC Conversions: Generalization Bounds via Regret Analysis', NeurIPS 2023. Also: Haddouche & Guedj, 'PAC-Bayes Generalisation Bounds for Heavy-Tailed Losses through Supermartingales', TMLR 2023. Foundational: McAllester, 'PAC-Bayesian model averaging', COLT 1999; Catoni, 'PAC-Bayesian Supervised Classification', IMS Lecture Notes 2007.

- **Algorithm Description:**
  > PAC-Bayes is a statistical learning framework that provides finite-sample generalization bounds for randomized (stochastic) predictors. Instead of bounding the risk of a single deterministic hypothesis, PAC-Bayes bounds the risk of a posterior distribution Q over hypotheses in terms of its KL divergence from a prior distribution P plus a complexity term that shrinks as 1/sqrt(n). The central inequality (McAllester-Catoni form) states: E_{h~Q}[L(h)] <= E_{h~Q}[L_train(h)] + sqrt( (KL(Q||P) + log(1/delta)) / (2n) ). In the RL setting, Haddouche & Guedj (2022-2023) extend this to policies: the expected return of a stochastic policy Q is bounded above by the empirical return on the training dataset plus a KL penalty term between Q and a prior policy P. This gives a principled justification for KL-regularized policy optimization (as in KL-PPO, SAC, TRPO) from a generalization perspective: the KL term is not just an algorithmic heuristic but a theoretically necessary regularizer to prevent overfitting of the policy to the training data. Haddouche & Guedj's 2023 work extends PAC-Bayes bounds to heavy-tailed losses via supermartingale arguments, and derives online-to-PAC conversions that apply when data arrives sequentially (as in the weekly-draw setting).

- **Action Space Handling:**
  > PAC-Bayes is a meta-framework, not a specific RL algorithm, so it does not itself handle action spaces. It bounds the generalization gap of any stochastic policy Q(a|s). For combinatorial action spaces like lottery (select k from N), the policy Q is any distribution over k-subsets of {1..N}, and the PAC-Bayes bound applies as long as the empirical return can be computed over the training dataset. The bound's tightness does not depend on action-space size, only on n (number of training trajectories) and KL(Q||P). This is a significant advantage over sample-complexity bounds that depend on |A| or |S|, which would be astronomically large for combinatorial lottery selection.

- **How Combinatorial Output Is Formed:**
  > PAC-Bayes theory makes no prescription on how the combinatorial output is formed — it bounds the generalization gap of whatever stochastic policy Q is used. In practice, for the lottery application, Q would be the stochastic actor (e.g., Gumbel-top-k, temperature-softmax top-k, or autoregressive sampling) and P would be a prior policy (e.g., uniform random selection, or the pretrained SharedLotteryTransformer checkpoint before fine-tuning). The PAC-Bayes bound then guarantees that the return achieved by Q on held-out draws will not be far worse than on training draws, provided KL(Q||P) is small. The bound motivates adding an explicit KL(Q||P) penalty term to the policy training objective, ensuring the fine-tuned policy stays close to the pretrained prior.

- **Sequential Generation Strategy:**
  > PAC-Bayes theory is agnostic to generation strategy. The bounds apply equally to joint flat multi-hot policies, autoregressive policies (where each ball is selected one at a time conditioned on previous selections), and flow-based policies. What matters is that Q is a well-defined distribution over plays (complete k-subsets) and that its KL divergence from P can be computed or approximated. For autoregressive policies, KL(Q||P) decomposes as a sum of per-step KL terms (chain rule of KL), which is computationally convenient and matches how autoregressive transformers compute their output distributions.

- **Gradient Estimator Type:**
  > PAC-Bayes is a theoretical framework, not an optimization algorithm with a specific gradient estimator. However, the PAC-Bayes objective (empirical return minus KL penalty) can be optimized using any policy gradient method. When optimizing a stochastic policy Q with discrete/combinatorial outputs, the gradient of the KL term KL(Q||P) = E_{Q}[log Q(a) - log P(a)] is analytically tractable if Q and P are in the same parametric family (e.g., both Gaussian, or both factored Bernoulli for multi-hot outputs). For the lottery transformer, the KL between the current policy's output logits and the prior policy's logits is computable in closed form if both are softmax distributions — no score-function estimator is needed for the KL term specifically. The policy gradient for the return term still requires REINFORCE or Gumbel-softmax.

- **Combinatorial Constraint Enforcement:**
  > PAC-Bayes theory does not enforce combinatorial constraints — constraint enforcement is delegated to the underlying policy architecture (same as with any RL method). The bound holds for any policy Q that produces valid k-distinct subsets. In practice, enforcement would use autoregressive masking (setting already-selected numbers to -inf before each step), Gumbel-top-k (which guarantees exactly k distinct samples by construction), or rejection sampling (sample until a valid distinct set is produced). The KL(Q||P) term in the PAC-Bayes objective naturally encodes how much the constrained policy Q diverges from the prior P, providing a measure of how much the constraint changes the distribution.

- **Sample Efficiency Rating:**
  > high — PAC-Bayes bounds are among the tightest finite-sample bounds available in statistical learning theory and are specifically designed to provide meaningful guarantees at small n. The bound E[L(h)] <= E_train[h] + sqrt(KL/2n) with KL=O(1) gives a generalization gap of O(1/sqrt(100)) ~ 0.1 for n=100 draws — meaningful but not tight. More practically, PAC-Bayes motivates KL-regularized fine-tuning of the pretrained prior, which is one of the most sample-efficient approaches to policy adaptation known: the prior encodes all pre-existing knowledge, and regularization prevents the small dataset from overcorrecting. This is the same principle behind RLHF fine-tuning of large language models with KL penalties.

- **Regularization Built In:**
  > The KL divergence penalty KL(Q||P) is the central, principled regularizer derived directly from PAC-Bayes theory. Minimizing the PAC-Bayes bound gives an objective of the form: maximize E_{h~Q}[R_train(h)] - (1/lambda) * KL(Q||P), where lambda controls the regularization strength. This is precisely the objective of maximum-entropy RL, SAC, and KL-regularized PPO — PAC-Bayes provides the theoretical justification for all of them. Additional regularization mechanisms compatible with PAC-Bayes include: (1) data-dependent priors (using a held-out subset of draws to fit P before computing the bound on the remainder); (2) aggregate posteriors (averaging over multiple policy snapshots); (3) Catoni's phi-function bound, which is more robust to heavy-tailed returns than the basic McAllester bound.

- **Known Failure Modes On Small Data:**
  > 1. Loose bounds at n=100: the sqrt(KL/2n) term gives a generalization gap of ~0.07-0.15 for typical KL values, which may be larger than the actual return signal (lottery rewards are in [0,1] when normalized). The bound is valid but not actionable for model selection. 2. Prior misspecification: if the prior P is poorly chosen (e.g., uniform random, far from the true lottery distribution), KL(Q||P) will be large for any reasonable Q, making the bound vacuous. The pretrained SharedLotteryTransformer checkpoint is a much better prior than uniform. 3. Sparse reward makes empirical return estimate noisy: E_train[R] computed over 100 draws with ~95% zero rewards has high variance, and the PAC-Bayes bound on this noisy estimate is also noisy. 4. The framework provides bounds on expected return, not on tail behavior — does not directly address the probability of winning (rare event), which is arguably more relevant for lottery. 5. Computing exact KL(Q||P) for autoregressive policies over combinatorial outputs requires summing over all possible orderings, which is intractable without approximation.

- **Convergence Guarantees Small N:**
  > PAC-Bayes provides explicit finite-sample bounds valid for any n >= 1 with probability at least 1-delta. The McAllester-Catoni bound gives: with probability >= 1-delta over the training set, E[R(Q)] >= E_train[R(Q)] - sqrt((KL(Q||P) + log(2*sqrt(n)/delta)) / (2n)). At n=100, delta=0.05, and KL=1 nat, the generalization gap is approximately sqrt((1 + log(20))/200) ~ 0.14 return units. This is a convergence guarantee in the sense that the true return is provably within 0.14 of the training return — useful for certifying that the policy has not overfit. Haddouche & Guedj's 2023 supermartingale-based bounds are tighter for heavy-tailed returns (relevant since lottery rewards are zero-inflated). Their online-to-PAC result implies that after observing n sequential draws, the cumulative regret bound converts to a generalization bound that tightens as n grows. These are among the strongest small-n guarantees available in the RL theory literature.

- **Reward Distribution Handling:**
  > PAC-Bayes theory in its basic form (McAllester bound) assumes bounded rewards in [0,1] and applies Hoeffding's inequality. Lottery rewards are bounded (0 to max prize) and zero-inflated (~95% zeros), which satisfies the bounded assumption but makes the empirical mean estimate high-variance. Haddouche & Guedj's 2023 TMLR paper on heavy-tailed losses is directly relevant: they derive PAC-Bayes bounds via supermartingales that are valid even when the reward distribution has heavy tails or high variance, without assuming subgaussian rewards. This is important because even bounded rewards can have near-Bernoulli distributions with variance close to 0.25. The framework does not natively support distributional return modeling (it bounds expected return), but can be extended to CVaR or other risk measures via distributionally robust PAC-Bayes (e.g., Catoni-type bounds with risk-sensitive loss functions). Minimum draws: valid at any n, but the bound becomes practically tight (gap < 0.05) only around n ~ 500-1000 for typical KL values.

- **Credit Assignment Granularity:**
  > PAC-Bayes theory is agnostic to credit assignment granularity. The bound applies at whatever granularity the reward R is defined: play-level (did the set win?), number-level (how many individual numbers hit?), or pattern-level. For the lottery application, applying PAC-Bayes with number-level semi-bandit rewards (reward_i = 1 if number i appeared in the draw) multiplies the effective sample size by approximately k=7, tightening the bound by a factor of sqrt(7) ~ 2.6. This is a concrete theoretical motivation for using semi-bandit credit assignment: not only does it provide more signal, but the PAC-Bayes bound on the resulting policy is provably tighter. The framework supports this naturally since R(Q) can be any function of the play and the draw outcome.

- **Diversity Mechanism:**
  > PAC-Bayes theory does not prescribe a diversity mechanism. However, the maximum-entropy interpretation of the PAC-Bayes objective (maximize return subject to KL(Q||P) <= budget) naturally produces stochastic policies with high entropy relative to the prior. If the prior P is a high-entropy distribution (e.g., the pretrained transformer with temperature T=1.2), the KL constraint encourages Q to also be high-entropy, which promotes diverse play generation. For generating multiple diverse plays per call, the stochastic policy Q can be sampled independently k times — the PAC-Bayes bound applies to the marginal distribution of each play. Explicit diversity rewards can be incorporated into the return function R without changing the PAC-Bayes framework.

- **Offline Vs Online Rl Compatibility:**
  > Fully compatible with offline RL. PAC-Bayes bounds apply to any setting where a fixed dataset of n training examples (draws) is available and the learner cannot generate new data. The Haddouche-Guedj online-to-PAC conversion also handles the sequential / online setting where draws arrive one at a time, which matches the weekly-draw regime. The framework requires only: (1) a fixed training dataset of draws, (2) a prior policy P (e.g., pretrained checkpoint), (3) a way to evaluate empirical return on the dataset. No online environment interaction is required. This makes it ideal for the lottery-nn setting where new data arrives at most once per week.

- **Complexity:**
  > low — PAC-Bayes is a theoretical framework, not an algorithm. Applying it in practice means adding a KL divergence regularization term to the existing policy training loss. This is a minor modification to an existing training loop.

- **Key Dependencies:**
  > PyTorch only (KL divergence between two softmax distributions is a one-liner in PyTorch: torch.nn.functional.kl_div). No additional libraries required. For tighter bound computation (e.g., Catoni's bound via numerical root-finding), scipy.optimize suffices.

- **Estimated Lines Of Code:**
  > 20-50 lines — Adding KL(Q||P) penalty to the existing training loop: ~10 lines. Saving the pretrained checkpoint as a frozen prior P: ~5 lines. Computing and logging the PAC-Bayes generalization bound as a diagnostic: ~20 lines. No new model architecture is needed.

- **Transfer Learning Compatibility:**
  > Extremely high — PAC-Bayes is specifically designed for the transfer/fine-tuning setting. The pretrained SharedLotteryTransformer checkpoint (models/best_swarm.pt) serves as the prior P. The fine-tuned policy Q is trained on new data with a KL(Q||P) regularization term that prevents deviation from the pretrained prior. This is exactly how RLHF (Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback) works for large language models: a pre-trained model is used as prior, and the fine-tuned policy is constrained to stay close to it via KL penalty. The theory provides a direct guarantee that the generalization gap of Q is controlled by how much Q deviates from the pretrained P, validating the use of the existing 253K-parameter backbone as a principled starting point.

- **Relevance Score:** 3
- **Specific Application In Project:**
  > PAC-Bayes provides the theoretical foundation for the KL penalty already implicit in the swarm system's use of a pretrained prior. Concretely: (1) Freeze the current best_swarm.pt checkpoint as the prior P before each weekly fine-tuning step. (2) Add a KL(Q||P) penalty term to the joint-train loss: total_loss = -expected_return + beta * KL(Q||P), where beta is a tunable regularization coefficient. (3) Compute and log the PAC-Bayes generalization bound after each joint-train run as a diagnostic: gap = sqrt((KL + log(2*sqrt(n)/0.05)) / (2*n)). This provides a principled upper bound on how much worse the policy will perform on future draws compared to training draws. (4) Use the bound to set expectations: at n=100 draws and KL=1, the gap is ~0.14, meaning the model's generalization is certified to be within 0.14 return units. This applies to all three lotteries via the shared backbone. The primary value is theoretical validation and regularization guidance, not a new algorithm.

- **Integration Point:** Phase 2 (Actor-Critic play generation)
- **Replaces Or Extends:**
  > Extends — adds a KL regularization term to the existing joint-train loss and adds a bound-computation diagnostic. Does not replace any existing component. The KL penalty is applied during fine-tuning by comparing the current policy Q to the frozen pretrained prior P (best_swarm.pt snapshot before training begins).

- **Notable Use Cases:**
  > 1. RLHF for large language models (GPT-4, LLaMA fine-tuning): the KL penalty between fine-tuned policy and base model is the practical implementation of PAC-Bayes regularization in a combinatorial output setting (token selection). 2. Safe RL for medical dosing: PAC-Bayes bounds used to certify that learned dosing policies generalize from training patients to new patients with small datasets (n~100-500 patients). 3. Recommendation systems with implicit feedback: PAC-Bayes bounds on ranking policies trained from sparse click data — structurally similar to lottery (sparse reward, combinatorial ranking output). 4. Robotics sim-to-real transfer: PAC-Bayes bounds quantify how much a policy learned in simulation generalizes to real hardware, used to set safety margins. The RLHF use case is the most structurally analogous: pretrained prior, combinatorial discrete output, KL-regularized fine-tuning on limited new data.

### Population-based Training (PBT) for Hyperparameter Adaptation {#population-based-training-pbt-for-hyperparameter-adaptation}

**Type:** Technique | **Relevance:** 3/5 | **Integration:** Phase 3 (probabilistic models, DeepAR, N-BEATS) -- specifically as a hyperparameter optimization wrapper over the Phase 2 Actor-Critic joint-training loop

**General**

- **Year:** 2017
- **Key Paper:**
  > Jaderberg, M., Dalibard, V., Osindero, S., Czarnecki, W., Donahue, J., Razavi, A., Vinyals, O., Green, T., Dunning, I., Simonyan, K., Fernando, C., Kavukcuoglu, K. (2017). Population Based Training of Neural Networks. arXiv:1711.09846. DeepMind technical report, 2017.

- **Algorithm Description:**
  > PBT maintains a population of K agents (workers), each with its own copy of the model weights and a distinct hyperparameter configuration (e.g., learning rate, entropy coefficient, discount factor). All agents train in parallel on the same task. Periodically, a performance-based exploit-explore cycle is triggered: the bottom fraction of agents (by validation score) copy the weights and hyperparameters of a top-performing agent (exploit), then perturb those hyperparameters by small random mutations (explore). This combines the parallelism of random search with the online adaptation of hand-tuning: hyperparameters are evolved during training rather than fixed before it, so agents adapt to the loss landscape as it changes. The process produces both a trained model and an optimized hyperparameter schedule simultaneously, with no need for separate hyperparameter sweeps.

- **Action Space Handling:**
  > PBT is a meta-training technique, not a policy architecture. It does not itself define how the action space is represented or sampled. The underlying agent within each PBT worker uses whatever policy architecture is already defined (e.g., the SharedLotteryTransformer with autoregressive masking). PBT's contribution is to each worker's hyperparameters -- learning rate, entropy bonus coefficient, temperature -- not to the action representation itself. The diversity of hyperparameters across the population implicitly creates diversity in how each worker explores the action space.

- **How Combinatorial Output Is Formed:**
  > PBT does not define a combinatorial output mechanism. Each worker in the population delegates combinatorial generation to its underlying policy. In the lottery-nn context, each worker would use the existing autoregressive masking strategy over the per-lottery softmax head. Different workers would generate combinatorially valid plays at different sampling temperatures (a hyperparameter evolved by PBT), creating natural diversity in the candidate plays produced across the population.

- **Sequential Generation Strategy:**
  > Not applicable at the PBT level -- PBT wraps and adapts whatever generation strategy the underlying policy uses. In the lottery-nn context, each worker would use the sequential autoregressive strategy already implemented in the SharedLotteryTransformer. PBT's population of K workers, each with a different temperature hyperparameter, effectively creates K parallel autoregressive generators with diverse exploration profiles.

- **Gradient Estimator Type:**
  > Not specific to PBT -- each worker uses whatever gradient estimator the underlying policy gradient algorithm employs (REINFORCE, PPO clip, etc.). PBT's exploit-explore mechanism operates above the gradient level: it copies weights and perturbs hyperparameters rather than modifying gradient computation. If the underlying workers use REINFORCE, PBT adapts the entropy coefficient and learning rate for each worker to reduce REINFORCE variance indirectly. PBT itself introduces no additional estimator.

- **Combinatorial Constraint Enforcement:**
  > Not handled at the PBT level. Constraint enforcement (autoregressive masking, rejection sampling, Gumbel-top-k) is the responsibility of each worker's policy. PBT's hyperparameter evolution does not affect structural constraints. A hyperparameter PBT might adapt for combinatorial tasks is the masking temperature or the penalty coefficient for constraint violations, but the enforcement mechanism itself remains unchanged across the population.

- **Sample Efficiency Rating:**
  > Medium-high. PBT is more sample-efficient than independent random search because it allows information (weights and hyperparameters) to flow from better-performing agents to weaker ones, concentrating compute on promising regions of hyperparameter space. On very small datasets (n<200), the benefit is primarily that the population identifies good hyperparameter schedules faster than manual tuning, avoiding wasted epochs with misconfigured learning rates or entropy coefficients that would otherwise overfit or underfit on the small draw history. However, each individual worker still trains on the same n<200 samples; PBT does not generate additional data, so the fundamental sample efficiency of the underlying policy algorithm is unchanged. Rated medium-high because the hyperparameter adaptation benefit is real but the data bottleneck remains.

- **Regularization Built In:**
  > Implicit regularization through population diversity: because each worker trains with a different hyperparameter configuration, the population as a whole does not converge to the same local minimum, providing an ensemble-like regularization effect when plays are aggregated across workers. The exploit step acts as a form of knowledge distillation -- weak workers periodically reinitialize from stronger workers, preventing overfitting to a single noisy gradient trajectory. The explore (perturbation) step adds stochastic noise to hyperparameters, functioning as a randomized regularizer on the training dynamics. Entropy coefficient is commonly treated as a PBT-adapted hyperparameter, providing indirect entropy regularization on the Actor's policy distribution.

- **Known Failure Modes On Small Data:**
  > Population collapse: if the population is too small (K<4) or the exploit fraction too aggressive, all workers quickly converge to the same hyperparameter configuration, eliminating diversity and reducing PBT to a single-run trainer with extra overhead -- particularly likely when n<200 since validation scores are noisy and the ranking of workers is unstable. Overfitting to validation noise: with very few validation draws, a worker may rank highly by chance (lucky validation split, not genuine generalization), causing its configuration to be exploited by weaker workers and spreading a spuriously good-but-overfit hyperparameter set through the population. Compute overhead with small data: running K parallel training processes for a dataset with n<200 samples may be computationally wasteful since each worker trains fast -- the population overhead may exceed the benefit of hyperparameter adaptation. Weight staleness on exploit: when a weak worker copies weights from a strong worker, the copied weights were trained under different hyperparameters, potentially causing a temporary loss spike before the new (perturbed) hyperparameters align with the inherited weight landscape.

- **Reward Distribution Handling:**
  > PBT handles sparse reward indirectly by adapting the entropy coefficient hyperparameter of each worker's policy gradient algorithm. Workers with high entropy coefficients generate more diverse plays and accumulate more varied reward signals, which may help distinguish signal from noise in a sparse lottery reward regime (~95% zero-hit). The population's diversity in entropy settings means some workers explore aggressively while others exploit current knowledge, and the exploit step propagates the configuration that performs best under the sparse reward. PBT does not natively implement distributional return modeling, but it could adapt the hyperparameters of a distributional Critic (e.g., number of quantiles in QR-DQN) as a PBT hyperparameter. Minimum scored draws for a meaningful population ranking: roughly 20-30 draws per worker to obtain a stable validation hit rate for ranking.

- **Credit Assignment Granularity:**
  > PBT operates at the meta-level and does not directly determine credit assignment granularity. Each worker's credit assignment is determined by its underlying policy gradient algorithm. PBT can treat the reward shaping coefficient (e.g., the weight on number-level semi-bandit credit vs. play-level sparse reward) as an evolved hyperparameter, allowing the population to discover the optimal credit assignment granularity for the lottery task through evolution rather than manual tuning.

- **Diversity Mechanism:**
  > PBT's primary diversity mechanism is the population of agents with heterogeneous hyperparameters. At any point during training, workers have different entropy coefficients, learning rates, and temperatures, producing qualitatively different play distributions. The explore step (random perturbation of hyperparameters after exploit) maintains this diversity over time, preventing the population from collapsing to a single configuration. Additionally, because the exploit step only replaces the bottom fraction (typically bottom 20-25%), the top workers retain their existing configurations, preserving the diversity of high-performing hyperparameter regimes. In lottery-nn, aggregating plays across K workers at prediction time provides structural diversity: plays from a high-temperature worker complement plays from a low-temperature worker, covering more of the number space.

- **Offline Vs Online Rl Compatibility:**
  > Fully compatible with offline training. PBT requires no online environment interaction -- it trains each worker on a fixed historical dataset (draws.csv etc.) and uses held-out validation draws as the fitness signal for the exploit-explore ranking. In the lottery-nn context, the population would be trained offline on the historical draw history (~100 draws per lottery), and validation loss or held-out hit rate would rank workers. The exploit-explore cycle fires on a schedule (e.g., every N epochs) without requiring new real-world draws. When a new draw is logged weekly, the entire population can be retrained or fine-tuned on the updated dataset. This makes PBT fully compatible with the once-per-week operational cadence of the lottery system.

- **Complexity:** Medium
- **Key Dependencies:**
  > PyTorch (already installed). No external RL library strictly required -- PBT can be implemented with vanilla Python multiprocessing or concurrent.futures. Optional libraries that simplify PBT: Ray Tune (ray[tune]) provides a first-class PBT scheduler (PopulationBasedTraining class) with automatic worker management; Optuna supports PBT-style pruning (HyperbandPruner); Ax platform (Meta) supports PBT as a search strategy. Ray Tune is the most practical choice for the lottery-nn project given PyTorch integration.

- **Estimated Lines Of Code:**
  > 150-250 lines (custom implementation without Ray Tune): population initialization and worker management (~40 lines), exploit step (rank workers by validation score, copy top weights and hyperparameters to bottom workers, ~30 lines), explore step (perturb hyperparameters by +/-20% with clipping, ~20 lines), training loop with periodic exploit-explore trigger (~50 lines), integration with main_swarm.py joint-train command and SharedLotteryTransformer checkpoint loading (~60 lines). With Ray Tune: ~80-120 lines (Ray handles worker management; code defines the training function and PBT scheduler config).

- **Transfer Learning Compatibility:**
  > Excellent. PBT is designed to adapt hyperparameters during training rather than replace the model architecture. In lottery-nn, the SharedLotteryTransformer encoder is already pre-trained and each PBT worker would start from (or fine-tune from) the existing best_swarm.pt checkpoint, then evolve the fine-tuning hyperparameters (learning rate, entropy coefficient, temperature). The exploit step copies both weights and hyperparameters -- since all workers share the same architecture (SharedLotteryTransformer), weight copying between workers is straightforward. The lottery_id embedding and per-lottery heads are preserved across workers. PBT can also treat the joint-training round-robin ratio across lotteries as an evolved hyperparameter, discovering the optimal training mixture for the shared backbone.

- **Relevance Score:** 3
- **Specific Application In Project:**
  > PBT is most applicable in Phase 3 as a hyperparameter optimization layer wrapping the joint-training loop in main_swarm.py. A population of K=4-8 SharedLotteryTransformer instances would be initialized from best_swarm.pt, each with a different hyperparameter configuration (learning rate in [1e-4, 1e-3], entropy coefficient in [0.001, 0.1], temperature in [0.8, 1.6], joint-training lottery weights). The exploit-explore cycle fires every 10-20 training epochs; the bottom 25% of workers (ranked by mean validation loss across all 3 lotteries) copy the hyperparameters and weights of the top 25%, then perturb. After PBT convergence, the best worker's hyperparameter schedule is used for all future joint-training runs. PBT maps naturally onto the existing 3-lottery swarm structure because the population can maintain per-lottery hyperparameter diversity across workers. Concretely, PBT replaces the fixed hyperparameters in main_swarm.py joint-train with an evolved schedule, potentially improving the Actor-Critic training loop introduced in Phase 2 by discovering optimal entropy and learning rate schedules for sparse lottery reward. The 3-lottery swarm with shared backbone is structurally analogous to the multi-task setups where PBT was shown to outperform grid search (Jaderberg et al. 2017, Section 4.2).

- **Integration Point:** Phase 3 (probabilistic models, DeepAR, N-BEATS) -- specifically as a hyperparameter optimization wrapper over the Phase 2 Actor-Critic joint-training loop
- **Replaces Or Extends:**
  > Extends: PBT wraps the existing joint-training loop in main_swarm.py without replacing any component. It extends the training infrastructure by adding population management and exploit-explore scheduling on top of the existing checkpoint save/load mechanism (models/best_swarm.pt). Does not replace the SharedLotteryTransformer, the Actor-Critic, or the Thompson bandit -- it optimizes their hyperparameters.

- **Existing Open Source Implementations:**
  > 1. Ray Tune (ray[tune]): first-class PBT scheduler (ray.tune.schedulers.PopulationBasedTraining) with PyTorch Lightning and vanilla PyTorch integration. Most mature and actively maintained PBT implementation. 2. Optuna HyperbandPruner: implements a related Successive Halving strategy; not pure PBT but similar exploit logic. 3. DeepMind's original PBT code: not publicly released as a standalone library, but described fully in the arXiv paper with enough detail for clean reimplementation. 4. PBT-Boilerplate (GitHub: angusfung/population-based-training): lightweight PyTorch reference implementation of Jaderberg et al. 5. CleanRL (GitHub: vwxyzjn/cleanrl): includes PBT-style hyperparameter sweeping for RL algorithms. Ray Tune is the recommended choice for lottery-nn given its PyTorch compatibility and the project's existing use of simple training loops.

- **Benchmark Results:**
  > Jaderberg et al. (2017): PBT outperformed random search and Bayesian optimization on Atari (57 games, median human-normalized score), machine translation (BLEU score on WMT), and GAN training (Inception Score on CIFAR-10). On Atari, PBT with a population of 10 workers achieved higher median score than a 20x compute budget of random search, demonstrating information reuse efficiency. On machine translation, PBT found a non-monotonic learning rate schedule (decay then increase then decay) that hand-tuning missed. In multi-task RL (StarCraft II), PBT adapted task mixture weights during training, outperforming fixed mixtures. No benchmarks on combinatorial prediction tasks or datasets with n<200. Parker-Holder et al. (2022, ICLR) extended PBT to meta-gradient methods, showing benefits in low-interaction RL regimes -- the closest analog to the lottery-nn weekly-draw constraint.

- **Notable Use Cases:**
  > 1. AlphaStar (Vinyals et al., 2019, Nature): DeepMind used PBT to train the league of StarCraft II agents, adapting hyperparameters and opponent selection in a population of hundreds of agents -- structurally analogous to a multi-lottery swarm. 2. OpenAI Five (Berner et al., 2019): used PBT-style population training for Dota 2 agent hyperparameter adaptation across distributed workers. 3. Multi-task neural machine translation (Google Brain, 2019): PBT adapted the per-language training mixture weights during training of a shared encoder, directly analogous to the 3-lottery joint-training mixture in lottery-nn. 4. GAN training stabilization (Jaderberg et al. 2017): PBT adapted discriminator/generator learning rates dynamically, preventing mode collapse -- analogous to preventing policy collapse in the Actor-Critic on sparse lottery reward. 5. Drug discovery (Godinez et al. 2022): PBT used for hyperparameter adaptation in molecular generation models with very small labeled datasets, the closest published use case to lottery-nn's small-data regime.


**Uncertain Fields**

- data_regime_evidence.minimum_training_samples_tested
- data_regime_evidence.performance_degradation_curve
- convergence_guarantees_small_n
