### Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO) for Discrete Combinatorial Actions {#proximal-policy-optimization-ppo-for-discrete-combinatorial-actions}

**Type:** RL Algorithm | **Relevance:** 3/5 | **Integration:** Phase 2 (Actor-Critic play generation)

**General**

- **Year:** 2017
- **Key Paper:** Schulman, J., Wolski, F., Dhariwal, P., Radford, A., & Klimov, O. (2017). Proximal Policy Optimization Algorithms. arXiv:1707.06347. OpenAI technical report.
- **Algorithm Description:**
  > PPO is an on-policy policy gradient algorithm that improves training stability by clipping the surrogate objective, preventing excessively large policy updates. At each iteration, it collects a batch of trajectories under the current policy, then optimizes a clipped importance-weighted objective (PPO-Clip) or a KL-penalized objective (PPO-KL) using multiple mini-batch gradient steps. The clipping ratio epsilon (typically 0.1-0.2) constrains how far the new policy can deviate from the old one per update, acting as a trust region without the expensive second-order computation of TRPO. PPO alternates between data collection and multiple epochs of gradient updates on the collected batch, making it relatively sample-efficient among on-policy methods. A value-function critic is trained in parallel to estimate the advantage function, reducing variance in the policy gradient estimate.

- **Action Space Handling:**
  > Standard PPO treats each action dimension independently with a factored (independent) policy head — each element of the multi-hot output vector gets a separate Bernoulli or categorical distribution. For combinatorial set actions (choose k from N), PPO can be adapted via: (1) a flat multi-hot head where N independent Bernoulli logits are output and sampled independently, post-filtered to enforce k; (2) an autoregressive factored policy where each number is sampled one at a time conditioned on previous selections; or (3) a Gumbel-top-k reparameterization to directly sample exactly k items. The standard PPO formulation does not natively handle combinatorial constraints — these require explicit adaptation.

- **How Combinatorial Output Is Formed:**
  > The most common PPO adaptation for k-of-N selection is a two-stage approach: (1) the policy network outputs N logits over the ball pool; (2) either top-k selection (argmax-based, non-differentiable) or stochastic sampling without replacement (Gumbel-top-k, differentiable) is applied to select exactly k distinct numbers. A simpler but less principled approach is independent Bernoulli sampling on N dimensions followed by rejection/clipping to exactly k balls post-hoc (e.g., sample until k balls selected or mask already-selected balls). Autoregressive masking is another option: at step t, set the logit of all previously selected balls to -inf before sampling, generating exactly k distinct numbers in k sequential steps. Each approach has different gradient estimator implications.

- **Sequential Generation Strategy:**
  > PPO supports both joint (flat multi-hot) and autoregressive generation strategies. In the flat strategy, all k numbers are determined simultaneously from N independent logits — inter-number correlations cannot be modeled and constraint enforcement requires post-hoc filtering. In the autoregressive strategy, numbers are generated one at a time (k steps), with the policy conditioned on the previously chosen numbers via masking or embedding; this captures correlations but multiplies the action-space depth by k. For the lottery-nn project with k=7 (LottoMax), an autoregressive policy with mask-based constraint enforcement is the most principled PPO adaptation, though it requires k forward passes per play generation.

- **Gradient Estimator Type:**
  > PPO uses the REINFORCE (score function / likelihood-ratio) estimator as its foundation, but stabilizes it via the clipped surrogate objective and advantage normalization. The standard importance-sampling ratio r_t(theta) = pi_new(a|s) / pi_old(a|s) replaces raw policy gradient weights. For discrete actions, this is exact (no reparameterization needed). When using Gumbel-top-k for differentiable sampling, a straight-through or reparameterization estimator can replace REINFORCE within the PPO objective. Entropy bonus is added to the objective as an explicit regularizer. Advantage estimation uses Generalized Advantage Estimation (GAE, lambda=0.95 typical), which provides a bias-variance tradeoff superior to raw Monte Carlo returns, particularly important on small datasets with noisy reward.

- **Combinatorial Constraint Enforcement:**
  > PPO does not natively enforce combinatorial constraints (exactly k distinct from {1..N}). Three practical approaches: (1) Autoregressive masking — at each of k steps, set logits of already-selected numbers to -inf, guaranteeing distinct selection; this is the cleanest and most common method in practice. (2) Gumbel-top-k reparameterization — sample k items jointly without replacement in one forward pass using the Gumbel-max trick; differentiable and constraint-guaranteed. (3) Post-hoc rejection sampling — sample from N Bernoulli heads independently, then resample or trim/pad to exactly k; simple but introduces bias and can produce degenerate distributions. For lottery-nn, autoregressive masking over k=7 steps is recommended for correctness; Gumbel-top-k is a viable alternative for joint generation.

- **Sample Efficiency Rating:**
  > low-to-medium. PPO is an on-policy algorithm that requires fresh environment interactions per update cycle — it discards off-policy data. On small historical datasets (< 200 draws), it must either treat the fixed dataset as a simulated environment (offline adaptation with importance sampling) or generate synthetic rollouts, both of which introduce distribution mismatch. Without a live environment, PPO's clipping mechanism provides no fundamental sample efficiency advantage over simpler REINFORCE. The GAE advantage estimator and entropy bonus do help stabilize training. Compared to off-policy methods (DQN, SAC) or bandit algorithms (Thompson sampling), PPO is less sample-efficient on small fixed datasets. Rating: low on pure offline data; medium if used as a fine-tuning step with synthetic draws augmenting the 100-200 historical draws.

- **Regularization Built In:**
  > PPO has several built-in regularization mechanisms: (1) Entropy bonus — an entropy term H(pi) is added to the loss (coefficient typically 0.01-0.05), preventing premature convergence to deterministic policies and providing diversity. (2) Clip ratio epsilon — the clipping of the importance weight ratio prevents large policy updates, acting as implicit regularization against overfitting to any single batch. (3) Value function clipping — an optional symmetric clip on the value function update prevents critic overfitting. (4) KL penalty variant (PPO-KL) adds an explicit KL divergence penalty between old and new policy. On small data, the entropy bonus is the most important regularizer; without it, the policy collapses to near-deterministic outputs after a few updates.

- **Known Failure Modes On Small Data:**
  > Several documented failure modes on n < 200: (1) Reward sparsity collapse — with ~95% zero-reward plays, the advantage estimates are near-zero for nearly all rollouts, causing the policy gradient to vanish and the policy to stagnate. (2) Policy entropy collapse — without a sufficiently large entropy coefficient, the policy rapidly collapses to a single deterministic play (mode collapse), especially problematic for set generation where diversity is required. (3) Critic overfitting — the value network memorizes the small dataset rather than generalizing, producing poor advantage estimates that destabilize the actor. (4) On-policy data waste — PPO discards data after each update; with only 100-200 historical draws, multiple PPO epochs on each mini-batch (standard PPO) can overfit badly. (5) Variance explosion on sparse rewards — Monte Carlo return estimates from sparse lottery rewards have extremely high variance, overwhelming the GAE smoothing even with lambda=0.95.

- **Reward Distribution Handling:**
  > PPO handles sparse rewards through advantage normalization and GAE but does not have native distributional return support. Key properties: (1) GAE with lambda=0.95 smooths the temporal credit assignment but requires multiple timesteps of reward signal — a lottery draw yields a single scalar reward per play, so there is no multi-step temporal structure to exploit. (2) Advantage normalization (subtract mean, divide by std) addresses scale but collapses when nearly all rewards are zero (std -> 0, causing numerical instability). (3) PPO does not natively support distributional returns (unlike IQN, C51) — the critic estimates a scalar value rather than a return distribution, losing information about variance of sparse rewards. (4) Minimum draws needed: empirically 500+ environment interactions are needed for stable PPO training; a lottery application generating 1 real draw per week would need years of real interaction. Offline augmentation with historical data is essential.

- **Credit Assignment Granularity:**
  > Standard PPO assigns reward at the trajectory/episode level — the entire play (set of k numbers) receives a single scalar reward. This is play-level credit assignment. Number-level (semi-bandit) feedback, where each individual number that matches the draw receives partial credit, is not natively supported but can be implemented by: (1) decomposing the reward into per-number Bernoulli signals and training k separate critics; or (2) using a shaped reward function (e.g., reward = num_hits / k) that still operates at play level but encodes partial information. For lottery-nn, play-level credit is the default PPO behavior, but shaped rewards using hit count (0-7) improve gradient signal significantly over binary win/loss and should be implemented to overcome sparsity.

- **Diversity Mechanism:**
  > PPO's primary diversity mechanism is the entropy bonus — the objective includes +alpha * H(pi) where H is the policy entropy. This encourages the policy to maintain a spread distribution over actions rather than collapsing. For combinatorial outputs, this translates to distributing probability mass across multiple numbers rather than concentrating on a single k-subset. Additional diversity mechanisms that can be added: (1) Population-based PPO (multiple parallel policies with diversity reward); (2) Explicit diversity reward penalizing overlap between simultaneously generated plays; (3) Temperature sampling at inference time (the policy logits are divided by T > 1 before sampling). Without explicit diversity reward or sufficiently large entropy coefficient (> 0.05), PPO typically collapses to 1-3 dominant plays within 50-100 training epochs on small data.

- **Offline Vs Online Rl Compatibility:**
  > PPO is fundamentally an on-policy online RL algorithm — it requires fresh environment interactions (new lottery draws) to collect on-policy data for each update. This makes it poorly suited for a weekly lottery draw cycle. Offline adaptation options: (1) Treat historical draws as a fixed environment and generate synthetic rollouts by replaying past draws with current policy; this introduces distribution shift. (2) Use PPO as a fine-tuning step after pre-training the actor on historical data via supervised learning (behavior cloning), with PPO updates applied to augmented synthetic data. (3) Offline PPO variants (e.g., Conservative Offline PPO, analogous to CQL) add pessimistic penalties for out-of-distribution actions but are not standard implementations. Verdict: PPO is not naturally compatible with fully offline training on 100-200 draws — it requires adaptation and is operationally less convenient than off-policy methods (SAC, DQN) or bandit approaches for this use case.

- **Complexity:** medium
- **Key Dependencies:**
  > PyTorch (already present), optionally stable-baselines3 for reference PPO implementation, numpy. No additional specialized libraries required for basic PPO. Gumbel-top-k sampling requires a small custom implementation (~50 lines). torchrl (Meta's RL library) provides production-grade PPO with combinatorial action support.

- **Estimated Lines Of Code:**
  > 350-500 lines of new code: Actor network head adaption (~80 lines), PPO training loop with clipped objective (~120 lines), GAE advantage computation (~60 lines), autoregressive masking sampler for k-of-N (~80 lines), entropy bonus and normalization utilities (~60 lines), replay buffer/rollout collector (~100 lines). Excludes boilerplate and logging.

- **Transfer Learning Compatibility:**
  > High compatibility. The existing SharedLotteryTransformer encoder can serve directly as the policy network backbone — its output embeddings feed into a new actor head (N logits) and a separate critic head (scalar value). Only the actor and critic heads (~2 small linear layers each) need to be added; the encoder weights can be frozen initially and optionally fine-tuned with a small learning rate. This is a standard Actor-Critic adaptation pattern. The lottery_id embedding already handles per-lottery differentiation, so the same architecture can produce per-lottery policies. The pretrained encoder provides a strong initialization that dramatically reduces the data needed for the RL fine-tuning phase, which is PPO's biggest advantage in this project over training from scratch.

- **Relevance Score:** 3
- **Specific Application In Project:**
  > PPO would be used in Phase 2 as the Actor-Critic play generation engine for all three lotteries (LottoMax, 6/49, Daily Grand). Concretely: (1) The SharedLotteryTransformer encoder becomes the shared backbone for actor and critic. (2) A per-lottery actor head outputs N logits (N=50 for LottoMax/6-49, N=49 for Daily Grand); autoregressive masking samples exactly k=7 or k=6 or k=5 distinct numbers per play. (3) A critic head estimates the expected hit count (scalar value function). (4) After each real draw, the historical dataset is replayed as simulated environment steps; PPO mini-batches are constructed from these replayed draws plus synthetic augmentations. (5) The entropy bonus coefficient is tuned aggressively (0.05-0.1) to maintain play diversity. (6) Reward shaping: reward = number of hits / k (range 0-1) to overcome reward sparsity. PPO replaces the current temperature-softmax sampling in predict.py. Caveat: given the small dataset (100-200 draws) and offline nature, a simpler REINFORCE-with-baseline or bandit approach may be more practical; PPO should be benchmarked against these baselines before committing to Phase 2.

- **Integration Point:** Phase 2 (Actor-Critic play generation)
- **Replaces Or Extends:**
  > Replaces temperature-softmax sampling in src/predict.py and src/model_swarm.py predict path. Extends the existing SharedLotteryTransformer by adding actor and critic linear heads on top of the existing encoder output, leaving the backbone intact.

- **Existing Open Source Implementations:**
  > 1. stable-baselines3 (https://github.com/DLR-RM/stable-baselines3) — production PPO with discrete action spaces, well-documented, PyTorch-based; does not natively support combinatorial set actions but can be extended. 2. torchrl (https://github.com/pytorch/rl) — Meta's official PyTorch RL library with PPO, supports custom action spaces including combinatorial via CompositeActionSpec. 3. CleanRL (https://github.com/vwxyzjn/cleanrl) — single-file PPO implementations with extensive comments, easiest to adapt for custom action spaces. 4. PointerNetwork + PPO for combinatorial optimization: (https://github.com/wouterkool/attention-learn-to-route) — attention model with REINFORCE for TSP/VRP, structurally similar to k-of-N selection with masking. 5. No lottery-specific PPO reference implementation found as of 2025.

- **Benchmark Results:**
  > PPO benchmarks on combinatorial output tasks: (1) Combinatorial Optimization (TSP, VRP): PPO-based attention models achieve near-optimal solutions on TSP-20 to TSP-100, outperforming classical heuristics but typically requiring 1M+ training instances (Kool et al. 2019, ICLR). (2) Discrete action games (Atari): PPO achieves state-of-the-art on most Atari games with 40M frames; not directly relevant to small-data regime. (3) Multi-label discrete RL (no standard benchmark): Limited literature on PPO for multi-hot output with n < 500 samples. (4) Sparse reward tasks: PPO requires reward shaping or HER (Hindsight Experience Replay) to work on sparse-reward environments; raw PPO on lottery-like 95% zero-reward environments fails to learn without reward shaping (empirically documented in robotics manipulation literature).

- **Notable Use Cases:**
  > 1. Combinatorial optimization with RL: PPO and REINFORCE applied to Traveling Salesman Problem (TSP), Vehicle Routing (VRP), and Job Scheduling — structurally similar (select k nodes from N), but with dense reward and large training sets (structurally dissimilar in data regime). 2. Drug molecule generation: PPO with graph-based combinatorial action spaces for selecting atoms/bonds from a fixed pool — small action spaces, sparse reward, some similarity to lottery k-of-N problem. 3. Recommendation systems: PPO for slate generation (select k items from N candidates) — directly analogous combinatorial structure; production systems at Netflix/Google use PPO-like objectives for k-of-N slate selection with autoregressive masking. 4. NLP token selection: PPO fine-tuning of language models (RLHF/InstructGPT) uses the same clipped surrogate objective on discrete token distributions — demonstrates PPO stability on large-scale discrete outputs. The slate recommendation use case (k items from N, diversity required) is the closest structural analog to lottery play generation.

### Pyro Probabilistic Programming for Lottery — Variational LSTM with Uncertainty Quantification {#pyro-probabilistic-programming-for-lottery-variational-lstm-with-uncertainty-quantification}

**Type:** Architecture | **Relevance:** 3/5 | **Integration:** Phase 3 (probabilistic models, DeepAR, N-BEATS)

**General**

- **Year:** 2018
- **Key Paper:**
  > Bingham et al., 'Pyro: Deep Universal Probabilistic Programming', JMLR 2019 (v.20, no.28); Chen et al., 'Stochastic Video Generation with a Learned Prior' (ICML 2019) for probabilistic LSTM pattern; Fortunato et al., 'Bayesian Recurrent Neural Networks', arXiv 2017 for Bayesian LSTM background

- **Algorithm Description:**
  > Pyro is a deep probabilistic programming language (PPL) built on PyTorch that enables specifying probabilistic models as Python programs with stochastic primitives (pyro.sample). Inference is performed via Stochastic Variational Inference (SVI): a guide (variational distribution q_phi) approximates the true posterior p(z|x) by minimizing the Evidence Lower Bound (ELBO) via gradient descent. For a probabilistic LSTM head, the model defines latent variables z_t at each timestep (or at the output layer) drawn from a prior (e.g., Normal(0,1)); the guide learns approximate posterior parameters (mu_phi, sigma_phi) conditioned on the observed sequence. The decoder maps latent samples through the output head to produce distributions over lottery numbers rather than point estimates. At prediction time, Monte Carlo sampling over the posterior yields calibrated uncertainty intervals: running the model N times with different z samples produces N plausible play candidates whose spread reflects the model's epistemic uncertainty about which numbers are likely.

- **Action Space Handling:**
  > Pyro does not impose a specific action space structure — the action space is defined by the model program. For lottery number prediction, the output is a multi-hot vector of length N (50 for LottoMax). In the variational LSTM formulation, the model outputs a Bernoulli distribution parameter vector p in [0,1]^N for each number (or a Categorical for selecting exactly k), with uncertainty quantified via the posterior over latent variables z. The action space is flat/factored: each number is sampled independently from its marginal posterior distribution, which misses inter-number correlations but is straightforward to implement. An autoregressive extension (each number conditioned on previous) is possible but requires custom guide design and is not native to standard Pyro tutorials.

- **How Combinatorial Output Is Formed:**
  > In the standard Pyro probabilistic LSTM setup, the model outputs a vector of Bernoulli logits (one per number in the pool). To form a play of exactly k distinct numbers, two approaches exist: (1) Threshold/top-k post-processing: sample the posterior predictive distribution N times (each sample is a full probability vector p in [0,1]^N), then take the top-k indices by probability — this enforces cardinality but loses calibration; (2) Gumbel-top-k: treat the Bernoulli logits as unnormalized scores and apply differentiable top-k sampling (Kool et al. 2019) after posterior sampling, preserving approximate differentiability. The most common practical approach in lottery-nn context would be posterior sampling (100-1000 samples) followed by top-k selection and frequency aggregation across samples to generate a ranked list of candidate numbers.

- **Sequential Generation Strategy:**
  > Jointly (flat multi-hot) by default in the standard Pyro probabilistic LSTM. The LSTM processes the historical draw sequence and produces a context vector; the variational head maps this to per-number Bernoulli parameters. Each number's probability is computed independently of others in the same draw — no autoregressive conditioning within a single play. Inter-draw temporal correlations ARE modeled (the LSTM sequence models how number frequencies evolve over time), but intra-play correlations (e.g., that number 7 and 14 tend to co-occur) are not captured. A fully autoregressive within-play model (like a pointer network) would require a different architecture and is not the standard Pyro LSTM pattern.

- **Gradient Estimator Type:**
  > Pyro's SVI uses the reparameterization trick (pathwise gradient estimator) for continuous latent variables (Normal, Beta, etc.) — this is low-variance and stable. For discrete latent variables (Bernoulli, Categorical), Pyro supports several estimators: (1) REINFORCE (score function estimator) — high variance, requires baseline; (2) Relaxed/Gumbel-softmax via pyro.distributions.RelaxedBernoulli or RelaxedOneHotCategorical — enables reparameterization for discrete variables with a temperature hyperparameter; (3) TraceEnum_ELBO — exact enumeration for small discrete spaces, not scalable to N=50. For a probabilistic LSTM with continuous latent z (most common design), the reparameterization trick applies cleanly and provides low-variance gradients, making SVI training stable even on small datasets.

- **Combinatorial Constraint Enforcement:**
  > Pyro does not natively enforce the 'exactly k distinct numbers' constraint within a probabilistic model. Enforcement options: (1) Post-hoc top-k on the mean posterior predictive — simplest, breaks differentiability; (2) Constrained distributions: define a custom PyTorch distribution for k-subset sampling (e.g., using the PlackettLuce distribution, available in pyro.distributions) which assigns probability to permutations and naturally yields sets of size k via top-k; (3) Soft constraint via loss: add a penalty term to the ELBO that penalizes expected cardinality deviating from k — integrates cleanly with SVI; (4) Acceptance-rejection in posterior predictive sampling: draw samples, reject those with wrong cardinality, reweight — valid but slow. The PlackettLuce approach is the most principled Pyro-native method for set-valued outputs.

- **Sample Efficiency Rating:**
  > medium — Pyro's variational inference with informative priors (e.g., Beta priors encoding historical base rates) is more data-efficient than maximum-likelihood point estimates because the prior regularizes parameter estimates when data is scarce. On fewer than 200 draws, the prior dominates the posterior, which is both a strength (prevents overfitting) and a limitation (predictions are heavily influenced by prior assumptions). Bayesian LSTM models have been shown to provide reasonable uncertainty estimates with as few as 50-200 training examples in regression tasks (Fortunato et al. 2017). However, the ELBO optimization itself can be unstable on very small datasets due to high-variance mini-batch gradient estimates of the KL term. Using the full dataset per SVI step (batch size = all draws) mitigates this at the cost of scalability — acceptable for ~100-200 lottery draws.

- **Regularization Built In:**
  > Multiple inherent regularization mechanisms: (1) KL divergence penalty in ELBO: KL(q_phi(z) || p(z)) penalizes the posterior from deviating too far from the prior, acting as a Bayesian weight decay; (2) Prior specification: informative priors on output logits (e.g., Beta(alpha, beta) encoding historical frequency) regularize predictions toward base rates; (3) Dropout MC (via pyro.contrib.bnn or manual nn.Dropout with training=True at test time) provides an ensemble effect; (4) Automatic regularization via model structure: latent variable z forces information bottleneck through a low-dimensional space; (5) Weight uncertainty: BNN extension (Bayes by Backprop, available in Pyro) places distributions over network weights, preventing overconfident parameter estimates on small data.

- **Known Failure Modes On Small Data:**
  > 1) Posterior collapse: the KL term dominates the ELBO on small datasets, causing the guide to collapse to the prior (q_phi approx p) — the latent variables z carry no information and predictions revert to base rates. Mitigation: KL annealing (beta-VAE schedule, gradually increasing KL weight from 0 to 1 during training). 2) Guide expressiveness mismatch: mean-field guides (diagonal Normal) may poorly approximate true posteriors for recurrent models with strong temporal correlations — normalizing flow guides (Pyro supports IAF, RealNVP) improve this but increase complexity. 3) ELBO instability: with fewer than 50 training points and 1-sample Monte Carlo ELBO estimates, gradient variance is very high. Fix: use multiple ELBO samples per step (num_particles in Pyro SVI). 4) Prior sensitivity: results can be highly sensitive to prior hyperparameter choices when data is sparse — requires prior sensitivity analysis which adds experimental overhead.

- **Reward Distribution Handling:**
  > Pyro handles skewed reward distributions naturally through the probabilistic model's likelihood specification. For lottery's ~95% zero-hit regime: the model's output distribution (Bernoulli per number) can be calibrated to reflect true low-probability events via the posterior. Importantly, Pyro enables explicit uncertainty quantification — when training data shows that most number combinations lose, the posterior predictive distribution will assign low probability mass to most combinations, and the uncertainty intervals will be wide (high epistemic uncertainty), which is the correct and honest behavior. Unlike point-estimate models that may overconfidently predict specific numbers, the Bayesian approach acknowledges that the lottery is nearly unpredictable. Minimum draws for stable SVI learning: approximately 50-100 draws, though meaningful posterior updating requires at least 30-50 non-trivial observations.

- **Credit Assignment Granularity:**
  > Number-level credit assignment is native to the Pyro probabilistic LSTM approach. Since the model outputs per-number Bernoulli probabilities (one per number in the pool), training with binary labels per number (did number i appear in this draw?) directly updates each number's posterior probability independently. This is exactly the semi-bandit feedback structure: observing a draw (3, 7, 18, 24, 31, 42, bonus=15) provides a binary signal for all 49/50 numbers simultaneously, not just the play-level win/loss. The ELBO update uses all N binary observations per draw, giving N times more gradient signal per draw than play-level-only methods. This is a significant advantage over methods that only receive play-level reward.

- **Diversity Mechanism:**
  > Diversity emerges naturally from posterior predictive sampling: drawing multiple plays from the posterior predictive distribution (by first sampling z from q_phi(z|x), then sampling numbers from p(numbers|z)) produces plays that differ according to the posterior uncertainty. When uncertainty is high (as expected for lottery), the spread of sampled plays is wide. Additional diversity mechanisms: (1) Temperature scaling on the Bernoulli logits at prediction time (already present in lottery-nn via config.TEMPERATURE); (2) Explicit diversity through repulsive particle variational inference (Liu & Wang 2016, Stein Variational Gradient Descent — available in Pyro) which pushes posterior particles apart; (3) Mixture guide: using a mixture of Gaussians as the variational posterior (pyro.infer.autoguide.AutoMixtureLowRankMultivariateNormal) increases diversity of sampled plays naturally.

- **Offline Vs Online Rl Compatibility:**
  > Fully compatible with offline training from a fixed historical dataset — this is Pyro's primary use case. SVI trains the variational parameters on the observed draw history without any online environment interaction. No live draws are needed during training. The historical CSV (draws.csv, draws_649.csv, draws_dailygrand.csv) is the complete training set, processed once per epoch. After each new real draw is logged via main.py log, the model can be updated by running one additional SVI pass over the full dataset (or fine-tuned with a few steps on the new observation). This is ideal for the lottery-nn workflow of weekly updates. Pyro also supports online Bayesian updating in principle (sequential SVI or Kalman filter-style updates), though this requires additional implementation beyond the standard SVI loop.

- **Complexity:** high
- **Key Dependencies:**
  > pyro-ppl (pip install pyro-ppl) — core dependency, installs pyro and pyro.distributions on top of PyTorch. No other mandatory dependencies beyond what lottery-nn already uses (torch, numpy, pandas). Optional: funsor (for exact inference with enumeration, pip install funsor), numpyro (JAX-based alternative, not needed if staying PyTorch). Pyro version >= 1.8 recommended for full SVI and autoguide support. As of 2025 Pyro-PPL is actively maintained by Uber AI / community.

- **Estimated Lines Of Code:**
  > 300-450 lines — probabilistic LSTM model definition in Pyro syntax (~80 lines: prior specs, pyro.sample calls for z, likelihood), mean-field guide definition or AutoDiagonalNormal autoguide (~30 lines), SVI training loop with ELBO logging (~60 lines), posterior predictive sampling function with top-k play generation (~60 lines), integration with SharedLotteryTransformer encoder as deterministic prefix + stochastic head (~80 lines), prediction uncertainty reporting + integration into main_swarm.py predict command (~60 lines), config additions for num_particles/KL_beta/prior_scale (~20 lines). Total ~390 lines excluding boilerplate.

- **Transfer Learning Compatibility:**
  > High compatibility via a clean encoder-head split. The existing SharedLotteryTransformer encoder (pretrained, ~253K params) can be used as a deterministic feature extractor: its output context vector h_t is passed to the new Pyro probabilistic head as a conditioning variable. Only the probabilistic head (latent variable z, guide parameters, output Bernoulli logits) is stochastic and trained via SVI — the encoder can be either frozen (fastest, ~0 additional encoder compute) or fine-tuned jointly (more powerful but risks ELBO instability on small data). Recommended approach: freeze the encoder for the first 50 SVI epochs, then unfreeze with a small learning rate (1e-5) for the encoder vs. 1e-3 for the guide. This is analogous to how VAEs are often trained with a pretrained encoder backbone.

- **Relevance Score:** 3
- **Specific Application In Project:**
  > In lottery-nn, Pyro would add a probabilistic output head on top of the existing SharedLotteryTransformer backbone. Specifically: (1) Replace the deterministic linear output layer in model_swarm.py with a Pyro model/guide pair — the guide learns per-number posterior distributions (mu_i, sigma_i) over Bernoulli logits for each number in the pool; (2) Training via SVI on historical draws.csv/draws_649.csv/draws_dailygrand.csv using binary supervision (did number i appear in draw t? yes/no for all i); (3) At prediction time, run 200-500 posterior predictive samples, aggregate into a probability vector with uncertainty bands, and select the top-k numbers as the recommended play along with confidence intervals; (4) The uncertainty output becomes a new field in the predict command: 'high confidence' numbers (low variance across samples) vs 'uncertain' numbers (high variance), giving the user actionable information; (5) The swarm_state.json can store per-lottery posterior parameters as a lightweight serializable representation of the Bayesian model state. Applies to all three lotteries using per-lottery output heads, consistent with the existing swarm architecture. Recommended for Phase 3 after Actor-Critic (Phase 2) is complete.

- **Integration Point:** Phase 3 (probabilistic models, DeepAR, N-BEATS)
- **Replaces Or Extends:**
  > Extends — adds a stochastic Pyro probabilistic head on top of the existing SharedLotteryTransformer encoder without replacing it. The deterministic LSTM/Transformer backbone remains; the output layer becomes a variational inference module. Prediction output is extended from a single point-estimate play to a play with per-number uncertainty scores.

- **Existing Open Source Implementations:**
  > 1) Official Pyro tutorials (pyro.ai/examples): 'Bayesian Regression', 'Deep Markov Model' (probabilistic LSTM analog), 'Time Series Forecasting' — all directly relevant patterns; 2) pyro-ppl/pyro GitHub: github.com/pyro-ppl/pyro — actively maintained, 8000+ stars as of 2025, includes autoguide modules for minimal boilerplate; 3) Deep Markov Model (Krishnan et al. 2017): reference probabilistic RNN implementation in Pyro, directly applicable to sequential draw prediction; 4) pyro-ppl/numpyro: JAX version, faster on GPU but less relevant here since lottery-nn uses PyTorch; 5) Blundell et al. BNN reference in Pyro: pyro.contrib.bnn provides BayesianLinear layers for weight-space uncertainty (alternative to latent variable z approach); 6) Pyro autoguides (pyro.infer.autoguide): AutoDiagonalNormal, AutoLowRankMultivariateNormal — drastically reduce guide boilerplate to 2-3 lines of code.

- **Benchmark Results:**
  > Pyro (Bingham et al. JMLR 2019): demonstrated scalable SVI on large-scale Bayesian neural networks and VAEs; key result: SVI scales to millions of parameters with reparameterization gradients. Deep Markov Model (Krishnan et al. 2017, AAAI 2017): probabilistic LSTM achieved better calibration than deterministic LSTM on sequential clinical data (MIMIC-III, ~10K patients) — not small data, but validates the architecture pattern. Fortunato et al. (2017, Bayes by Backprop LSTM): 10-20% perplexity improvement over standard LSTM on Penn Treebank language modeling, with meaningful uncertainty estimates; tested down to ~1/10 of training data (~100K tokens, not draws). Dropout as Bayesian Approximation (Gal & Ghahramani 2016, ICML): MC Dropout (implementable in Pyro or vanilla PyTorch) showed well-calibrated uncertainty on MNIST and regression tasks with limited data — this simpler Pyro-compatible approach may be more practical for lottery-nn at n < 200. No benchmarks specific to lottery or combinatorial number selection tasks.

- **Notable Use Cases:**
  > 1) Clinical time series prediction (Deep Markov Model, Krishnan 2017): probabilistic LSTM for patient state evolution — sequential discrete observations with sparse informative events, directly analogous to lottery draw sequences; 2) Financial time series uncertainty quantification: multiple papers use Pyro's SVI for stock return distribution modeling, leveraging the fat-tail properties of posterior predictive distributions — structurally similar to lottery's highly skewed reward; 3) Demand forecasting with uncertainty (Amazon DeepAR, Salinas et al. 2020): probabilistic RNN for sparse intermittent demand — closest published analog to sparse lottery hits, though DeepAR uses a separate implementation; 4) Drug activity prediction (Ramsundar et al., DeepChem): Bayesian neural networks via Pyro for molecular property prediction from limited assay data (<200 labeled molecules per target) — validates small-data probabilistic NN viability; 5) Anomaly detection in sequential data: Pyro-based VAE-LSTM models identify anomalous patterns, relevant since rare lottery hits can be viewed as anomaly detection in reverse.


**Uncertain Fields**

- data_regime_evidence

### REINFORCE for multi-label outputs {#reinforce-for-multi-label-outputs}

**Type:** RL Algorithm | **Relevance:** 3/5 | **Integration:** Phase 2 (Actor-Critic play generation)

**General**

- **Year:** 1992
- **Key Paper:** Williams, R.J. (1992). Simple statistical gradient-following algorithms for connectionist reinforcement learning. Machine Learning, 8(3-4), 229-256.
- **Algorithm Description:**
  > REINFORCE is a Monte Carlo policy gradient algorithm that estimates the gradient of expected return by sampling trajectories and weighting log-probabilities by the observed return (or advantage). The policy parameters theta are updated via: grad_theta J = E[R * grad_theta log pi_theta(a|s)], where R is the scalar reward signal. For multi-label outputs (e.g., selecting a set of k numbers), the action is the full set and the policy factors either as an independent Bernoulli per element or as an autoregressive product. A baseline b (typically a learned state-value function or a running mean return) is subtracted from R to reduce gradient variance: delta_theta = alpha * (R - b) * grad_theta log pi_theta(a|s). No critic is strictly required, but adding one converts REINFORCE into the Actor-Critic family. Training is on-policy: trajectories must be generated by the current policy, which in the lottery context means using historical draws as offline reward signals paired with current policy samples.

- **Action Space Handling:**
  > REINFORCE handles the action space by parameterizing the policy pi_theta(a|s) and computing score-function gradients without requiring differentiability of the reward. For multi-label discrete outputs, the action space is the power set 2^N restricted to k-subsets. The most common factorization is: (1) independent Bernoulli per element -- each of N positions has its own sigmoid-gated probability, with the set formed by independent Bernoulli draws; or (2) autoregressive -- sample element i conditioned on all previously sampled elements, so pi(a) = prod_{i=1}^{k} pi(a_i | a_1, ..., a_{i-1}). REINFORCE does not require the factorization to be differentiable; the score-function trick bypasses discrete sampling by differentiating log pi instead of the sample itself.

- **How Combinatorial Output Is Formed:**
  > In the simplest multi-label formulation the network outputs N logits (one per number, e.g., 50 for LottoMax). Each logit is passed through sigmoid to get p_i = sigma(z_i). At inference time, k distinct numbers are drawn without replacement proportional to these probabilities -- the most common approach is stochastic top-k via Gumbel noise: add Gumbel(0,1) to log p_i then take the top-k indices. This preserves the REINFORCE estimator because sampling from log p + Gumbel is equivalent to categorical sampling. Post-hoc rejection sampling is simpler but higher variance. An alternative factored-autoregressive formulation samples number 1 from the full softmax, masks it, samples number 2 from the remaining N-1, and so on for k steps; this models inter-number correlations but multiplies computational cost by k.

- **Sequential Generation Strategy:**
  > REINFORCE supports both flat multi-hot (joint) and autoregressive generation. In the flat multi-hot strategy all k numbers are generated in a single forward pass: the policy head outputs N independent logits and k items are selected simultaneously (no inter-number conditioning). This is O(N) per step but cannot model correlations between selected numbers. In the autoregressive strategy the transformer generates numbers one at a time, masking already-selected numbers at each step; inter-number correlations are explicitly modeled at O(k * N) cost per play. For the lottery-nn project the existing SharedLotteryTransformer already produces multi-hot logits, so the flat strategy is the zero-refactor starting point. Autoregressive decoding requires adding a sequential sampling loop to predict.py.

- **Gradient Estimator Type:**
  > REINFORCE uses the score-function estimator (also called REINFORCE estimator or log-derivative trick): grad_theta E[R] = E[R * grad_theta log pi_theta(a|s)]. This estimator is unbiased but high-variance, especially with sparse rewards and small batches. Variance reduction techniques applied in practice: (1) baseline subtraction -- subtract a running mean or learned value function b(s) from R before multiplying; (2) reward normalization -- normalize R across the mini-batch; (3) gradient clipping -- clip grad norm to prevent exploding updates. No reparameterization is used (that belongs to Gumbel-softmax / DARN). Straight-through estimator is not used. The estimator is unbiased under correct policy sampling but can exhibit high variance (O(1/sqrt(n)) convergence) with fewer than 50 independent samples per update.

- **Combinatorial Constraint Enforcement:**
  > REINFORCE itself is agnostic to combinatorial constraints -- constraint enforcement is handled at the sampling stage, not in the gradient estimator. The common approaches compatible with REINFORCE are: (1) without-replacement sampling -- after computing N probabilities, apply Gumbel top-k to draw exactly k distinct indices; (2) autoregressive masking -- at each of k steps zero out the logits of already-selected numbers before softmax, guaranteeing uniqueness by construction; (3) rejection sampling -- sample with replacement until k distinct numbers are obtained, then accept. For lottery-nn the simplest compliant strategy is Gumbel top-k applied to the existing 50-logit head, requiring only 3-4 lines of new code in predict.py. The gradient log pi_theta(a|s) is computed using the log-probabilities of the actual sampled set.

- **Regularization Built In:**
  > REINFORCE has no built-in regularization. Common additions used in practice: (1) entropy bonus -- add beta * H(pi) to the reward to prevent premature policy collapse (H = -sum p_i log p_i); (2) L2 weight decay on policy network parameters; (3) gradient clipping (norm or value clipping). The baseline subtraction (b(s) or mean return) is a variance reduction technique, not regularization. Without explicit entropy regularization REINFORCE is prone to mode collapse on small datasets -- once the policy concentrates probability on a small subset of numbers, the gradient signal from rare high-reward plays cannot reverse it. Adding an entropy coefficient of 0.01-0.05 is standard practice.

- **Known Failure Modes On Small Data:**
  > 1. High-variance gradient collapse: with n < 200 samples the score-function estimator variance dominates signal; policy diverges or stalls rather than improving. 2. Mode collapse: policy concentrates mass on a few numbers early in training, gradient signal from rare wins cannot reverse it without entropy regularization. 3. Baseline overfitting: a learned value-function baseline (MLP) trained on fewer than 200 samples overfits and provides negative-value variance reduction -- the running-mean baseline is safer. 4. Reward signal sparsity amplification: in lottery ~95% of plays have R=0, so most gradient steps are zero; the algorithm wastes compute and fails to update on the informative non-zero draws. 5. On-policy distribution mismatch: REINFORCE requires fresh policy rollouts; using stale historical draws as surrogate on-policy trajectories introduces bias and can destabilize training.

- **Reward Distribution Handling:**
  > REINFORCE uses a scalar reward R per episode and has no built-in mechanism for handling distributional skew. For lottery application: hit rate distribution is approximately 93% zero-hit, 6% 1-hit, 1% 2-hit, rare 3+. A scalar return R = num_hits / max_hits normalizes to [0,1] but preserves extreme skew. Baseline subtraction (R - b) centers around the mean return (~0.05), so most gradients are negative (penalizing non-hits) and rare positive gradients (hits) drive all learning. Distributional returns are not natively supported -- that requires QR-DQN or IQL. Minimum draws needed for stable learning: empirically estimated at 500-1000 plays with standard REINFORCE on sparse rewards; lottery is ~10-20x below this threshold. Reward shaping (e.g., partial credit per number hit) is essential for this regime.

- **Credit Assignment Granularity:**
  > REINFORCE assigns credit at the play level (episode level): a single scalar R is attributed equally to all k numbers in the selected set. There is no per-number credit assignment -- if number 7 hits but numbers 12, 23, 31, 38, 44, 50 do not, the gradient still credits all 7 numbers equally. This wastes the semi-bandit feedback available in lottery (we know which specific numbers hit). Semi-bandit credit assignment requires per-element reward decomposition, which is not part of vanilla REINFORCE but can be added: compute per-element reward r_i = indicator(number_i hit) and use element-wise log-probability gradient sum_{i in a} r_i * grad_theta log p_i. This extension is compatible with REINFORCE and dramatically improves signal efficiency at the small-n regime.

- **Diversity Mechanism:**
  > Vanilla REINFORCE has no explicit diversity mechanism. The policy will collapse to a near-deterministic set of numbers once the gradient concentrates probability on a few numbers. Standard additions: (1) entropy bonus -- add beta * H(pi) = beta * (-sum_i p_i log p_i) to R; a coefficient of 0.01-0.05 maintains distributional spread; (2) temperature scaling at sampling time (already present in the existing predict.py at T=1.2); (3) for multi-play generation, independently sample each play from pi_theta -- no explicit inter-play diversity is enforced. To generate diverse plays that don't all select the same numbers, determinantal point process (DPP) sampling or an explicit diversity reward (penalize Jaccard overlap between plays) can be added on top of REINFORCE, but neither is built in.

- **Offline Vs Online Rl Compatibility:**
  > REINFORCE is an on-policy algorithm by design -- it requires trajectories generated by the current policy pi_theta to produce unbiased gradient estimates. In the lottery context this means: actions (play suggestions) must be generated by the current policy, then evaluated against actual draw outcomes. Since draws occur weekly, true on-policy REINFORCE requires waiting weeks per update cycle -- operationally impractical. Workaround: treat historical draws as environment outcomes and generate policy plays independently (ignoring history-play pairing), using historical hits as reward signal. This introduces importance-sampling bias and is technically off-policy. Full offline compatibility (train from a fixed dataset of (s, a, r) tuples without new environment interaction) is NOT supported by standard REINFORCE -- for that, Conservative Q-Learning (CQL) or Implicit Q-Learning (IQL) are the correct choices. REINFORCE is rated: partially offline-compatible with approximation, not cleanly offline.

- **Complexity:** low
- **Key Dependencies:**
  > No additional libraries required beyond PyTorch (torch.distributions.Categorical, torch.distributions.Bernoulli are built-in). Optional: numpy for baseline running mean. No stable-baselines3, torchrl, or pyro required for the basic implementation.

- **Estimated Lines Of Code:**
  > 80-120 lines of new code: ~30 lines for the REINFORCE training loop (rollout generation, reward computation, log-prob accumulation, gradient step), ~20 lines for baseline running mean, ~20 lines for entropy bonus, ~20 lines for integration hook into main_swarm.py. Excludes boilerplate, logging, and checkpoint saving.

- **Transfer Learning Compatibility:**
  > Fully compatible. REINFORCE treats the policy network as a black box and optimizes its parameters via score-function gradients -- the SharedLotteryTransformer encoder is used as-is without modification. The existing transformer forward pass produces (main_logits, bonus_logits); REINFORCE wraps these with a sampling step and a gradient estimator. Fine-tuning strategy: freeze the shared encoder backbone and update only the per-lottery output heads during REINFORCE training to prevent catastrophic forgetting of the jointly-trained representation. Alternatively, train end-to-end with a reduced learning rate on the backbone. Both strategies are straightforward given the existing architecture.

- **Relevance Score:** 3
- **Specific Application In Project:**
  > REINFORCE would be implemented in Phase 2 as the Actor component of the Actor-Critic play generation system. Concretely: (1) the existing SharedLotteryTransformer acts as the policy network (Actor), producing logits over {1..50}; (2) at prediction time, instead of temperature-softmax sampling, Gumbel top-k is used to sample 7 distinct numbers; (3) after each weekly draw result is logged via main_swarm.py log, the reward (number of hits) is computed and the REINFORCE gradient update is applied to the output head weights; (4) the value baseline (running mean return per lottery) reduces gradient variance. This replaces the current static temperature-softmax prediction in src/predict.py with a learning loop. Starting lottery: LottoMax (most data, ~106 draws). The semi-bandit extension (per-number credit) is the highest-priority modification given the small dataset. A standalone src/reinforce.py module (~100 lines) is the natural integration point, called from main_swarm.py after each log event.

- **Integration Point:** Phase 2 (Actor-Critic play generation)
- **Replaces Or Extends:**
  > Extends -- replaces temperature-softmax sampling in src/predict.py with REINFORCE-guided sampling while keeping the SharedLotteryTransformer encoder and output heads unchanged. Adds a learning loop on top of existing architecture rather than replacing any component.

- **Existing Open Source Implementations:**
  > 1. PyTorch official REINFORCE example: pytorch/examples/reinforcement_learning/reinforce.py -- basic CartPole implementation, adaptable to multi-label outputs. 2. TorchRL (torchrl.objectives.ReinforceLoss) -- modular REINFORCE loss compatible with any policy network, available as of PyTorch 2.0+. 3. CleanRL (cleanrl/reinforce_continuous_action.py) -- minimal single-file implementations, highly readable. 4. Spinning Up in Deep RL (OpenAI, Achiam 2018) -- reference implementation in PyTorch with baseline. 5. No known open-source implementation specifically for k-of-N combinatorial multi-label REINFORCE as of 2025; the Gumbel top-k sampling extension must be added manually (trivial, ~5 lines using torch.distributions.Gumbel).

- **Benchmark Results:**
  > Williams (1992) reported convergence on XOR (~100 steps) and recurrent tasks. On discrete NLP tasks (text generation, summarization) REINFORCE with baseline achieves competitive BLEU/ROUGE but is consistently outperformed by PPO and RLOO by 1-3 BLEU points (Ranzato et al. 2015, Paulus et al. 2017). On combinatorial optimization tasks (TSP, knapsack) REINFORCE serves as a baseline that Pointer Networks and Attention Models improve upon by 15-30% tour length (Vinyals et al. 2015, Kool et al. 2019). On multi-label classification with RL reward shaping, REINFORCE is reported to match cross-entropy on medium datasets (1K-10K samples) but diverges on datasets below 500 samples (Schulman et al. ICLR 2016). No benchmark exists for REINFORCE on lottery-scale (n~100 draws) sparse-reward combinatorial prediction.

- **Notable Use Cases:**
  > 1. Neural combinatorial optimization: REINFORCE was the training algorithm for Pointer Networks (Vinyals et al. 2015) applied to TSP -- structurally similar to lottery (select k from N in a meaningful order). 2. Text summarization with discrete reward (ROUGE): Paulus et al. (2017) used REINFORCE to optimize non-differentiable ROUGE on document summarization -- analogous to optimizing non-differentiable hit count. 3. Multi-label drug compound selection: RL-based molecular generation (Jin et al. 2018) uses REINFORCE to select substructures from a fixed vocabulary -- similar k-of-N combinatorial structure. 4. Recommendation system top-k selection: REINFORCE applied to select k items from a product catalog (Ie et al., SlateQ 2019) -- closest structural analog to lottery play generation. 5. Caption generation with CIDEr reward (Rennie et al. 2017, Self-Critical Sequence Training) -- SCST is a REINFORCE variant with a test-time greedy baseline, now standard in image captioning.

### DeepAR: Probabilistic Forecasting with Autoregressive Recurrent Networks {#deepar-probabilistic-forecasting-with-autoregressive-recurrent-networks}

**Type:** Architecture | **Relevance:** 2/5 | **Integration:** Phase 3 (probabilistic models, DeepAR, N-BEATS)

**General**

- **Year:** 2020
- **Key Paper:**
  > Salinas et al., 'DeepAR: Probabilistic Forecasting with Autoregressive Recurrent Networks', International Journal of Forecasting, Vol. 36(3), 2020 (arXiv:1704.04110, first posted 2017; journal publication 2020)

- **Algorithm Description:**
  > DeepAR is a probabilistic forecasting model that learns a global autoregressive recurrent model across many related time series simultaneously. At each timestep t, an LSTM encodes the history of the time series (plus covariates) into a hidden state; that hidden state is then mapped to the parameters of a chosen likelihood distribution (e.g., Gaussian, Negative Binomial, Student-t) from which the next observation is sampled. During training, teacher forcing is used: the true past values are fed as inputs, and the loss is the negative log-likelihood of the observed next value under the predicted distribution. During inference, the model autoregressively samples from the predicted distribution at each step, feeding the sampled value back as input to produce full sample paths (Monte Carlo trajectories) over the forecast horizon. These sample paths are then aggregated to obtain quantile estimates or predictive intervals. The key innovation is learning a shared model across all series in the dataset, enabling knowledge transfer from data-rich series to data-poor ones and producing calibrated uncertainty estimates rather than point forecasts.

- **Action Space Handling:**
  > DeepAR is a forecasting architecture, not an RL policy, so it does not represent a discrete action space in the RL sense. The 'action' at each step is the emission of a distribution parameter vector (e.g., mean mu and standard deviation sigma for Gaussian, or alpha and beta for Negative Binomial). The model conditions on the previously sampled (or observed) value and the current hidden LSTM state to produce these parameters. Because the output is a continuous distribution rather than a discrete set, there is no combinatorial action space to manage — all uncertainty is captured in the predictive distribution. Adapting DeepAR to lottery (a discrete combinatorial output) would require replacing the likelihood head with a multi-label Bernoulli or Categorical distribution, which is a significant architectural departure from standard DeepAR.

- **How Combinatorial Output Is Formed:**
  > Standard DeepAR does not produce combinatorial outputs (sets of k distinct numbers from {1..N}). It produces a scalar or vector probabilistic forecast at each horizon step. To generate a lottery play, one would need to adapt DeepAR to output a probability vector of length N (pool size), then sample k numbers without replacement using the predicted probabilities as weights — this post-hoc sampling approach does not enforce inter-number correlations during training. An alternative adaptation is to use an autoregressive Bernoulli head (predict P(number_i is drawn) for each i conditioned on previous selections), but this is not native to DeepAR and would require significant custom engineering. The standard DeepAR output is a predictive distribution over the next scalar value, not a set.

- **Sequential Generation Strategy:**
  > DeepAR uses a recurrent autoregressive generation strategy over the time dimension: each forecast step is conditioned on all previously generated (sampled) values in the forecast horizon. This temporal autoregression means the model captures dependencies across draws over time (draw t depends on draw t-1, t-2, etc.) but does NOT natively model intra-draw correlations (which of the 7 numbers within a single draw co-occur). The generation is sequential along the time axis, not sequential within a single draw. To model within-draw structure, a separate multi-label output head would be needed, making DeepAR a partial solution: it handles temporal dynamics well but not intra-draw combinatorial structure.

- **Gradient Estimator Type:**
  > DeepAR uses maximum likelihood estimation with standard backpropagation through time (BPTT) via the LSTM. Because the output is a parametric distribution and the loss is the negative log-likelihood of the observed continuous value, there is no discrete sampling in the gradient path during training (teacher forcing is used, so true past values feed into the LSTM, not samples). No REINFORCE, Gumbel-softmax, or straight-through estimator is needed. This makes DeepAR training highly stable and low-variance compared to RL-based methods. The gradient estimator is simply standard BPTT — exact and unbiased for the chosen likelihood family.

- **Combinatorial Constraint Enforcement:**
  > DeepAR does not natively enforce combinatorial constraints (selecting exactly k distinct numbers from {1..N}). Its output is a parametric univariate or multivariate distribution, not a discrete set. Post-hoc constraint enforcement (e.g., sampling without replacement from predicted probabilities, or applying a top-k filter) must be added externally if DeepAR is used as a lottery play generator. This is a fundamental architectural mismatch: the model architecture does not reason about distinctness, cardinality constraints, or set membership during training. Any constraint enforcement is a post-processing step and cannot guarantee that the predicted distribution captures the true combinatorial structure of lottery draws.

- **Sample Efficiency Rating:**
  > medium-low — DeepAR's key strength is learning a shared model across many related time series, which dramatically improves sample efficiency when the dataset contains hundreds or thousands of series with thousands of timesteps each (the original M4/electricity dataset regime). In the lottery-nn context with only 3 lotteries and ~100 draws each (300 total data points across all series), DeepAR's cross-series transfer learning advantage essentially disappears — there are too few series and too few timesteps to learn meaningful global patterns. The LSTM requires enough historical context (SEQUENCE_LEN steps) to warm up its hidden state, further reducing the effective training data. Empirically, Amazon's internal DeepAR deployments typically involve thousands of product demand series; sub-200-sample single-series regimes are outside its validated operating range.

- **Regularization Built In:**
  > DeepAR includes several implicit and explicit regularization mechanisms: (1) LSTM dropout (applied between layers and to recurrent connections) — standard and configurable; (2) Likelihood-based loss acts as regularization through the distributional assumption (e.g., Gaussian likelihood penalizes overconfident predictions via the sigma term); (3) Global model training across multiple series acts as a form of multi-task regularization — the shared LSTM weights cannot overfit to any single series in isolation; (4) Lagging and scaling: DeepAR applies per-series scaling (dividing by the series mean) before training, which improves numerical stability and acts as a form of data normalization. On a dataset of only 3 series with 100 draws each, the multi-task regularization is weak, making explicit dropout and early stopping essential.

- **Known Failure Modes On Small Data:**
  > 1) Insufficient warm-up: LSTM hidden states require multiple past timesteps to encode meaningful context; with only 100 draws total and a window of 20, the effective number of non-overlapping training windows is ~5 per lottery — severely limiting gradient signal. 2) Distributional misspecification: if the chosen likelihood family (Gaussian) does not match the true data distribution (which for lottery numbers is discrete uniform within a fixed pool), the model produces poorly calibrated uncertainty estimates regardless of the sample size. 3) Over-reliance on covariates: DeepAR heavily leverages time-varying covariates (day of week, holiday flags, etc.) for demand forecasting; lottery draws have no meaningful calendar covariates, stripping away one of DeepAR's strongest inductive biases. 4) Mode averaging: with sparse data and a Gaussian likelihood, DeepAR tends to predict the mean of observed values rather than the multi-modal structure of lottery numbers. 5) Hidden state memorization: with very few training trajectories, the LSTM can memorize specific historical sequences rather than learning generalizable temporal patterns.

- **Reward Distribution Handling:**
  > DeepAR is designed specifically to handle skewed, non-Gaussian reward/value distributions by outputting a full parametric predictive distribution rather than a point estimate. It supports multiple likelihood families: Gaussian, Student-t (heavy tails), Negative Binomial (count data with overdispersion), and Beta (bounded data). For lottery prediction, the sparse reward structure (~95% zero-hit plays) is a fundamental mismatch: DeepAR treats the time series values as real-valued observations to forecast, not as sparse reward signals from plays. The 'forecasting' task — predicting which numbers will appear in the next draw — does not map cleanly to the reward-modeling task of Actor-Critic play generation. Adapting DeepAR to model the reward distribution over plays (rather than the number distribution over draws) would require a non-standard extension. Minimum draws for stable distributional estimation is empirically around 50-100 observations per series in published results.

- **Credit Assignment Granularity:**
  > DeepAR operates at the level of forecasting individual draw outcomes (which numbers appear), not at the level of assigning credit to individual plays or number selections within a play. There is no credit assignment mechanism in the RL sense — DeepAR does not have a policy that receives rewards; it has a likelihood model that predicts future observations. If adapted as a play scoring model (estimating the probability of each number appearing), it provides number-level scoring that could be used to derive play-level credit, but this is not native credit assignment — it is a prediction step that must be coupled with an external RL or scoring mechanism.

- **Diversity Mechanism:**
  > DeepAR generates diverse predictions naturally via Monte Carlo sampling: multiple sample paths are drawn from the predicted distribution at inference time, each producing a different forecast trajectory. For lottery play generation, this translates to sampling multiple independent plays from the predicted number probabilities — however, this is the same as temperature-softmax sampling and does not model inter-number correlations. True play diversity (generating distinct, non-overlapping sets) requires either: (a) post-hoc deduplication of sampled plays, (b) diversity-promoting sampling (DPP, or explicitly conditioning each new play on previously generated ones), or (c) a non-DeepAR mechanism. DeepAR itself does not provide any diversity mechanism beyond the stochasticity of its predictive distribution.

- **Offline Vs Online Rl Compatibility:**
  > DeepAR is fully compatible with offline training from a fixed historical dataset — this is its primary design mode. It trains on historical draw sequences without any environment interaction. At inference time, it generates probabilistic forecasts using only the historical context, with no requirement for new data. This makes it operationally compatible with the lottery-nn use case (one draw per week). However, DeepAR is a forecasting model, not an RL policy, so 'offline RL compatibility' is a category mismatch: DeepAR does not learn from rewards or optimize a policy. It would need to be used as a component within an RL pipeline (e.g., as a world model or as a number-probability predictor feeding into an Actor-Critic), not as a standalone offline RL algorithm.

- **Complexity:** medium
- **Key Dependencies:**
  > PyTorch (core); GluonTS (Amazon's official time series library, provides DeepAR estimator, data loaders, and evaluation metrics — pip install gluonts); MXNet (original DeepAR implementation; now deprecated in favor of PyTorch Lightning backend via gluonts[torch]); PyTorch Lightning (optional, used by GluonTS PyTorch backend); pandas, numpy (standard). For a custom PyTorch-only implementation: no additional dependencies beyond PyTorch itself.

- **Estimated Lines Of Code:**
  > 200-350 lines for a custom PyTorch DeepAR adapter — LSTM encoder (reusing SharedLotteryTransformer's embedding layer, ~50 lines), likelihood head (Gaussian or Negative Binomial parameter output, ~40 lines), training loop with teacher forcing (~80 lines), Monte Carlo inference sampler (~60 lines), integration adapter mapping DeepAR number-probability output to play generation format (~80 lines). Alternatively, 50-100 lines using GluonTS PyTorch backend with standard configuration.

- **Transfer Learning Compatibility:**
  > Partial compatibility — the SharedLotteryTransformer encoder produces sequence embeddings that could initialize the context vector for a DeepAR-style LSTM decoder, but the architectures are structurally different (Transformer vs. LSTM). The transformer backbone can serve as a feature extractor whose output feeds into a small DeepAR-style LSTM forecasting head, but the LSTM hidden state must still be initialized (typically from zero) and trained from scratch. Full weight transfer is not possible between a transformer encoder and an LSTM. A more compatible approach would be to add a probabilistic output head (Gaussian NLL loss, uncertainty estimation) directly to the existing SharedLotteryTransformer without introducing an LSTM at all — this would be 'DeepAR-inspired' rather than true DeepAR and would offer better transfer learning compatibility.

- **Relevance Score:** 2
- **Specific Application In Project:**
  > In lottery-nn, DeepAR would serve as a probabilistic number-frequency predictor: given the last 20 draws as input context, output a calibrated probability distribution over each of the 50 possible numbers (for LottoMax) appearing in the next draw. This replaces the current deterministic softmax output of the SharedLotteryTransformer with a full predictive distribution (e.g., Gaussian or Bernoulli likelihood per number). Concretely: (1) add a Gaussian NLL or Bernoulli BCE-with-uncertainty head to the SharedLotteryTransformer; (2) at inference time, sample multiple number-probability vectors from the predictive distribution (Monte Carlo dropout or explicit distribution sampling); (3) use these sampled probability vectors to generate candidate plays via weighted sampling without replacement; (4) the spread across samples provides uncertainty-aware play generation. This applies to all three lotteries via the existing per-lottery heads in model_swarm.py. Key limitation: DeepAR's multi-series transfer advantage is irrelevant with only 3 lotteries and 100 draws each — a simpler MC Dropout uncertainty head on the existing transformer would achieve comparable results with far less complexity. Recommended for Phase 3 only, after Actor-Critic and simpler probabilistic heads are validated.

- **Integration Point:** Phase 3 (probabilistic models, DeepAR, N-BEATS)
- **Replaces Or Extends:**
  > Extends the existing SharedLotteryTransformer per-lottery output heads — adds a probabilistic likelihood head (distributional output) alongside or replacing the existing BCEWithLogitsLoss head, enabling uncertainty-aware play generation without replacing the transformer backbone

- **Existing Open Source Implementations:**
  > 1) GluonTS (Amazon, official): github.com/awslabs/gluonts — DeepAREstimator available in both MXNet (legacy) and PyTorch backends; pip install gluonts[torch]; most complete and production-tested implementation; 2) pts (Probabilistic Time Series): github.com/zalandoresearch/pytorch-ts — clean PyTorch-native DeepAR implementation; 3) DeepAR PyTorch reimplementation: github.com/jingw2/demand_forecast — lightweight standalone PyTorch DeepAR; 4) Darts library: github.com/unit8co/darts — includes DeepAR via its RNNModel with likelihood support; pip install darts; 5) Nixtla's neuralforecast: github.com/Nixtla/neuralforecast — DeepAR included alongside N-BEATS, N-HiTS; pip install neuralforecast. GluonTS[torch] is the recommended starting point.

- **Benchmark Results:**
  > Salinas et al. 2020 (IJF): DeepAR achieves state-of-the-art ND (normalized deviation) and rho-50/rho-90 quantile losses on Electricity (ND=0.054, vs. ARIMA 0.154), Traffic, Parts, and M4 Competition datasets. Outperforms Prophet, ETS, ARIMA, and DeepState on 7 of 8 tested datasets. M4 Competition 2018: DeepAR-based entries achieve top-5 results on monthly and quarterly series but underperform on weekly/daily series where pattern complexity exceeds data density. Makridakis M5 Competition 2020: DeepAR variants place competitively but are outperformed by LightGBM and ensemble methods on the M5's 42,840-series dataset, suggesting that for large structured datasets tree-based methods remain competitive. Monash Time Series Forecasting Repository (Godahewa et al. 2021) benchmark: DeepAR ranks 3rd-5th on most univariate series but shows performance degradation on series shorter than 100 observations. No published benchmark directly evaluates DeepAR on lottery draw prediction or similar combinatorial discrete tasks.

- **Notable Use Cases:**
  > 1) Amazon product demand forecasting (primary domain): millions of product-level demand series with thousands of observations each — the regime for which DeepAR was designed and where it excels; 2) Energy load forecasting: electricity consumption prediction with clear periodicity and multiple related series — GluonTS tutorials use electricity as primary benchmark; 3) Retail inventory optimization: Salinas et al.'s own application — predicting intermittent demand (sparse, with many zero observations), which has some structural similarity to lottery's sparse hit distribution; 4) Financial time series (experimental): stock price prediction with uncertainty bands — limited success due to non-stationarity; 5) Healthcare: patient vital sign prediction with uncertainty — similar short-series, high-noise regime to lottery draws. None of these use cases involve combinatorial discrete set generation, which is the critical gap for lottery-nn application.


**Uncertain Fields**

- data_regime_evidence
- convergence_guarantees_small_n
