### Diffusion Models for Discrete Combinatorial Generation (D3PM, Discrete Flow Matching) {#diffusion-models-for-discrete-combinatorial-generation-d3pm-discrete-flow-matching}

**Type:** Architecture | **Relevance:** 2/5 | **Integration:** Phase 3 (probabilistic models, DeepAR, N-BEATS)

**General**

- **Year:** 2021
- **Key Paper:**
  > Austin et al., 'Structured Denoising Diffusion Models in Discrete State-Spaces (D3PM)', NeurIPS 2021; Gat et al., 'Discrete Flow Matching', NeurIPS 2024; Campbell et al., 'A Continuous Time Framework for Discrete Denoising Models', NeurIPS 2022

- **Algorithm Description:**
  > Discrete diffusion models learn to generate structured discrete objects by training a neural network to reverse a corruption (noising) process defined over discrete state spaces. In the forward process, a clean discrete object x0 is progressively corrupted over T timesteps by applying a categorical transition matrix Qt (e.g., randomly masking tokens, uniform noise, or absorbing-state masking) until the object is fully noised into a known prior distribution. The reverse process trains a denoising network p_theta(x_{t-1} | x_t) to predict the original clean object from any noisy intermediate state, parameterized as a distribution over the discrete vocabulary at each position. D3PM (Austin et al. 2021) generalizes this to arbitrary absorbing, uniform, and token-specific transition matrices. Discrete Flow Matching (Gat et al. 2024) further simplifies training by framing the reverse process as learning a conditional flow that interpolates between noise and data along straight paths in probability simplex space, achieving lower variance training and requiring fewer denoising steps at inference. At generation time, the model starts from pure noise and iteratively applies the learned reverse transitions to produce a clean discrete object.

- **Action Space Handling:**
  > Discrete diffusion models handle the action space as a joint distribution over all positions simultaneously at each denoising step — the model predicts p(x0 | x_t) for all positions in parallel, then samples x_{t-1} from the posterior q(x_{t-1} | x_t, x0). This is fundamentally different from autoregressive generation: all k numbers in the output set are generated jointly at each reverse step, with inter-position correlations modeled through the shared neural backbone (typically a transformer). For combinatorial set generation, each of the N ball positions is treated as a binary token (selected / not selected), making the state space {0,1}^N. The denoising network outputs logits over {0,1} for each of the N positions at each reverse step, and the joint sample at step T=0 yields the final multi-hot set.

- **How Combinatorial Output Is Formed:**
  > The lottery set {k numbers from 1..N} is represented as a binary multi-hot vector of length N. The forward process corrupts this vector by randomly flipping bits (uniform diffusion) or masking selected positions to an absorb state (absorbing diffusion). The denoising network p_theta(x0 | x_t) predicts the clean multi-hot vector from any noisy intermediate. At the final reverse step, the predicted probabilities for each position are thresholded or top-k selected: specifically, the N Bernoulli probabilities p_i from the final denoising step are used to select exactly k numbers via deterministic top-k argmax or by Gumbel top-k sampling, enforcing the cardinality constraint post-hoc. Alternatively, the number of selected bits can be guided during denoising by conditioning on a count token embedding. The distinctness constraint is automatically satisfied because each of the N positions is a separate binary variable.

- **Sequential Generation Strategy:**
  > Joint (non-autoregressive) diffusion process — all N positions of the multi-hot output vector are denoised simultaneously across T reverse steps. At each reverse step t, the transformer backbone attends over all N positions at once and predicts the clean vector x0, from which x_{t-1} is sampled jointly. This is neither fully autoregressive (one number at a time) nor flat single-shot (one forward pass): it is an iterative joint refinement over T steps (typically T=10 to 1000). Inter-number correlations are captured because the transformer's self-attention over all N positions at each step allows the model to reason about the full set jointly. Discrete Flow Matching (Gat 2024) achieves similar quality with T as low as 10-50 steps, making inference fast even for large N.

- **Gradient Estimator Type:**
  > Discrete diffusion training does not require discrete gradient estimators — the denoising network is trained with a standard cross-entropy loss on the predicted clean tokens x0 given noisy input x_t. Because the loss is computed on the predicted distribution over discrete states (not through a discrete sample), gradients flow directly through the softmax output without any REINFORCE, Gumbel-softmax, or straight-through trick. This is a key advantage: the training objective is a proper variational lower bound (ELBO) on the log-likelihood, minimized with standard backpropagation. Discrete Flow Matching replaces the ELBO with a flow-matching regression loss (predicting the clean state from any interpolated state), which is even simpler and avoids KL divergence terms entirely. Neither estimator introduces variance from discrete sampling during training.

- **Combinatorial Constraint Enforcement:**
  > The cardinality constraint (exactly k numbers selected) is enforced post-hoc at the final inference step. During denoising, the model freely predicts probabilities for all N binary positions without an explicit cardinality constraint. At T=0, the final predicted probabilities p_1,...,p_N are converted to a valid set by selecting the top-k positions by probability (deterministic top-k), or by sampling k positions without replacement using these probabilities as weights (stochastic top-k / Gumbel-top-k). Distinctness is structurally guaranteed because positions are independent binary variables. For stronger constraint enforcement during denoising, classifier guidance can steer intermediate samples toward the k-hot manifold, or the count k can be provided as a conditioning signal. No rejection sampling is needed.

- **Sample Efficiency Rating:**
  > medium — Discrete diffusion models are generative models trained to fit p(data), not reward-maximizing policies, which gives them an advantage on small datasets: they do not require reward signals or online environment interaction. They learn the underlying distribution of historical draws directly from data. However, fitting a good diffusion model on fewer than 200 training examples is challenging because the denoising network (typically a transformer) can overfit the small training set, producing samples that replicate training draws rather than generalize. The Discrete Flow Matching formulation (Gat 2024) with its simpler regression objective and fewer steps may generalize better than full D3PM on small datasets. Compared to policy-gradient methods (low sample efficiency) discrete diffusion is relatively more data-efficient, but compared to lightweight methods like REINFORCE with good baselines it requires more model capacity and training time.

- **Regularization Built In:**
  > The diffusion framework provides several implicit regularization mechanisms: (1) Noise schedule regularization — by training on all noise levels simultaneously (from t=1 to T), the model sees each training example in hundreds of different corrupted forms, effectively multiplying the dataset by T, which is a strong implicit data augmentation on small datasets. (2) Masked/absorbing diffusion naturally acts as a dropout-like regularizer, forcing the model to predict tokens from partial observations. (3) The variational ELBO objective includes a KL penalty term against the prior p(xT), which prevents the learned reverse process from diverging. (4) Discrete Flow Matching does not have a KL term but benefits from the same data augmentation effect across interpolation levels. Explicit weight decay and dropout can be added to the denoising transformer network.

- **Known Failure Modes On Small Data:**
  > 1) Memorization: with <200 training examples, the denoising network can memorize the training draws, generating plays that are near-duplicates of historical draws rather than novel combinations — especially problematic for absorbing diffusion where the model can trivially learn to output training examples from the [MASK] state. 2) Cardinality drift: post-hoc top-k selection can break down if the model's predicted probabilities become poorly calibrated (many positions near 0.5), making the top-k boundary unstable and effectively random. 3) Mode collapse: the learned distribution can collapse to a small number of high-probability multi-hot patterns if the training data has strong repeat patterns. 4) Overcorrection in few steps: Discrete Flow Matching with very few reverse steps on small data can produce incoherent intermediate states that the model cannot recover from, leading to high variance in the final generated sets.

- **Credit Assignment Granularity:**
  > In the standard generative setting, discrete diffusion models have no credit assignment mechanism — they learn from draw-level supervision (the full historical draw is the training signal). If used with reward-weighted training, credit is assigned at the play level (the entire multi-hot vector is scored as a unit). However, because discrete diffusion operates position-by-position in the denoising network, it is possible to inject number-level credit by constructing per-position reward signals: each position i receives reward proportional to how often number i has historically co-occurred with high-reward plays. This enables semi-bandit-style credit assignment within the diffusion framework, but requires custom reward shaping and is not the standard approach in any published paper.

- **Diversity Mechanism:**
  > Diversity is structurally built into the stochastic reverse diffusion process: starting from independently sampled noise at x_T, each reverse chain produces a different sample even from the same trained model. Multiple plays can be generated by running T independent chains. The temperature of sampling can be controlled by scaling the logits of the denoising network's output (high temperature = more uniform = more diverse plays, low temperature = more peaked = plays closer to the highest-probability combinations). Discrete Flow Matching supports temperature scaling naturally by scaling the interpolation endpoint. Unlike mode-seeking methods (argmax decoding), diffusion models structurally avoid collapsing to a single play as long as the noise initialization varies between chains.

- **Offline Vs Online Rl Compatibility:**
  > Highly compatible with offline training — discrete diffusion is a purely generative model trained on fixed historical data with no environment interaction required. The entire training procedure uses only the fixed draws.csv dataset without any need for live draw results or online exploration. This is a significant operational advantage for lottery-nn where draws occur at most twice weekly. The reward-weighted variant also operates offline: all historical draws and their rewards are available upfront. No simulator, online environment, or replay buffer is needed. The main limitation is that the model cannot adapt to new reward signals faster than the generative model can be retrained on updated data.

- **Complexity:** high
- **Key Dependencies:**
  > Core PyTorch only for the denoising network; no required external libraries beyond what lottery-nn already uses. Optional: huggingface diffusers (supports some discrete diffusion variants as of 2024), or mdlm (github.com/kuleshov-group/mdlm) for Masked Diffusion Language Models which are closely related. The Discrete Flow Matching implementation by Gat et al. 2024 is available at github.com/andrew-cr/discrete_flow_models. No stable-baselines3, Pyro, or torchrl needed.

- **Estimated Lines Of Code:**
  > 500-700 lines — noise schedule definition and forward process (Qt matrices, ~80 lines), denoising transformer head on top of SharedLotteryTransformer (~100 lines), ELBO/flow-matching loss computation (~100 lines), reverse sampling loop with T steps (~80 lines), top-k post-processing to enforce cardinality (~40 lines), reward-weighted training variant (~80 lines), classifier guidance implementation (~100 lines), integration into main_swarm.py predict flow (~80 lines), unit tests (~60 lines)

- **Transfer Learning Compatibility:**
  > High compatibility — the SharedLotteryTransformer encoder from Phase 1 can be used as the backbone of the denoising network p_theta(x0 | x_t). The noisy multi-hot vector x_t (plus noise level embedding t) is fed as input to the transformer, and the output head predicts the clean multi-hot probabilities x0. The lottery_id embedding already in the shared backbone handles the per-lottery conditioning. Only a lightweight denoising head (small MLP mapping transformer output to N-dimensional Bernoulli logits) and a noise-level embedding (sinusoidal or learned, ~64 dim) need to be added on top of the frozen or fine-tuned Phase 1 checkpoint. This is structurally analogous to how diffusion models in vision are built on top of pretrained U-Net/ViT backbones.

- **Relevance Score:** 2
- **Specific Application In Project:**
  > In lottery-nn, a discrete diffusion model would serve as a generative model of lottery draws, used to sample novel plausible play candidates that reflect the statistical structure of historical draws. Concretely: (1) Train D3PM or Discrete Flow Matching on the joint training set of all three lotteries (using lottery_id conditioning from the existing SharedLotteryTransformer), learning p(draw | lottery_id) as a denoising objective on multi-hot vectors of size N. (2) At prediction time, run T=50-100 reverse diffusion steps starting from noise, then apply top-k selection to produce k distinct numbers. (3) Optionally use reward-weighted training or classifier guidance to bias generation toward historically profitable number patterns. This would replace or augment the current temperature-softmax sampling in src/predict.py. The main obstacle is that the training dataset (~100 draws per lottery) is well below what discrete diffusion models are typically validated on, making overfitting a serious risk. Recommended for Phase 3 experimentation after Actor-Critic and bandit methods are validated in Phase 2.

- **Integration Point:** Phase 3 (probabilistic models, DeepAR, N-BEATS)
- **Replaces Or Extends:**
  > Replaces temperature-softmax sampling in src/predict.py — the diffusion model's reverse process becomes the play generator, producing complete multi-hot sets via iterative denoising rather than sampling each number independently from the transformer's final-layer logits

- **Existing Open Source Implementations:**
  > 1) D3PM reference code: github.com/google-research/google-research/tree/master/d3pm — original TensorFlow implementation from Austin et al. 2021, not PyTorch-native; 2) Discrete Flow Matching: github.com/andrew-cr/discrete_flow_models — PyTorch, Gat et al. 2024, clean and minimal; 3) MDLM (Masked Diffusion Language Model): github.com/kuleshov-group/mdlm — PyTorch, absorbing diffusion for sequences, well-maintained as of 2024; 4) DiffusionBERT: github.com/Hzfinfdu/Diffusion-BERT — absorbing discrete diffusion for text, PyTorch; 5) huggingface/diffusers: has discrete diffusion support as of 2024 for some architectures. The MDLM codebase is the most practical starting point for adapting to multi-hot vector generation given its clean PyTorch design and absorbing-state formulation which suits binary multi-hot inputs.

- **Benchmark Results:**
  > D3PM (Austin et al. NeurIPS 2021): On text8 character-level language modeling, D3PM with absorbing diffusion achieves 1.45 bits/char, competitive with autoregressive models. On CIFAR-10 image generation, D3PM absorbing achieves FID ~7.3 (vs. ~3.2 for DDPM). Campbell et al. (NeurIPS 2022) continuous-time discrete diffusion: achieves state-of-the-art on protein sequence generation (perplexity) with efficient training. Discrete Flow Matching (Gat et al. NeurIPS 2024): outperforms D3PM on text generation benchmarks with 10x fewer function evaluations (steps), achieving comparable quality to autoregressive models on language tasks with T=32 steps. No benchmark results exist for lottery-style combinatorial set generation or datasets below 200 training samples.

- **Notable Use Cases:**
  > 1) Text generation (primary domain): character and token-level language modeling — demonstrates ability to generate coherent discrete sequences, structurally analogous to generating ordered or unordered number sequences; 2) Protein sequence design: generating novel protein sequences with desired properties from limited wet-lab data (~hundreds of labeled examples) — the closest analog to small-data lottery generation; 3) Molecular graph generation: discrete diffusion over graph adjacency matrices and atom types, where the combinatorial structure (which atoms are connected) is analogous to selecting which numbers co-occur; 4) Code generation: discrete diffusion for program synthesis with structured output constraints; 5) Drug SMILES generation: discrete diffusion on chemical formula strings with structural validity constraints — analogous to the cardinality constraint in lottery selection

### MCTS-Guided Policy Improvement for Combinatorial Outputs {#mcts-guided-policy-improvement-for-combinatorial-outputs}

**Type:** RL Algorithm | **Relevance:** 2/5 | **Integration:** Phase 3 (probabilistic models, DeepAR, N-BEATS) — too complex and data-hungry for Phase 2 given current dataset size. Could be revisited as Phase 2 late addition if dataset grows to 300+ draws.

**General**

- **Year:** 2024
- **Key Paper:**
  > Wang, R., et al. (2024). EfficientZero V2: Mastering Discrete and Continuous Control with Limited Data. arXiv:2403.00564. Preprint (no venue listed as of Aug 2025). Primary predecessor: Schrittwieser, J., et al. (2020). Mastering Atari, Go, Chess and Shogi by Planning with a Learned Model (MuZero). Nature 588, 604-609.

- **Algorithm Description:**
  > MCTS-guided policy improvement uses Monte Carlo Tree Search as a planning oracle that refines a neural network policy's action proposals via lookahead simulation. The policy network (actor) proposes candidate action distributions, which seed MCTS rollouts from the current state; the visit-count statistics from MCTS simulations produce an improved target distribution (pi_MCTS) that is typically sharper and better than the raw network output. The actor is then distilled toward pi_MCTS via KL-divergence minimization, completing the policy improvement step. A learned world model predicts next states and rewards so that MCTS can simulate trajectories without real environment interaction. EfficientZero V2 extends this framework to both discrete and continuous action spaces with explicit data-efficiency improvements: priority replay, Siamese self-supervised consistency loss, and careful hyperparameter scheduling allow training from as few as 100K environment frames rather than the millions required by original MuZero.

- **Action Space Handling:**
  > Standard MCTS-guided policy improvement operates over a flat discrete action space (single categorical choice per step). For combinatorial set outputs (choose k distinct items from {1..N}), the action space is expanded to one of two representations: (1) Factored autoregressive MCTS — each of the k selection steps is a separate MCTS node level, and the search tree has depth k; at step t, the policy masks already-selected items and MCTS searches over the remaining N-t candidates. (2) Joint exponential space — all C(N,k) subsets are enumerated as flat actions; impractical for N=50, k=7 (approximately 99 million combinations). The autoregressive factored approach is the only tractable option for lottery-scale N and k, and maps naturally onto sequential MCTS tree expansion with masking at each ply.

- **How Combinatorial Output Is Formed:**
  > Under the autoregressive MCTS formulation, each play of k numbers is built step-by-step: at step t (t=0..k-1), the policy network outputs logits over {1..N} with already-selected indices masked to -inf; MCTS runs from this state using the policy as a prior, accumulates visit counts via UCB1/PUCT exploration, and the visit-count distribution over unmasked candidates becomes the improved target. The selected number is sampled (or argmax-selected) from the MCTS visit distribution; the chosen number is appended to the running set and the next step begins with an updated mask. After k steps, exactly k distinct numbers have been selected. Alternatively, if a learned world model is available, MCTS can internally simulate multi-step lookahead of the remaining selections using the world model's predicted states, evaluating candidate subsets before committing to any choice.

- **Sequential Generation Strategy:**
  > MCTS-guided policy improvement is inherently sequential/autoregressive when applied to combinatorial outputs: each number selection is one MCTS decision step, conditioned on all previously selected numbers via the accumulated mask. This means inter-number correlations are fully modeled — the policy can learn that selecting ball 7 at step 1 makes ball 14 or ball 21 more or less likely at step 2 (e.g., based on historical co-occurrence patterns in draw data). This is a strict improvement over flat joint multi-hot generation which treats each ball independently. The cost is k forward passes through both the policy network and the world model per play generation, and a search tree of depth k per play at inference time.

- **Gradient Estimator Type:**
  > MCTS-guided policy improvement does not rely on REINFORCE or reparameterization gradient estimators in the standard sense. The policy is trained via supervised distillation: the MCTS visit-count distribution pi_MCTS serves as a soft target, and the loss is a KL divergence (or cross-entropy) between pi_MCTS and the current policy output pi_theta. This is a direct regression target rather than a sampled policy gradient — gradients flow through a differentiable cross-entropy loss, making training stable and low-variance compared to REINFORCE. The value function is trained via TD-learning (n-step returns from MCTS value estimates). This distillation-based update is the key reason MCTS-guided methods are more stable than raw policy gradient on small data: the MCTS target provides a low-variance, planning-improved supervision signal rather than a high-variance Monte Carlo reward gradient.

- **Combinatorial Constraint Enforcement:**
  > Combinatorial constraints (exactly k distinct from {1..N}) are enforced via autoregressive masking within the MCTS tree: at each tree node representing step t, the valid action set is restricted to {1..N} minus already-selected indices. MCTS expansion only considers valid (unmasked) actions, and the policy prior is renormalized over valid candidates before computing PUCT scores. This guarantees exact k selection with no duplicates by construction. No post-hoc rejection sampling is needed — the constraint is baked into the MCTS action space at every node. The world model also only needs to predict transitions over valid actions, simplifying the state representation (the current partial set plus the step index t).

- **Sample Efficiency Rating:**
  > low-to-medium. MCTS-guided methods like EfficientZero V2 dramatically improve sample efficiency over model-free RL (PPO, DQN require millions of frames; EfficientZero V2 achieves comparable performance in 100K frames on Atari). However, '100K frames' still far exceeds 100-200 lottery draws. The core challenge is that the learned world model requires sufficient state-action-reward transitions to generalize — with only 100-200 draws, the world model will overfit severely and produce hallucinated rollouts that mislead MCTS. If the world model is replaced by a hand-designed lottery simulator (historical draw replay as environment), MCTS-guided improvement becomes more feasible but degrades to replaying historical data, eliminating the lookahead advantage. Rating: low for fully learned world model on 100-200 draws; medium if world model is simplified to a historical-replay oracle.

- **Regularization Built In:**
  > Several regularization mechanisms are inherent: (1) MCTS smoothing — MCTS visit counts naturally smooth the policy target, distributing probability across multiple plausible actions rather than collapsing to a single argmax; this acts as implicit regularization against mode collapse. (2) Dirichlet noise at MCTS root — standard AlphaZero/MuZero adds Dirichlet noise to the root prior to encourage exploration during MCTS, providing stochasticity that prevents deterministic policy collapse. (3) Self-supervised consistency loss (EfficientZero V2) — a Siamese representation consistency objective regularizes the encoder, critical for small datasets where supervised signal alone is insufficient. (4) L2 weight decay on the policy and value networks. (5) Value target clipping and n-step return truncation prevent outlier rewards from dominating. (6) KL-based policy distillation loss is inherently soft and avoids hard argmax targets, providing label smoothing similar to knowledge distillation.

- **Known Failure Modes On Small Data:**
  > Critical failure modes with n < 200 draws: (1) World model hallucination — a learned transition model trained on < 200 draws produces unreliable state predictions; MCTS lookahead through a broken world model yields confidently wrong value estimates that mislead the policy distillation step. (2) MCTS depth vs. data tension — deep MCTS trees require many reliable rollouts; with few training samples, simulation quality degrades rapidly with lookahead depth. (3) Value function overfit — the critic memorizes return statistics of the small training set; MCTS backpropagation propagates these overfitted values upward, amplifying overfit through lookahead. (4) Exploration collapse — without sufficient experience, MCTS explores poorly and converges to a narrow set of candidate actions; Dirichlet noise alone cannot compensate for severely limited training data. (5) Computational overhead without benefit — MCTS is expensive per inference step; on small data with an unreliable world model, the computational cost is not justified by improvement over simpler policy gradient methods.

- **Reward Distribution Handling:**
  > MCTS-guided methods handle sparse rewards through value bootstrapping and n-step returns within the search tree: MCTS propagates value estimates up the tree via backpropagation, effectively computing multi-step return estimates. For a lottery reward structure (95% zero-reward plays): (1) Reward scaling — raw hit counts (0-7) are typically normalized; near-all-zero rewards cause value estimates to collapse near zero, providing weak gradient signal. (2) Distributional value (MuZero Reanalyze variant) — a distributional critic can be used instead of a scalar value to capture the shape of the sparse return distribution, retaining information about the rare high-reward events. (3) Priority replay — EfficientZero V2 uses prioritized experience replay that over-samples rare high-reward transitions; this is directly beneficial for lottery-like sparse rewards, ensuring the few positive-reward draws are replayed frequently. (4) Minimum draws for stable learning: MCTS-guided methods need a sufficient number of non-zero reward samples to calibrate the value function; with only 5-10 non-zero reward plays in 200 draws, value function training is severely data-limited. Practical minimum: 500+ draws for stable value learning with MCTS.

- **Credit Assignment Granularity:**
  > Standard MCTS assigns reward at the episode/play level — a single scalar reward is backpropagated through the search tree at terminal nodes. For lottery application: (1) Play-level credit is default: the reward (number of hits, 0 to k) is assigned to the entire k-number play and propagated backward through the k-step MCTS tree. (2) Step-level credit within MCTS: because the autoregressive MCTS tree selects numbers one at a time, each selection step can in principle receive intermediate rewards. A shaped intermediate reward (e.g., +1 if the number selected at step t appears in the actual draw, 0 otherwise) provides number-level credit assignment directly within the MCTS lookahead, similar to semi-bandit feedback. This is a significant advantage over flat play-level methods: MCTS can naturally incorporate number-level feedback without architectural changes, simply by providing step-wise rewards during tree expansion. (3) This makes MCTS-guided generation one of the few methods that can natively exploit number-level semi-bandit feedback in the combinatorial setting.

- **Diversity Mechanism:**
  > MCTS generates diverse plays through several mechanisms: (1) Dirichlet root noise — at the root of each MCTS tree, Dirichlet-distributed noise (alpha=0.3, epsilon=0.25 in AlphaZero) is added to the prior policy, ensuring stochastic exploration across different number combinations for each play generated. (2) Temperature-scaled visit count sampling — after MCTS, action selection uses a temperature parameter tau to sample from visit counts rather than taking argmax; tau=1 early in training transitions to tau->0 for exploitation at inference. (3) Multiple independent MCTS trees — generating N plays requires N separate MCTS search instances from the same root state; each tree accumulates independent random noise and produces a different play. (4) Explicit play diversity constraint (custom extension) — a diversity reward penalizing overlap between simultaneously generated plays can be incorporated by treating batch play generation as a multi-agent MCTS problem. The Dirichlet noise and temperature combination is the primary diversity mechanism and is well-established in AlphaZero/MuZero literature.

- **Offline Vs Online Rl Compatibility:**
  > MCTS-guided policy improvement with a learned world model is primarily designed for online or near-online settings where the world model can be continually updated from real environment interactions. Compatibility with offline lottery-nn constraints: (1) Offline via model-based RL — the world model can be trained entirely on historical draws and frozen; MCTS then simulates rollouts using this fixed model without additional real draws. This enables offline policy improvement but inherits all world model overfit problems on small data. (2) MuZero Reanalyze (Schrittwieser et al. 2021) allows replaying historical data through the current world model to generate fresh value/policy targets — this is a form of offline MCTS that is more data-efficient. (3) Historical replay as simulator — treating the draw history as a finite replay environment and replaying MCTS from past states; fully offline but limited by the 100-200 historical draws. Verdict: MCTS-guided methods can be adapted to offline settings but require a reliable world model; with 100-200 draws and a weekly draw cycle, the world model training data is insufficient for reliable offline MCTS. Operationally feasible only if the world model is simplified or replaced by domain knowledge (e.g., a frequency-weighted lottery simulator).

- **Complexity:** high
- **Key Dependencies:**
  > PyTorch (already present). No standard off-the-shelf MCTS-guided RL library handles combinatorial set outputs natively. Useful libraries: (1) mctx (Google DeepMind's JAX MCTS library — JAX, not PyTorch, port required); (2) muzero-general (PyTorch MuZero implementation, GitHub); (3) torchrl (Meta's RL library with some MCTS support); (4) Custom MCTS implementation is typically needed (~300-500 lines). Additional: numpy, scipy for Dirichlet sampling. No pip-installable complete solution as of 2025.

- **Estimated Lines Of Code:**
  > 700-1000 lines of new code: MCTS tree data structure with PUCT scoring (~200 lines), world model (transition + reward + representation networks, ~200 lines), autoregressive combinatorial action masking within MCTS (~100 lines), policy distillation training loop with value targets (~150 lines), Dirichlet noise and temperature sampling utilities (~50 lines), replay buffer with priority sampling (~150 lines), integration with SharedLotteryTransformer backbone (~100 lines). Excludes evaluation harness and logging.

- **Transfer Learning Compatibility:**
  > Medium-to-high compatibility with the existing SharedLotteryTransformer. The encoder serves as the representation network (h_theta) that maps draw sequences to latent states for MCTS. The existing actor head can seed the MCTS prior policy. However, MCTS-guided methods additionally require a dynamics network (g_theta) that predicts the next latent state given current state and action — this is entirely new and cannot be derived from the current architecture. The reward prediction head is also new. The encoder and policy head weights from the pretrained backbone provide a strong initialization for the representation and prior networks, significantly reducing the training burden. Overall: the backbone is reusable for 2 out of 4 required networks; the dynamics and reward prediction networks must be trained from scratch on the lottery draw data, which is the highest-risk component given data scarcity.

- **Relevance Score:** 2
- **Specific Application In Project:**
  > In lottery-nn, MCTS-guided policy improvement would be used in Phase 3 (or a late Phase 2 extension) as a planning-enhanced play generator. Concretely: (1) The SharedLotteryTransformer encoder becomes the representation network h_theta encoding the sequence of recent draws into a latent state z. (2) A lightweight dynamics network g_theta predicts the next latent state z' given z and the number selected at each MCTS step t (simulating 'what if we chose ball i next?'). (3) A reward predictor r_theta estimates the expected hit count for a given state-action pair. (4) At play generation time, for each of 5 plays: run MCTS from the current state with k=7 plies deep, masking previously selected balls at each ply; sample from the visit-count distribution for each step. (5) After each real draw, MuZero Reanalyze replays historical draws through the current world model to generate updated policy/value targets and retrain. The primary benefit over simpler methods (REINFORCE, PPO) is the lookahead capability: MCTS can evaluate candidate number combinations multiple steps ahead, potentially discovering synergistic combinations (co-occurrence patterns) that one-step policy gradient methods miss. However, given only 100-200 draws, the dynamics network is the critical bottleneck — it must generalize from very few transitions. Recommended: prototype first with a simplified hand-designed simulator (frequency-weighted random draw as 'world model') before training a learned dynamics network.

- **Integration Point:** Phase 3 (probabilistic models, DeepAR, N-BEATS) — too complex and data-hungry for Phase 2 given current dataset size. Could be revisited as Phase 2 late addition if dataset grows to 300+ draws.
- **Replaces Or Extends:**
  > Extends the existing SharedLotteryTransformer by adding a dynamics network, reward prediction head, and MCTS planning loop on top. Does not replace the encoder backbone. Replaces temperature-softmax sampling in src/predict.py with MCTS-driven play generation. Extends rather than replaces the Actor-Critic framework — MCTS provides the policy improvement operator instead of PPO's clipped surrogate.

- **Benchmark Results:**
  > EfficientZero V2 benchmarks (Wang et al. 2024): Achieves human-normalized median score of ~194% on Atari-100K (100K frames budget), compared to ~105% for DreamerV3 and ~153% for IRIS at the same budget. On continuous control (DMControl suite at 100K steps), EfficientZero V2 achieves state-of-the-art on 12/16 tasks. Original MuZero (Schrittwieser et al. 2020): superhuman on Go (2840 Elo), Chess, Shogi, and Atari — with millions of self-play games. AlphaZero (Silver et al. 2018): combinatorial game (Go, Chess, Shogi) policy improvement via MCTS with millions of games. Key limitation for lottery-nn applicability: all published MCTS-guided results assume a reliable simulator (perfect for board games, learned but validated for Atari); no published results on combinatorial set selection from {1..N} with k distinct elements and n < 500 training samples.

- **Notable Use Cases:**
  > 1. Board games (AlphaZero, MuZero) — MCTS as policy improvement operator for combinatorial game trees (Go has branching factor ~250, qualitatively similar to choosing from N=50 balls). 2. Combinatorial optimization with MCTS — MCTS applied to TSP and graph coloring, where each step selects one node from remaining candidates (structurally identical to autoregressive ball selection with masking). 3. Drug discovery / molecular design — MCTS guided by a GNN policy for molecular fragment assembly, selecting chemical building blocks sequentially from a fixed vocabulary; sparse reward (biological activity), combinatorial output — the most structurally similar use case to lottery-nn. 4. Chip placement (Mirhoseini et al. 2021, Nature) — RL with lookahead for combinatorial placement decisions; demonstrated feasibility of RL on combinatorial design with limited labeled data via reward shaping. 5. Recommendation slate generation — MCTS applied to sequential item selection for k-of-N slates, with diversity rewards; structural analog to generating multiple lottery plays with diversity constraints.

### Temporal Fusion Transformer (TFT) {#temporal-fusion-transformer-tft}

**Type:** Architecture | **Relevance:** 2/5 | **Integration:** Backbone (replace SharedLotteryTransformer)

**General**

- **Year:** 2021
- **Key Paper:**
  > Lim, B., Arik, S. O., Loeff, N., & Pfister, T. (2021). Temporal Fusion Transformers for Interpretable Multi-horizon Time Series Forecasting. International Journal of Forecasting, 37(4), 1748-1764. arXiv:1912.09363. Earlier version presented at NeurIPS 2019 workshop.

- **Algorithm Description:**
  > TFT is a multi-horizon time series forecasting architecture that explicitly separates and processes three types of inputs: static (time-invariant) covariates, known future inputs, and observed past inputs. It uses Gated Residual Networks (GRN) with ELU activations and gating mechanisms to suppress irrelevant inputs, a Variable Selection Network (VSN) to identify informative features at each timestep, and a sequence-to-sequence LSTM encoder-decoder for local temporal processing. Temporal self-attention (interpreted as multi-head attention across the time axis) then captures long-range dependencies. Quantile regression heads produce prediction intervals rather than point estimates. The architecture was specifically designed for heterogeneous multi-entity forecasting where entities have different static properties (e.g., store type, region) that condition the temporal dynamics.

- **Action Space Handling:**
  > TFT is a supervised forecasting architecture, not an RL algorithm — it does not natively define or sample from an action space. In a lottery-nn context, TFT would function as the policy network (actor) backbone that maps historical draw sequences and static lottery_id covariates to a probability distribution over balls. The output quantile heads can be repurposed as logits over the ball pool (N=50 for LottoMax), with sampling from the resulting distribution serving as the action selection mechanism. TFT does not natively handle combinatorial (set-valued) outputs — adapting it as an actor requires replacing the quantile regression head with N Bernoulli logits or an autoregressive masked sampler.

- **How Combinatorial Output Is Formed:**
  > TFT does not have a native mechanism for combinatorial k-of-N output. In a lottery-nn adaptation, the quantile regression head would be replaced by N logits (one per ball in the pool), and combinatorial constraint enforcement would be handled externally: (1) post-hoc Gumbel-top-k sampling from the N logits to select exactly k distinct balls; or (2) an autoregressive masking wrapper that calls TFT k times, masking already-selected balls at each step. The static covariate pathway (intended for entity embeddings like store ID) maps naturally to lottery_id embedding, conditioning the output distribution on which lottery is being played. Neither approach is built into TFT — both require custom wrappers around the TFT backbone.

- **Sequential Generation Strategy:**
  > TFT's native output is point or quantile forecasts at multiple future horizons — it generates predictions jointly across horizons in a single forward pass (non-autoregressive in the horizon dimension). For lottery number prediction, this joint strategy translates to producing all N ball logits simultaneously in one forward pass (flat multi-hot generation). This means inter-number correlations in the output cannot be modeled natively — all N ball logits are independent given the encoder output. To model correlations (e.g., numbers that tend to co-occur), an autoregressive wrapper with k sequential forward passes and masking would be needed, but this is not how TFT was designed. The LSTM encoder-decoder inside TFT does capture temporal correlations across historical draws, which is TFT's actual strength.

- **Gradient Estimator Type:**
  > TFT is trained with standard backpropagation through differentiable operations — it uses no discrete sampling during training. The quantile regression loss (pinball loss) is differentiable with respect to all network parameters. When adapted for combinatorial lottery output using Gumbel-softmax/Gumbel-top-k, a reparameterization estimator can be used to propagate gradients through the sampling step. For REINFORCE-based training (e.g., when TFT serves as an actor in an Actor-Critic setup), the score function estimator applies. The GRN gating mechanism uses sigmoid gates that are fully differentiable. TFT's native training does not involve discrete sampling, making it gradient-friendly when the output head is repurposed to produce continuous logits for downstream sampling.

- **Combinatorial Constraint Enforcement:**
  > TFT has no built-in mechanism for combinatorial constraint enforcement (exactly k distinct numbers from {1..N}). Any constraint enforcement must be added externally: (1) Gumbel-top-k applied to the N output logits — draws k samples without replacement in a single differentiable operation; (2) autoregressive masking — iterate k times, setting the logit of already-selected balls to -inf before each sampling step; (3) post-hoc top-k selection — take the k balls with highest logit values (deterministic, non-differentiable, no diversity). The static covariate and VSN pathways naturally produce lottery-conditioned logits, but the constraint itself must be imposed by a wrapper, not by TFT itself.

- **Sample Efficiency Rating:**
  > medium. TFT has more inductive bias than a vanilla transformer due to its LSTM encoder, GRN gating, and variable selection network — these components suppress irrelevant features and stabilize training on small datasets. The static covariate pathway reduces the effective variance each entity (lottery) introduces. However, TFT was benchmarked on datasets with hundreds to thousands of entities and time steps per entity; the paper's smallest benchmark (Favorita retail) has ~300K training samples. On 100-200 historical lottery draws, TFT is significantly over-parameterized: it typically has 500K-2M parameters vs. ~250K in the current SharedLotteryTransformer. Without aggressive regularization (high dropout, weight decay, early stopping), overfitting to 100-200 draws is likely. Rating: medium — better than a vanilla transformer of similar size, worse than simpler models like LSTM or GRN-only networks at this extreme small-data limit.

- **Regularization Built In:**
  > TFT includes several regularization mechanisms: (1) Gated Residual Networks (GRN) with ELU — the gating mechanism learns to zero out irrelevant features, reducing effective model capacity; (2) dropout applied within GRNs and attention layers (typically p=0.1-0.3); (3) variable selection networks learn sparse feature weights, effectively doing input regularization; (4) layer normalization in the GRN after gating; (5) skip connections (residual links) throughout, easing gradient flow and preventing co-adaptation. No explicit entropy bonus (this is not an RL method). The gating and sparsity via VSN provide the most meaningful regularization for small datasets, but dropout and weight decay still need to be tuned externally.

- **Known Failure Modes On Small Data:**
  > Documented and expected failure modes at n < 200: (1) Over-parameterization — a standard TFT has far more parameters than can be supported by 100-200 training samples; validation loss diverges or oscillates without heavy regularization. (2) Static covariate pathway collapse — with only 3 lotteries as static entities, the static embedding layer learns almost nothing useful; the entity-conditioned gating cannot generalize from 3 training entities. (3) Quantile head miscalibration — the pinball loss requires enough samples per quantile to estimate return distributions; with n < 200 there is insufficient coverage of the tail quantiles, producing degenerate prediction intervals. (4) LSTM encoder instability — the sequence-to-sequence LSTM component can diverge early in training when batch sizes are small, producing NaN losses; gradient clipping (max_norm=1.0) is required. (5) Attention collapse — with short sequences (20 draws of history), the temporal self-attention learns to attend uniformly, providing no benefit over the LSTM alone.

- **Reward Distribution Handling:**
  > TFT is a supervised forecasting model, not an RL model — it does not inherently handle reward distributions. In a supervised adaptation, the output can be interpreted as a probability distribution over ball numbers (using quantile heads or softmax logits), which implicitly models uncertainty. The quantile regression formulation is actually well-suited to the lottery's skewed draw distribution: different quantile outputs can model different frequency regimes (hot vs. cold numbers). However, TFT does not handle the 95% zero-reward sparsity of actual lottery play outcomes — that is an RL concept. If TFT is used as a supervised predictor (predict next draw numbers from history), reward sparsity is irrelevant; if used as an actor in an Actor-Critic framework, reward assignment and credit propagation must be handled by the surrounding RL training loop, not by TFT itself.

- **Credit Assignment Granularity:**
  > As a pure forecasting model, TFT assigns no reward and has no credit assignment mechanism — it is trained on next-draw prediction targets using pinball or cross-entropy loss, where each individual number's presence/absence in the next draw serves as a label. This is equivalent to number-level (semi-bandit) credit assignment in RL terms: each ball logit is trained against a binary label (did this ball appear in the draw?). This is actually the most granular level of supervision and is an advantage of using TFT in a supervised fashion rather than with sparse play-level RL reward. If TFT is wrapped in an Actor-Critic loop, the surrounding RL algorithm determines credit granularity independently of TFT.

- **Diversity Mechanism:**
  > TFT has no built-in diversity mechanism for combinatorial output generation. In its native forecasting use, TFT generates quantile predictions that span different percentiles, providing output diversity via the quantile regression head. In a lottery-nn adaptation, diversity must be introduced externally: (1) temperature scaling on the output logits before Gumbel-top-k sampling; (2) stochastic dropout at inference time (MC Dropout) to introduce prediction variance across multiple forward passes; (3) running k independent TFT forward passes with different dropout masks to generate k diverse plays. The variable selection network can learn to spread attention across multiple input features, providing some implicit diversity in which historical patterns are attended to, but this does not directly translate to diverse combinatorial outputs.

- **Offline Vs Online Rl Compatibility:**
  > TFT is natively a fully offline supervised learning model — it trains on a fixed historical dataset without any online environment interaction. This is the strongest compatibility advantage for the lottery-nn use case (one real draw per week). Training requires no live draws; the model learns to predict next-draw numbers from historical sequences. After each new real draw, the draw is appended to the CSV and the model is retrained (or fine-tuned) on the updated dataset — exactly the existing log -> train -> predict routine. No online RL loop is needed. If TFT is wrapped in an Actor-Critic framework, the offline compatibility depends on the surrounding RL algorithm (e.g., offline RL with CQL), but TFT as a backbone is fully compatible with offline training.

- **Complexity:** high
- **Key Dependencies:**
  > PyTorch (already present); pytorch-forecasting (pip install pytorch-forecasting) — the primary open-source TFT implementation, depends on PyTorch Lightning; lightning (pip install lightning); optionally neuralforecast (pip install neuralforecast) for a lighter TFT implementation. A from-scratch TFT implementation without pytorch-forecasting requires ~800-1200 lines of custom PyTorch code for GRN, VSN, temporal attention, and quantile heads.

- **Estimated Lines Of Code:**
  > 600-900 lines of new code for a from-scratch integration: GRN module (~80 lines), VSN module (~100 lines), static covariate encoder (~60 lines), LSTM encoder-decoder (~120 lines), temporal self-attention with interpretable weights (~150 lines), quantile/logit head replacement (~80 lines), data pipeline adapter for TFT input format (~150 lines), training loop integration (~160 lines). Using pytorch-forecasting reduces custom code to ~200-300 lines (data adapter + head replacement + training loop), but adds a heavy PyTorch Lightning dependency.

- **Transfer Learning Compatibility:**
  > Low-to-medium compatibility with the existing SharedLotteryTransformer. TFT has a fundamentally different internal architecture (GRN + VSN + LSTM + temporal attention) compared to the Pre-LN transformer backbone currently in use — the two architectures cannot share weights directly. However, the lottery_id embedding concept maps naturally onto TFT's static covariate mechanism: the existing lottery_id embeddings (0=LottoMax, 1=6/49, 2=DailyGrand) can be fed into TFT's static encoder as entity embeddings without change. The draw feature representation (multi-hot + rolling frequency, 2*50=100 features per timestep) is compatible with TFT's expected input format. Practically, integrating TFT means replacing the SharedLotteryTransformer backbone rather than extending it, which means losing the pretrained encoder weights — a significant cost given the small dataset.

- **Relevance Score:** 2
- **Specific Application In Project:**
  > TFT's most natural application in lottery-nn is as a replacement backbone for the SharedLotteryTransformer in a supervised next-draw prediction setting, not as an Actor-Critic component. Concretely: (1) TFT would replace model_swarm.py's SharedLotteryTransformer, taking draw sequences + lottery_id static covariate as input. (2) The quantile regression head would be replaced with N=50 binary logits (multi-hot draw prediction). (3) Training uses the existing BCE loss against historical draw targets — no RL required. (4) At predict time, Gumbel-top-k samples k=7/6/5 distinct balls from the logit distribution. The key TFT advantage is the static covariate pathway handling lottery_id more explicitly than a simple embedding add, and the VSN learning which historical features (hot numbers, recent frequency, gap patterns) are most informative. However, given the 100-200 draw constraint, TFT's over-parameterization makes it a worse fit than the current ~250K SharedLotteryTransformer. It is a Phase 3 candidate to evaluate if the dataset grows to 500+ draws.

- **Integration Point:** Backbone (replace SharedLotteryTransformer)
- **Replaces Or Extends:**
  > Replaces the SharedLotteryTransformer backbone in src/model_swarm.py entirely. Would require a new src/model_tft.py and updates to preprocessing_swarm.py to format inputs according to TFT's expected (static, known_future, observed_past) triple. Does not extend the current architecture — it is a full backbone swap.

- **Existing Open Source Implementations:**
  > 1. pytorch-forecasting (https://github.com/jdb78/pytorch-forecasting) — the most widely used TFT implementation in PyTorch, backed by PyTorch Lightning; includes the original TFT from Lim et al. 2021 with full quantile regression. 2. neuralforecast (https://github.com/Nixtla/neuralforecast) — Nixtla's forecasting library with a lighter TFT implementation alongside N-HiTS, PatchTST, and others; less Lightning overhead, easier to adapt. 3. GluonTS (https://github.com/awslabs/gluonts) — AWS's probabilistic time series library includes a TFT implementation in MXNet and PyTorch. 4. Original reference implementation by Google Research: https://github.com/google-research/google-research/tree/master/tft — TensorFlow-based, matches the paper exactly. 5. A PyTorch port of the Google reference: https://github.com/mattsherar/Temporal_Fusion_Transform — community-maintained, fewer dependencies. 6. Darts (https://github.com/unit8co/darts) — includes TFT as a built-in model with a clean scikit-learn-style API.

- **Benchmark Results:**
  > From the original paper (Lim et al. 2021): (1) Electricity dataset (370 entities, hourly consumption): TFT achieves P50/P90 RMSE of 0.055/0.027, outperforming DeepAR, MQRNN, and N-BEATS. (2) Traffic dataset (862 time series): TFT P50 normalized deviation 0.094 vs. DeepAR 0.161. (3) Retail Favorita (largest benchmark): TFT achieves best quantile loss across all quantiles (0.1-0.9). (4) Volatility (financial daily returns, smaller dataset ~400 samples/entity): TFT competitive but not dominant vs. simpler baselines. Key finding: TFT's advantage over simpler models increases with the number of entities and the richness of covariates — it is least useful for single-entity short series, which is closest to the lottery use case. No published benchmark with n < 200 per entity.

- **Notable Use Cases:**
  > 1. Retail demand forecasting (Favorita, M5 competition): multi-entity time series with static store/product covariates — structurally most similar to lottery multi-lottery prediction. 2. Electricity consumption forecasting: 370 consumer entities with temporal and static features — TFT's static covariate mechanism demonstrated on real entity heterogeneity. 3. Financial volatility prediction: short, noisy time series with heavy tails — closest data regime to lottery draws in terms of non-stationarity and small samples per entity, though TFT was not shown to dominate simpler models here. 4. Healthcare patient outcome prediction: patient-level static covariates (age, diagnosis) + temporal vitals — the static covariate pathway is analogous to lottery_id. 5. Traffic speed forecasting (METR-LA, PEMS-BAY): spatial-temporal multi-entity with known future covariates (time of day, day of week). None of these use cases involve combinatorial discrete output or sparse reward, so TFT's applicability to Actor-Critic lottery generation is indirect.
